Topic 3 — The Indian Crime Reality

Typology of crime (mala in se & mala prohibita) · Crime Statistics · Traditional Crimes · Social & Economic Offences · Organised Crime (Cyber Crime, Trafficking, Narcotic Trade, Money Laundering) · Privileged Class Deviance

How this document is built. The primary source is the DU Case Material for Criminology (Faculty of Law, University of Delhi). For Topic 3 the material prescribes two readings — the 47th Report of the Law Commission of India on “The Trial and Punishment of Social and Economic Offences” (1972), which the material reproduces in extract, and the NCRB “Figures at a Glance”, which the material directs you to download; and two suggested readings — Prof. Usha Razdan on trafficking in the SAARC region and B.B. Pande on privileged class deviance. The classic statement of mala in se v. mala prohibita reproduced at §4 is taken from Bachan Singh v. State of Punjab, para 62, which the DU material prints in full. Everything drawn from the case material is flagged DU. Where the material is silent — and it is silent on typology as such, on crime statistics as a discipline, and on each of the four organised-crime heads — the gaps are filled from the standard Indian texts in the project folder and from the statutes themselves, and those passages are marked as supplementary. Statutory references are given in the BNS/BNSS/BSA 2023 numbering with the old IPC/CrPC/Evidence Act numbers alongside, because the readings and the previous-year papers use the old numbering.

Suggested Readings: Prof. Usha Razdan, “Combating Trafficking of Women and Children in SAARC Countries: Issues and Intricacies”, Journal of Criminology and Criminal Justice (2012); B.B. Pande, “Privileged Class Deviance — Nature and Dimensions”, The Other Side of Development (1987).

Note: the syllabus prescribes no cases for Topic 3. The cases briefed at §39 are therefore illustrative — they are the vehicles through which the concepts are demonstrated in Indian law, and they are what will earn marks in an answer.

1. Why Topic 3 matters, and how it is examined

Topics 1 and 2 were about ideas — what crime is, and why people commit it. Topic 3 is about facts: what crime in India actually consists of, how much of it there is, who commits it, who is punished for it, and who is not. It is the empirical hinge of the paper. Every theory you learnt in Topic 2 is tested here against the Indian data, and the single most examinable proposition in the whole topic is this: the crimes that fill India’s prisons are not the crimes that do India the most harm. That gap — between the ordinary (“traditional”) crime that is heavily policed and the socio-economic, organised and privileged-class crime that is lightly policed — is “the Indian crime reality”.

What the examiner is testing
(i) Can you classify an offence and say why the classification matters legally (bail, cognizance, burden of proof, forum)? (ii) Can you read a statistic critically — crime rate v. crime number, the principal-offence rule, the dark figure? (iii) Do you know the 47th Report’s six characteristics of socio-economic offences and can you apply them? (iv) Can you set out the statutory machinery for cyber crime, trafficking, narcotics and money laundering with sections? (v) Can you explain why the powerful are under-punished and point to the specific legal devices that produce that result?
The three answer-winning moves
1. Quote the 47th Report verbatim — the six characteristics in para 1.4 and the “intersecting circles” in para 1.9. 2. Use NCRB 2022 figures with the caveat — give the number, then say what it does and does not prove. 3. Name the statute and the section — “trafficking” is not an argument; “s.143 BNS read with s.5 ITPA and Art. 23” is.
Links to the rest of the paper
Topic 1 supplies the conflict perspective on who defines crime; Topic 2 supplies Sutherland (white-collar and differential association), Merton (innovation) and Bonger (economic causation); Topic 5 supplies sentencing; Topics 6–7 supply prisons and the victim. A Topic 3 answer that cites Sutherland and Merton reads like a criminology answer; one that does not reads like a bare list of statutes.

2. “The Indian Crime Reality” — the frame of the topic

2.1 What the phrase means

The heading chosen by the Faculty is deliberate. It does not say “crime in India”; it says the Indian crime reality. The word “reality” carries three separate claims, and a good answer opens by unpacking all three.

(a) Reality as against the legal image
The Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita presents crime as a tidy catalogue of individual wrongs committed by identifiable persons against identifiable victims. The reality is that a very large part of the harm done in India — adulterated food and drugs, industrial disaster, tax evasion, environmental destruction, financial fraud, trafficking — has no identifiable individual victim and is therefore under-reported, under-investigated and under-punished. The 47th Law Commission Report (§18) is entirely about this gap.
(b) Reality as against the statistical image
NCRB data records what the police register, not what happens. The dark figure (§10) is larger than the recorded figure, and it is unevenly dark: a stolen motorcycle is reported, marital rape is not an offence at all, a bribe is reported almost never. Statistics are therefore a measure of the criminal justice system’s output before they are a measure of crime.
(c) Reality as against the popular image
Public fear is organised around the “traditional” street crimes — murder, dacoity, rape, theft — which are visible, sensational and heavily reported by the media. The quantitatively and economically larger problem lies in socio-economic and organised crime, which is invisible, technical and dull. Sutherland’s point from Topic 2 (§18 of those notes) is the governing insight of this whole topic.

2.2 The organising thesis to carry through every answer

Indian criminal justice is built for the visible, individual, violent, lower-class offence and is structurally ill-equipped for the invisible, collective, non-violent, upper-class offence. The first is over-policed and over-punished; the second is under-criminalised, under-detected, under-prosecuted and under-sentenced. Everything in Topic 3 is a demonstration of this asymmetry. Use this as the thesis sentence of any general Topic 3 question.
The asymmetry in one comparisonUnder s.303(2) BNS (old s.379 IPC) an ordinary theft carries up to three years. The Bhopal gas disaster of 1984, which killed thousands and injured over five lakh persons, ended in convictions under s.304-A IPC carrying a maximum of two years (Keshub Mahindra v. State of M.P. (1996) 6 SCC 129; trial court judgment, 2010). That single comparison, stated in one sentence, is worth more in an answer than a page of generalities.

3. Typology of crime — what a typology is and why lawyers and criminologists build them

3.1 The concept

Supplementary text — standard criminology reading “The general term ‘crimes’ covers a wide variety of types of crimes, with their own distinct features. Murder and arson, for example, both are crimes. They have the same essential elements, including a criminal intent (mens rea) and a harm element. But these elements take different forms in different crimes. In murder the criminal intent takes the form of intending to kill another human being wrongfully, while in arson the intent is that of wrongfully burning the property of another. Lawyers and criminologists have searched for a system of grouping the many types of crimes into coherent, rational categories, for ease of understanding, of learning, and of finding them in the law books, and for purposes of studying them from both a legal and a criminological perspective. Such categorizations are called TYPOLOGIES.”

3.2 A short history of classification

  • “The ancient Romans classified their crimes as those ‘against the gods’ and those ‘against other human beings’.” This is the sin/crime distinction of Topic 1 in its earliest form.
  • “As late as the eighteenth century, some English lawyers simply listed crimes alphabetically” — that is, they had no typology at all.
  • “The French of the early nineteenth century created a typology with three categories: serious crimes (felonies); medium serious crimes (misdemeanours); and crimes of a petty character (violations). The more serious crimes were grouped into categories based on the harm those crimes entailed, such as harm against life, against physical integrity, against honour, against property, and so on. Nowadays the French categorization is generally accepted worldwide.”
  • India’s own inheritance: Macaulay’s Indian Penal Code, 1860, adopted the harm-based French model in its chapter headings — offences against the State (Ch. VI), against public tranquillity (VIII), by or relating to public servants (IX), affecting the human body (XVI), against property (XVII), relating to documents (XVIII), relating to marriage (XX), defamation (XXI). The Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 has reorganised but not abandoned this scheme: it now opens with offences against the human body (Ch. VI) and places offences against women and children in a chapter of their own (Ch. V) — a deliberate signal about priorities.

3.3 Why lawyers and criminologists classify differently

Supplementary text “Lawyers and criminologists may differ on the desirability of lumping various crime types together into categories. Lawyers, after all, may be much more interested in the PROCEDURAL CONSEQUENCES that flow from the categorizations, while criminologists may be much more concerned with CRIMINOLOGICAL IMPLICATIONS for studying different types of perpetrators and devising schemes of crime prevention.

There are also political considerations in devising a typology. For example, the criminal codes of the former communist countries had large categories of political crimes, which were given the most prominent place in those codes. They included many crimes that in Western democracies are grouped in other categories, such as property crimes or crimes against the person, or that may have no counterpart at all.”
The LAWYER’s typologyThe CRIMINOLOGIST’s typology
PurposeTo determine procedure, jurisdiction, burden and sentenceTo explain causation and design prevention
Organising ideaThe act and the interest harmedThe offender, the victim, the modus and the social setting
Indian examplesCognizable/non-cognizable; bailable/non-bailable; compoundable/non-compoundable; summons-case/warrant-case; the First Schedule to the BNSSBlue collar/white collar; traditional/socio-economic; organised/individual; violent/property/victimless; conventional/emerging
Consequence of the differenceThe legal typology cannot ask whether the category is just; it takes the legislature’s valuation as givenThe criminological typology can, and does, ask why hoarding is a two-year offence and pickpocketing a three-year one
The examinable point. A typology is never neutral. What a society chooses to group together tells you what it fears. The modern Indian statute book is now grouping organised crime (s.111 BNS), petty organised crime (s.112 BNS) and terrorist act (s.113 BNS) into the general penal code for the first time — a classificatory decision that says a great deal about how the Indian State now reads its own crime reality. Conversely, the BNS has dropped adultery (s.497 IPC), attempted suicide (s.309 IPC) and unnatural offences (s.377 IPC) — three de-classifications, each the product of a shift in moral consensus and of Joseph Shine, s.115 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 and Navtej Singh Johar respectively. Typology is a political act, exactly as the conflict criminologists of Topic 1 said.

4. MALA IN SE and MALA PROHIBITA — the central distinction

Syllabus head A — expressly namedDU Case Material — Bachan Singh, para 62

4.1 The classic statement — reproduce this verbatim

Verbatim — Bachan Singh v. State of Punjab (1980) 2 SCC 684, para 62, as printed in the DU Case Material DU “62. This is particularly true of crimes inherently vicious and pernicious, which under the English Common Law were classified as crimes mala in se as distinguished from crimes mala prohibita. Crimes mala in se embrace acts immoral or wrong in themselves, such as, murder, rape, arson, burglary, larceny (robbery and dacoity); while crimes mala prohibita embrace things prohibited by statute as infringing on others’ rights, though no moral turpitude attaches to such crimes. Such acts constitute crimes only because they are so prohibited….”
Why the Supreme Court was saying this. The context matters and shows you have read the case. In Bachan Singh the Court was deciding whether s.302 IPC and the death penalty must satisfy Article 19. It held that penal laws “in pith and substance” do not deal with the subject-matter of Article 19(1) rights, because “it cannot, reasonably or rationally, be contended that any of the rights mentioned in Article 19(1) of the Constitution confers the freedom to commit murder or, for the matter of that, the freedom to commit any offence whatsoever.” Para 62 then adds that this is “particularly true” of offences mala in se — because with such offences there is no imaginable protected liberty on the other side of the scale. The distinction therefore does constitutional work: the more clearly an offence is malum in se, the less scrutiny the restriction attracts; the more clearly it is malum prohibitum, the more the State must justify it. That is the sophisticated point most candidates miss.

4.2 The distinction, elaborated

PointMALA IN SE (malum in se, sing.)MALA PROHIBITA (malum prohibitum, sing.)
Literal meaning“Wrongs in themselves”“Wrongs because prohibited”
Source of wrongfulnessIntrinsic — the act is immoral independently of any statute; it violates the natural or moral lawExtrinsic — the act derives its wrongfulness entirely from the prohibition
Moral turpitudePresent; the act shocks the conscienceAbsent — “though no moral turpitude attaches to such crimes” (Bachan Singh)
Examples given by the Supreme CourtMurder, rape, arson, burglary, larceny (robbery and dacoity)“Things prohibited by statute as infringing on others’ rights”
Indian examplesss.101–103 BNS (murder), 63–70 (rape), 326(f)/324 (mischief by fire), 305–309 (theft, robbery, dacoity), 115–117 (hurt), 137 (kidnapping)Traffic offences (Motor Vehicles Act, 1988); helmet and seat-belt rules; s.15 NDPS possession; company filing defaults (Companies Act, 2013); Food Safety and Standards Act standards; foreign-exchange and customs contraventions; prohibition laws; s.42 of the Motor Vehicles Act; the Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024
Mental elementMens rea is essential and is presumed to be required unless expressly excludedOften strict or absolute liability; the presumption of mens rea is readily displaced by the object of the statute
UniversalityBroadly constant across time, cultures and legal systems (the “natural crime” of Garofalo — offending pity and probity)Highly variable — what is prohibited in Gujarat and Bihar (liquor) is lawful in Goa; cow slaughter; satta; gambling; cannabis
Community responseStrong, spontaneous, emotional; the Durkheimian reaffirmation of the collective conscienceWeak, calculating; widely regarded as a “technical” or “regulatory” breach; often no social stigma at all
Attempt, abetment, conspiracyFully punishable; the inchoate offences are developedAttempt is often not punished; liability is frequently vicarious or corporate
Who is likely to offendCuts across classes but is policed mainly among the poorPredominantly the trading, professional and corporate classes — which is why the enforcement is soft
Usual enforcementPolice, FIR, arrest, charge-sheet, trialDepartmental/regulatory authority; adjudication, penalty, compounding, settlement
Theoretical homeClassical school — proportionate punishment for a freely chosen wrongUtilitarian regulation — the deterrence of socially costly conduct; “public welfare offences” (47th Report, para 3.15)

4.3 Criticism of the distinction — you must give these

  1. It is circular. How do we know an act is “wrong in itself”? Usually because the law punishes it severely. The distinction then explains the law by the law.
  2. The line is historically mobile. Sati, dowry, untouchability, child marriage and manual scavenging were lawful custom; adultery (until Joseph Shine, 2019), attempted suicide (until s.115 MHA 2017 and the BNS) and consensual same-sex conduct (until Navtej Singh Johar, 2018) were crimes. The category of “natural” crime therefore moves — which is precisely the changing concept of crime studied in Topic 1.
  3. It understates the harm of mala prohibita. Adulterated cough syrup, a collapsing building, an unsafe pesticide plant and a ₹13,000-crore bank fraud are all “regulatory”, and each does more damage than most murders. The 47th Report was written precisely to answer this objection: these offences “require to be put down with a heavy hand” (para 3.13).
  4. It is class-loaded. The offences of the poor tend to be classified as mala in se and attract the full penal apparatus; the offences of the propertied tend to be classified as mala prohibita and attract adjudication, penalty and compounding. This is Sutherland’s and the conflict theorists’ central charge.
  5. It cannot cope with modern hybrid offences. Is insider trading morally wrong or merely prohibited? Is environmental pollution causing death an act malum in se? Indian law has answered the second question by creating absolute liability (M.C. Mehta v. Union of India (1987) 1 SCC 395) and by criminalising environmental harm — a migration of conduct from one category to the other.
  6. It is not a rule of Indian law. Indian courts use it as a descriptive and interpretive aid — chiefly in deciding whether mens rea is implied and in the law of “moral turpitude” for disqualification from public office and service — but no Indian statute uses the terms. Say this expressly.

4.4 Where the distinction actually bites in Indian law Application

FieldHow the distinction operatesAuthority
Implied mens reaFor offences mala in se the presumption that mens rea is an ingredient is very strong; for regulatory offences it may be excluded by the object and language of the statuteNathulal v. State of M.P. AIR 1966 SC 43; State of Maharashtra v. M.H. George AIR 1965 SC 722 (FERA — strict liability upheld); Sherras v. De Rutzen [1895] 1 QB 918; Sweet v. Parsley [1970] AC 132
“Moral turpitude”Disqualification of legislators, dismissal of public servants, refusal of a licence or of admission to the Bar turns on whether the offence involved moral turpitude — in substance, whether it was malum in sePawan Kumar v. State of Haryana (1996) 4 SCC 17 (three tests of moral turpitude); Baleshwar Singh v. District Magistrate AIR 1959 All 71; s.8 Representation of the People Act, 1951
Sentencing and probationCourts routinely deny probation and the benefit of s.4 of the Probation of Offenders Act, 1958 in offences mala in se and grant it liberally in technical or regulatory breachesChhanni v. State of U.P. (2006) 5 SCC 396
Compounding and plea bargainingThe offences excluded from compounding (s.359 BNSS) and from plea bargaining (offences affecting the socio-economic condition of the country, and offences against a woman or a child below 14 — s.290 BNSS) track the distinction, though imperfectlys.265A CrPC → s.290 BNSS; the Central Government’s notification of “socio-economic offences” for this purpose
Extradition and double criminalityExtradition requires the conduct to be an offence in both States — easy for mala in se, contested for mala prohibita (e.g. foreign-exchange and gambling offences)Extradition Act, 1962, s.2(c) and the Schedule
Retrospectivity and Art. 20(1)The constitutional bar on ex post facto criminal law applies to both, but is felt most acutely in mala prohibita, where the citizen has no moral notice and must rely on publication of the ruleArt. 20(1); Rao Shiv Bahadur Singh v. State of Vindhya Pradesh AIR 1953 SC 394
Real-world exampleThe prohibition laws of Gujarat and Bihar are the best Indian teaching example. Consumption of liquor is malum prohibitum: identical conduct is a serious offence in Patna and a licensed leisure activity in Panaji. The Bihar Prohibition and Excise Act, 2016 at one stage made even first-time consumption punishable with a minimum five years and clogged the Patna High Court with bail matters — the Supreme Court in Ankit Kumar v. State of Bihar and a series of orders in 2022 recorded that the Act was “choking” the docket, and the State amended it in 2022 to make first-time consumption compoundable on payment of a fine. Moral: when a malum prohibitum is punished as though it were a malum in se, the criminal justice system itself breaks down.

5. Consequences of the distinction — mens rea, strict liability and public welfare offences

5.1 The general rule and its displacement

The common-law presumption is actus non facit reum nisi mens sit rea. In India the presumption is a rule of construction, not of constitutional law: the legislature may create an offence of strict liability, and in regulatory and socio-economic legislation it frequently does. The tests the courts apply are:

  1. The words of the section — the absence of “knowingly”, “wilfully”, “fraudulently”, “dishonestly”, “voluntarily” is an indication, though not conclusive.
  2. The object of the statute — is it to regulate an activity in the interest of public welfare, or to punish a moral wrong? (State of Maharashtra v. M.H. George.)
  3. Whether the imposition of strict liability will actually promote the object — i.e. will it make persons in that trade more careful? (Lim Chin Aik v. The Queen [1963] AC 160; Nathulal.)
  4. The severity of the penalty and the social stigma — the graver the punishment, the stronger the presumption of mens rea (Sweet v. Parsley).

5.2 Strict and absolute liability in Indian socio-economic legislation

StatuteDevice usedEffect
NDPS Act, 1985s.35 — presumption of culpable mental state; s.54 — presumption from possession; s.37 — twin conditions for bailOnce conscious possession is shown, the accused must disprove the mental element “beyond reasonable doubt” — the sharpest reversal of burden in Indian criminal law. See Noor Aga v. State of Punjab (2008) 16 SCC 417 upholding it with safeguards, and Tofan Singh v. State of Tamil Nadu (2021) 4 SCC 1 (s.67 statements to NCB officers not admissible as confessions)
PMLA, 2002s.24 — presumption that the property is proceeds of crime; s.45 — twin conditions for bail; s.50 — statements to ED officers admissibleUpheld in Vijay Madanlal Choudhary v. Union of India (2022) SCC OnLine SC 929 (§36)
Food Safety and Standards Act, 2006 (and the repealed PFA Act, 1954)Liability for selling adulterated or misbranded food essentially without proof of knowledge; s.66 vicarious liability of company officersSarjoo Prasad v. State of U.P. AIR 1961 SC 631 — a servant who sells adulterated food is liable even without knowledge

Union of India (1987) 1 SCC 395 (Oleum Gas Leak) — no exceptions to the rule in Rylands v. Fletcher; Indian Council for Enviro-Legal Action v. Union of India (1996) 3 SCC 212 (Bichhri) — polluter pays

(2011) 1 SCC 74 — a company can be prosecuted for offences requiring mens rea, the mental state of the directing mind being attributed to it
Tie it back to the syllabus. Strict liability is the legislature’s technique for coping with offences mala prohibita whose social cost is high but whose proof of intention is impossible — and it is exactly what the 47th Report recommended for socio-economic offences (§21). It is also the principal reason why those offences fail to attract stigma: society reserves its moral condemnation for conduct it believes was chosen.

6. The Indian statutory classifications — the lawyer’s typology in operation

The criminologist classifies by cause; the lawyer classifies by consequence. Every offence in India is simultaneously located on five statutory axes, each of which is set out in the First Schedule to the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 (old First Schedule to the CrPC). A question on “typology of crime and their characteristics” that omits these axes has answered only half the question.

6.1 Cognizable and non-cognizable offences

COGNIZABLENON-COGNIZABLE
Definitions.2(1)(g) BNSS (old s.2(c) CrPC) — an offence for which a police officer may arrest without warrants.2(1)(o) BNSS (old s.2(l) CrPC) — an offence for which a police officer has no authority to arrest without warrant
Registration of FIRMandatory — s.173 BNSS (old s.154); Lalita Kumari v. Govt. of U.P. (2014) 2 SCC 1Entry in the station diary; no investigation without the order of a Magistrate — s.174 BNSS (old s.155(2))
InvestigationPolice may investigate suo motu — s.175 BNSS (old s.156)Only with the Magistrate’s order; thereafter the police have the same powers except arrest without warrant
Typical offencesMurder, rape, dacoity, theft, kidnapping, rioting, dowry deathSimple hurt (s.115(2) BNS), defamation (s.356), public nuisance, criminal trespass in some forms
Criminological significanceThis is where the police choose — and where “burking” and selective registration create the dark figureEffectively decriminalised in practice: few victims pursue a Magistrate’s order
Two new BNSS features worth a mark. (i) Zero FIR is now statutory — the proviso to s.173(1) BNSS allows an FIR to be registered irrespective of the area where the offence is committed, to be transferred to the jurisdictional station. (ii) s.173(3) BNSS creates a preliminary enquiry of 14 days for offences punishable with 3 years or more but less than 7 years, with the prior permission of a DSP-rank officer — a legislative narrowing of Lalita Kumari that is already being litigated and is an excellent critical point.

6.2 Bailable and non-bailable

  • s.2(1)(c) BNSS (old s.2(a) CrPC): a bailable offence is one shown as bailable in the First Schedule or made bailable by any other law; a non-bailable offence is any other offence. The classification is therefore purely a matter of the Schedule — there is no conceptual test.
  • In a bailable offence bail is a matter of right (s.478 BNSS, old s.436); in a non-bailable offence it is discretionary (s.480 BNSS, old s.437; s.483 BNSS, old s.439, for the Sessions Court and the High Court).
  • Special statutes override the Schedule and reverse the presumption: s.37 NDPS Act, s.45 PMLA, s.43-D(5) UAPA, s.21(4) MCOCA — the “twin conditions” regime under which the court must be satisfied that there are reasonable grounds for believing the accused is not guilty. Notice the pattern: the most severe bail restrictions in Indian law attach to precisely the socio-economic and organised offences of Topic 3 head C.
  • s.479 BNSS (new): an undertrial who has served one-half of the maximum sentence — or, for a first-time offender, one-third — shall be released on bond; not applicable to offences punishable with death or life. Read with Satender Kumar Antil v. CBI (2022) 10 SCC 51, which categorised offences (A–D) and laid down bail guidelines, including a separate and stricter approach for economic offences.

6.3 Compoundable and non-compoundable

s.359 BNSS (old s.320 CrPC) lists offences compoundable without the court’s permission (Table 1 — e.g. simple hurt, criminal trespass, defamation, adultery-type matrimonial offences now removed) and those compoundable with permission (Table 2 — e.g. theft under ₹5,000 by a servant, criminal breach of trust, cheating, grievous hurt). No other offence is compoundable (s.359(9)). Offences mala in se of the gravest kind — murder, rape, dacoity — are never compoundable. But the High Court’s inherent power under s.528 BNSS (old s.482) has been used to quash non-compoundable offences in matrimonial and commercial disputes on settlement: B.S. Joshi v. State of Haryana (2003) 4 SCC 675; Gian Singh v. State of Punjab (2012) 10 SCC 303; Narinder Singh v. State of Punjab (2014) 6 SCC 466 — the Court holding expressly that heinous offences and socio-economic offences cannot be quashed on settlement because they are crimes against society, not against an individual. That holding is a judicial statement of the 47th Report’s central thesis.

6.4 Summons-case and warrant-case; forum and sentencing power

AxisContentProvision
Summons case / warrant caseA warrant case relates to an offence punishable with death, life imprisonment or imprisonment exceeding two years; every other case is a summons case. Different trial procedures follow (Ch. XX and Ch. XIX BNSS)s.2(1)(x) and (z) BNSS (old s.2(w), (x))
ForumCourt of Session; Chief Judicial Magistrate; Judicial Magistrate first class; second class — as set out in column 6 of the First Scheduless.21–23 BNSS (old ss.26–29); sentencing powers: CJM up to 7 years, JMFC up to 3 years and fine up to ₹50,000 (raised from ₹10,000)
Summary trials.283 BNSS makes summary trial mandatory for petty theft, receiving stolen property, etc., where the value is under ₹20,000 — a new provision aimed at the volume of petty property crimess.283–288 BNSS (old ss.260–265)
Special courtsNDPS Special Courts (s.36 NDPS); PMLA Special Courts (s.43); CBI courts; POCSO Special Courts (s.28); SC/ST Special Courts (s.14 of the 1989 Act); NIA Special Courts; MP/MLA courts directed in Ashwini Kumar Upadhyay v. Union of IndiaEach socio-economic and organised-crime statute creates its own forum — a structural feature you should note
IPC/BNS offences v. SLL offencesNCRB’s master division: “IPC crimes” (now BNS) and “Special and Local Laws (SLL) crimes”. In 2022, 35,61,379 IPC and 22,63,567 SLL casesThe SLL head is where nearly all socio-economic and regulatory offending is counted — and it is the head that is most affected by policy-driven enforcement drives
How to use §6 in an answer. After defining mala in se and mala prohibita, write: “Indian law does not adopt these Latin categories, but it achieves substantially the same result through five statutory classifications…”, then give the five axes in a short table, and close with the observation that the classification chosen determines who is arrested, who gets bail, who is tried where, and who can buy his way out by compounding — which is to say that typology is not an academic exercise but the operative distribution of liberty.

7. Other typologies of crime — the full catalogue

An answer on “typology of crime and their characteristics” is expected to go well beyond mala in se and mala prohibita. The following is the working catalogue; give the definition, one or two Indian illustrations, and the characteristic that distinguishes each category.

7.1 Blue-collar crime — “crime in the streets”

Supplementary text “In criminology, blue-collar crime is any crime committed by an individual from a lower social class as opposed to white-collar crime which is associated with crime committed by individuals of a higher social class. These are easier to detect, people are more afraid of these crimes. Blue collar crimes are more sensational crimes and thereby put more pressure on police due to fear.

The opportunity to commit crime. Every potential criminal is limited in the opportunity to commit crime by the ‘situation’ which he or she occupies in society. If the individual is employed in a low or unskilled job and lives in an inner-city environment, stealing inventory from the workplace may not produce value and not many neighbours may have valuable property to steal. This has significance both for the type of crimes likely to be committed and for law enforcement. Because there are fewer opportunities to use skill, more blue-collar crime may involve the use of FORCE and, because more people are injured, there is a greater chance that the victim will report the crime. In contrast, in cases of white-collar crime it shades into corporate crime and there is less chance that any crime will be reported.”

Sub-types given in the same source: (i) crimes against the person — “violence or the threat of violence against others, violence including homicide, rape, robbery, causing grave and ordinary injuries and aggravated assaults”; (ii) crimes against property — “theft of goods belonging to others, including burglary, theft, motor vehicle theft, and arson”; (iii) victimless crime — “violation of laws in which there are no readily apparent victims, a misnomer, including prostitution, gambling and drug abuse”; and (iv) juvenile delinquency — “offences committed by persons under [the age of majority]… a status in a juvenile or a young person characterized by anti-social behaviour, waywardness and incorrigibility.”

Street crime: “the most common forms of predatory crime — rape, robbery, assault, burglary, larceny, and auto theft — occur most frequently on urban streets”, and minority citizens “account for a disproportionately high number of the arrests for street crimes” — the differential enforcement point of Topic 1 and Topic 2.

7.2 White-collar crime — “crime in suits”

Sutherland’s definition (1949): “a crime committed by a person of respectability and high social status in the course of his occupation.” The 47th Report’s working description is set out verbatim at §19. The supplementary text adds the four modern sub-types:

Workplace / occupational crime
“Crimes committed against employers by employees for individual gain”; “an individual’s or group’s illegal use of their professional position to secure something of value, found at all levels of the labour force, e.g. fraud.” Indian examples: bank officers’ fraudulent loan sanctions; pilferage from PDS godowns; fake medical certificates; billing frauds in hospitals.
Organizational / corporate crime
“Committed by the decision-makers of a corporation. Or it may involve government engaging in illegal activity for corporate or organizational advantage as opposed to personal gain. Other types of offences falling under this class are terrorism, selling products which are known to be unsafe or defective.” Indian examples: Satyam (2009); the sale of substandard cough syrup implicated in child deaths abroad (2022–23); Union Carbide at Bhopal.
Strategic bankruptcy
“A company which is successfully sued declares bankruptcy and thus avoids having to pay up, and the company is then reorganized into a new company which is clear of personal/company liabilities.” Indian answer: s.29A of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016, which disqualifies defaulting promoters from bidding for their own company — see ArcelorMittal India v. Satish Kumar Gupta (2019) 2 SCC 1 and Swiss Ribbons v. Union of India (2019) 4 SCC 17; and the Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018.
“Patriotic” / state crime
“Crimes committed in the name of achieving important national goals, actions taken outside legitimate channels, e.g. violation of international law, ‘protecting’ national security, undeclared warfare, false imprisonment, failure to regulate pollution, tax laws, etc.” Indian counterparts: custodial death and fake encounters (PUCL v. State of Maharashtra (2014) 10 SCC 635 — the 16-point guidelines), illegal detention, and surveillance.

7.3 Victimless and public-order crime

Supplementary text “Consensual acts (in which people are willing participants) and violations in which only the perpetrator is hurt, such as the personal use of illegal drugs, are called victimless crimes. In criminology public order crime is defined by Siegel (2004) as ‘crime which involves acts that interfere with the operations of society and the ability of people to function efficiently’ — i.e. it is behaviour that has been labelled criminal because it is contrary to shared norms, social values, and customs… Generally speaking, deviancy is criminalized when it is too disruptive and has proved uncontrollable through informal sanctions.

Public order crime should be distinguished from political crime. In the former, although the identity of the ‘victim’ may be indirect and sometimes diffuse, it is cumulatively the community that suffers, whereas in a political crime, the state perceives itself to be the victim and criminalizes the behaviour it considers threatening… ‘Public order crime’ is now the preferred term as against the use of the word ‘victimless’, based on the idea that there are SECONDARY VICTIMS (family, friends, acquaintances, and society at large) that can be identified.

Following the work of Schur (1965), the types of crime usually referred to include the sexually based offences of prostitution, paraphilia, underage sex, and pornography; and the offences involving substance abuse… This classification of crime contains many instances of criminality where the only injured party appears to be the accused who has made the personal choice to engage in some form of self-destructive behaviour… Thus, there is continuing political debate on criminalization versus decriminalization… Respect for the law in general can only be maintained if the law is worthy of respect.”
Indian applicationThe victimless-crime debate is the framework for four recent Indian developments: (i) attempted suicide — s.309 IPC effectively neutralised by s.115 of the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 and not re-enacted in the BNS; (ii) consensual adult same-sex conduct — Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India (2018) 10 SCC 1; (iii) adultery — Joseph Shine v. Union of India (2019) 3 SCC 39; (iv) begging — Harsh Mander v. Union of India 2018 SCC OnLine Del 10427. Each is a judicial acceptance of J.S. Mill’s harm principle and of the Wolfenden Report’s distinction between crime and sin — the material of Topic 1 (Hart–Devlin). Pending questions: personal cannabis use under s.27 NDPS, sex work (the Supreme Court’s directions in Budhadev Karmaskar v. State of W.B. (2022) recognising sex work as a profession and forbidding police harassment of consenting adult sex workers), and online real-money gaming.

7.4 Political crime, hate crime and international crime

CategoryDefinitionIndian law and illustrations
Political crimeCrime by the government and crime against the government; “political goals motivate political criminals”; the State perceives itself as the victimCh. VII BNS — offences against the State: s.147 (waging war), s.148 (conspiracy to wage war), s.152 (acts endangering sovereignty, unity and integrity of India — the successor to sedition under s.124-A IPC, which is in abeyance following S.G. Vombatkere v. Union of India (2022) 7 SCC 433); UAPA, 1967 ss.13, 15, 18, 43-D(5); the National Security Act, 1980; Official Secrets Act, 1923
Hate crime“A criminal act motivated by racial or other bias such as religion, ancestry, sexual orientation or physical disability”India has no general hate-crime statute. The functional equivalents are: ss.196, 197, 299, 302 BNS (promoting enmity; imputations prejudicial to national integration; outraging religious feelings); the SC/ST (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989, s.3(1) and (2); s.103(2) BNS — the new provision on mob lynching (murder by a group of five or more on the ground of race, caste, community, sex, place of birth, language, personal belief “or any other ground”), enacting the directions in Tehseen S. Poonawalla v. Union of India (2018) 9 SCC 501
International crime“The major criminal offences so designated by the community of nations for the protection of interests common to all humankind”Genocide, crimes against humanity, war crimes and aggression — the Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court, 1998 (India is not a party); piracy — the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022; torture — India has signed but not ratified CAT, and the Law Commission’s 273rd Report (2017) recommended a Prevention of Torture Bill; transnational organised crime — UNTOC (Palermo Convention), 2000 and its Protocols, ratified by India in 2011
Environmental / green crimeHarm to the environment criminalised in the public interest; the archetypal malum prohibitum with catastrophic consequencesEPA 1986 ss.15–17; Water Act 1974; Air Act 1981; Wild Life (Protection) Act, 1972; Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980; NGT Act, 2010; the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Act, 2023, which decriminalised a large number of environmental and business offences and converted them into monetary penalties — an important and examinable recent development
Cyber crime“Criminal activity where a computer or network is the source, tool, target, or place of a crime”IT Act, 2000; BNS ss.111(1) Expl., 303, 318, 319, 336–340; see §28–§30

8. Crime statistics — meaning, nature and importance

Syllabus head APrescribed reading: NCRB Figures at a Glance 2022

8.1 Definition and function

Supplementary text — the standard Indian formulation “Crime statistics are the indices of intensity of crimes recorded annually in a particular country, region or place. [They reflect] upon the ascending or descending trends in crime and also [give] information as to how new forms of crime are emerging and the old ones are disappearing or assuming new dimensions. Thus crime statistics are indicative of the general moral tune of a given society and throw light on the general efficacy of police, prosecuting agencies and law courts. Therefore, the role of crime statistics in analysing [the] causation of crime and devising measures to combat criminality need not be over-emphasized. The statistics of crime help the law enforcement agencies to spot out the preponderance of crime at a particular time, place and region.”

8.2 What should crime statistics cover? The debate on scope

“Criminologists differ as to their views about the scope of criminal statistics. Some assert that the data should mainly concern offences and offenders, administrative actions and decisions of the Courts, while others maintain that it should only be limited to offenders and convicted persons. But the generally accepted view is that crime statistics should not only be confined to data on offences and offenders but also include numerical figures pertaining to the criminal law administration agencies such as police, prosecution, courts, parole and probation services, juvenile delinquency, prisons, drug law violations and trafficking records. The data so presented should be scientifically classified, tabulated and analysed so as to present a realistic picture… The periodical publication of such statistics is equally important.”

8.3 The classic endorsements — quote at least two

Donald Taft
“Quantification, which includes counting, measuring and collating the phenomena under study, is the basic process in [the] modern scientific approach to criminal science. Without this process, investigative efforts would hardly serve any useful purpose. Crime statistics also depict the picture of distribution of crimes in different areas, regions and locations. It must, however, be stated that mathematical accuracy of crime figures at a given place and time is rather difficult to ascertain. The statistics only present an over-all picture of incidence of crime and make it possible to compare the crime rate at regional, national and international level.”
Jeremy Bentham
On periodical statistical records of prisons and prisoners: “The ordering of these returns is a measure of excellent use in furnishing data for the legislature to work upon. They will form together a kind of POLITICAL BAROMETER by which the effect of every legislative operation relative to the subject may be indicated and made palpable. It is not till lately [that] legislators have thought of providing themselves with necessary documents.” Note how this links Topic 3 to the classical school of Topic 2 — Bentham’s felicific calculus required measurement.
Edwin Sutherland
“These statistics are sometimes usefully utilized in the formulation of social policies and theories of criminality. Besides, [they] also provide valuable source-material for crime investigators. The social information contained in them forms the basis of extensive research in the field of criminology. In the absence of [a] statistical record of crime it would become virtually impossible to form any valid opinion about the crime picture in a given place.”
Quetelet and Guerry — the origin
Crime statistics as a discipline began with the first French national judicial statistics of 1827, and with André-Michel Guerry’s Essai sur la statistique morale de la France (1833) and Adolphe Quetelet’s Sur l’homme (1835). Quetelet’s finding that crime rates were remarkably stable year on year — the “budget of the scaffold… paid with frightening regularity” — is what first made a science of criminology possible. Every modern crime-statistics answer should open by saying so: the subject is not book-keeping; it is the empirical foundation of the discipline (Topic 2, §7).

8.4 The uses of crime statistics — six, with Indian illustrations

  1. Legislative. The 2013 Criminal Law Amendment followed the Verma Committee’s reading of the sexual-offence data; the Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024 followed the paper-leak record; the insertion of s.111 BNS (organised crime) followed the Vohra Committee and the organised-crime data.
  2. Police deployment and planning. Crime mapping, hotspot policing, the Crime and Criminal Tracking Network and Systems (CCTNS) and the Inter-operable Criminal Justice System (ICJS).
  3. Judicial. Courts rely on data expressly: Bachan Singh on the deterrent effect of capital punishment; the Law Commission’s 262nd Report (2015); In Re Inhuman Conditions in 1382 Prisons (2016) 3 SCC 700 on overcrowding; Arnesh Kumar on arrest rates.
  4. Budgetary and administrative. Finance Commission and Home Ministry allocations; the Modernisation of Police Forces scheme; the Nirbhaya Fund.
  5. Research and theory-testing. Merton, Bonger, Shaw & McKay — every macro theory of Topic 2 is an empirical claim about rates.
  6. Comparative and international. UNODC’s Global Study on Homicide; the Trafficking in Persons report; FATF mutual evaluations.

9. Sources of crime statistics — police, judicial and penal

9.1 The threefold classification

Supplementary text “Considered from the point of view of different agencies connected with the administration of criminal justice, criminal statistics may be placed under three broad heads, namely: (i) Police statistics; (ii) Judicial or Court statistics; and (iii) Penal statistics.

Police statistics are primarily concerned with the number of crimes reported, the number of persons apprehended and the number of offences cleared or accounted for by the arrests made.

Judicial or Court statistics are concerned with the number of offences prosecuted, the number convicted and the method of procedure followed in determining guilt, the number not convicted and the stage at which cases were dropped. These statistics also account for the number of convictions and the type of sentences imposed upon guilty persons.

Penal statistics comprise the details of different types of custodial measures, the characteristics of inmates, time spent in custody, number of escapes and offence-wise number of recidivists.

[E]qually important is the data of post-correctional criminal behaviour, but it is practically not feasible to follow up the post-release conduct of convicted offenders. But the Government should at least make efforts to collect the statistical data concerning repetition of crimes, which may help the administration in suitably dealing with the habitual [offender].”
Add the fourth and fifth sources — this is what lifts the answer. (iv) Victimisation surveys, which ask a sample of the population what has happened to them rather than what the police recorded — the Crime Survey for England and Wales and the US National Crime Victimization Survey are the models; India has no national equivalent, which is a major and citable gap (the India Justice Report, the Common Cause–CSDS “Status of Policing in India Report” and the National Family Health Survey are partial substitutes; NFHS-5 (2019–21) found that only about 14% of women who had suffered physical or sexual violence ever sought help). (v) Self-report studies, which ask offenders directly and which consistently show that offending is far more widely distributed across social classes than the official data suggest — the empirical basis of Sutherland’s critique.

9.2 The Indian statistical machinery Institutional

BodyWhat it producesLegal/administrative basis
National Crime Records Bureau (NCRB), Ministry of Home Affairs, set up 11 March 1986Crime in India (since 1953); Accidental Deaths and Suicides in India (ADSI); Prison Statistics India; Crime in India — Figures at a Glance; Fingerprint in IndiaExecutive body; merged the Directorate of Coordination Police Computers, the Inter-State Criminality Data Branch of the CBI, and the Central Finger Print Bureau. Runs CCTNS, ICJS, the National Digital Crime Resource and Training Centre, the National Automated Fingerprint Identification System (NAFIS), the Crime Multi Agency Centre (Cri-MAC) and the National Cybercrime Reporting Portal data feed

10. The DARK FIGURE of crime — why crime statistics are unreliable

This is the single most examinable sub-head in the statistics portion. The classic Indian formulation gives eight reasons; learn them as a numbered list.

Supplementary text — the eight reasons, substantially verbatim “The magnitude of the problem… can be ascertained after an analysis of the criminal statistics. But the fact remains that these statistics deal mainly with RECORDED crimes… It is for this reason that it is generally believed that statistics of crime and criminals are the most deceptive of all statistics and hardly present a true picture of the crime position. Some of the reasons generally attributed to the unreliability of crime statistics are as follows:
  1. The concept of crime being dynamic, it is difficult to determine the quantum of crime with accuracy.
  2. Quite a large number of crimes committed remain undetected; there are others which are detected but not reported, and many more are reported but not recorded. There are several reasons for not reporting crimes. The offence may be considered trivial; the police post might be too far away; one sincerely wants that the culprit should be punished but he may apprehend harassment from him; or he may not be willing to go through the cumbersome process of criminal trial; or he or she may feel embarrassed, as in case of sex offences. The victim may also not be interested in reporting crime because he may not have confidence in the criminal justice system.
  3. At times, there is a deliberate non-registration of crimes because a lesser number of crimes projects a better image of police performance.
  4. Quite a large number of crimes are lost between arrest and prosecution and many more are lost between prosecution and conviction. Explaining this point further, Leon Radzinowicz observed that crimes fully brought out into the open and punished “represent not more than FIFTEEN PER CENT of the great mass actually committed”.
  5. The crime statistics at different places do not present a true picture of the volume of crimes because of socio-economic differences and variations in the criminal law. The behaviour which may be a crime at one place may not necessarily be so in another place or time. This reduces the significance of the crime index for the purposes of comparison… comparisons of the crime rates of various countries are seriously limited by wide variations in their national legal systems.
  6. The crime figures for the purpose of comparison are to be stated in proportion to population or some other base and, as E.H. Sutherland rightly points out, determination of this base is often difficult. The accuracy of population figures is equally a matter of doubt and suspicion.
  7. The judicial statistics… regarding number of convictions do not generally tally with the statistics of prisoners compiled by prison authorities, because all convictions do not necessarily result in imprisonment. Many convicted persons are let off after admonition or fine, or released on probation or parole, or booked to a correctional institution.
  8. The prison statistics often give a distorted picture of criminality. They are least reliable to be used for appreciating the magnitude of the real crime problem or the types of crimes committed, or the types of offenders booked for crimes. In fact, they are simply indicative of the persons who are institutionalized rather than having any bearing upon the actual number of crimes or criminals. Moreover, these statistics are reflective of WHY PEOPLE ARE CAUGHT AND PROSECUTED rather than of WHY THEY COMMIT CRIMES, which is the central concern of the criminologist.
…accuracy of crime statistics depends, by and large, upon the societal reaction towards different crimes and the honesty, efficiency and working of the Police Department… Crime being concerned with behavioural patterns, accuracy of crime figures is a MYTH. Statistics, therefore, depict only a general picture of criminality at a given place and time. Commenting on the unreliability of crime statistics, B. Wehner observed that ‘unreported crimes vary between a minimum of twice and a maximum of four times the number actually shown in criminal statistics’.”

10.1 Indian-specific causes of the dark figure — add these

Refusal to register an FIR
Despite s.173 BNSS (old s.154 CrPC) and the Constitution Bench in Lalita Kumari v. Government of U.P. (2014) 2 SCC 1 — registration of an FIR is mandatory where the information discloses a cognizable offence, and no preliminary inquiry is permissible except in the categories identified (matrimonial/family disputes, commercial offences, medical negligence, corruption, and abnormal delay) — refusal and “burking” remain endemic. Remedies: s.173(4) BNSS (send the substance to the SP), s.175(3) BNSS (application to the Magistrate), private complaint under s.223 BNSS, and s.199 BNS (old s.166A IPC) which makes a public servant’s failure to record information about specified sexual offences an offence.
The PRINCIPAL OFFENCE RULE
NCRB counts one case per FIR under the most heinous offence only. A single incident of abduction + gang rape + murder + destruction of evidence is counted as one murder. Consequently the published counts of rape, abduction and lesser offences are systematically understated. This is the single most important methodological criticism of the NCRB and should be stated in every statistics answer.
Under-reporting concentrated in particular offences
Sexual offences (shame, victim-blaming, the trial process); domestic violence (dependence, family pressure); caste atrocities (fear of the dominant caste); corruption (the complainant is a participant); white-collar and corporate crime (no individual knows he is a victim); cyber fraud (embarrassment, and the belief that nothing will be done); crimes against the elderly and against persons with disabilities.
Definitional and legal change breaks the series
The Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2013 widened the definition of rape and created new offences (ss.354A–354D IPC), so the post-2013 numbers are not comparable with the pre-2013 numbers. The BNS, BNSS and BSA, in force from 1 July 2024, renumber every offence and create new ones (organised crime, petty organised crime, mob lynching, snatching, terrorist act), which will break the series again. Say this: a rise in the number may reflect a change in the law, in reporting behaviour, or in police practice, and not a rise in crime.
“More cases” may mean better policing
The NCRB itself cautions that increases “may be attributed to citizen-centric police initiatives” — e-FIR facilities, women’s help desks, the 1930 cyber helpline, Zero FIR under s.173(1) BNSS and mandatory e-FIR. A State with an honest police force will always look more criminal than a State with a dishonest one. This is the paradox of crime statistics.
Population base and the missing Census
The “crime rate” is per lakh of population. The 2021 Census has not been conducted, so every rate published since then rests on projected population figures — an under- or over-estimate of the denominator distorts every rate. Sutherland’s objection that “determination of this base is often difficult” is therefore live in India today.
Real-world exampleIn 2015 the Delhi Police recorded a dramatic “rise” in rape cases. Analysts showed that the rise coincided with a directive, after the 2012 Nirbhaya case and the Verma Committee, that every complaint be registered and that refusal to register be punished (s.166A IPC). The number went up because the recording practice changed. Conversely, several States have repeatedly been found to “burke” crime to protect their ranking in the NCRB tables. The correct conclusion in an answer: crime statistics are primarily a measure of the criminal justice system, and only secondarily a measure of crime.

11. The National Crime Records Bureau — institution, publications and methodology

Prescribed reading DUhttp://ncrb.gov.in

11.1 What the NCRB is

The National Crime Records Bureau was set up on 11 March 1986 under the Ministry of Home Affairs on the recommendation of the National Police Commission (1977–81) and the Task Force of the MHA (1985). It was formed by merging the Directorate of Coordination Police Computers, the Inter-State Criminality Data Branch of the CBI, the Central Finger Print Bureau and the statistical branch of the BPR&D. It is an executive body with no statutory charter of its own — a fact worth stating, because it means the NCRB has no power to compel a State to send accurate data, and no power to audit what it receives.

Publications
Crime in India — the flagship, published annually since 1953, with the short companion volume Crime in India — Figures at a Glance which is the prescribed reading; Accidental Deaths and Suicides in India (ADSI), since 1967; Prison Statistics India, since 1995; Fingerprint in India; and the Missing Persons and Unidentified Dead Bodies data.
Systems it runs
CCTNS (Crime and Criminal Tracking Network and Systems) — connecting some 17,000 police stations; ICJS (Inter-operable Criminal Justice System) linking police, courts, prisons, prosecution and forensics; NAFIS (National Automated Fingerprint Identification System, 2022); Cri-MAC (Crime Multi Agency Centre); the National Database on Sexual Offenders (NDSO), 2018; the Investigation Tracking System for Sexual Offences (ITSSO); and the national Missing Person/ Talash and KHOYA-PAYA portals.
The data chain
Police Station → District Crime Records Bureau → State Crime Records Bureau → NCRB. Every weakness in the chain — an unregistered FIR, a mis-classified offence, a delayed consolidation — is reproduced in the national figure. The NCRB is a compiler, not an investigator, of data.
Legal touchpoints
Registration of the underlying case: s.173 BNSS. Collection of biometrics: the Criminal Procedure (Identification) Act, 2022, which replaced the Identification of Prisoners Act, 1920 and empowers the NCRB to collect, store for 75 years, and share “measurements” (fingerprints, palm prints, footprints, photographs, iris and retina scans, physical and biological samples and behavioural attributes) — a major and heavily criticised expansion, now under challenge on Art. 20(3) and Puttaswamy privacy grounds.

11.2 The methodology you must know — and criticise

  1. The PRINCIPAL OFFENCE RULE. Where several offences are registered in a single FIR, NCRB counts the case only once, under the offence carrying the most severe punishment. A kidnapping-plus-gang-rape-plus- murder is counted as one murder. Consequence: the published counts of every offence other than the gravest are understated; and the total count of “crimes” is a count of FIRs, not of criminal acts or of victims.
  2. Case-based, not victim-based, counting. An FIR relating to a massacre of seventeen persons is one case. Victim counts are separately published for some heads only.
  3. “Crime rate” = cases per lakh of population. Rate is the correct comparative measure (absolute numbers rise with population), but the denominator since 2021 is a projected population because the Census has not been held.
  4. “Charge-sheeting rate” = charge-sheets filed ÷ cases in which investigation was completed. It measures police output, not guilt.
  5. “Conviction rate” = convictions ÷ cases in which trials were completed. A high conviction rate may indicate good investigation or selective charge-sheeting; a low rate may indicate poor investigation, hostile witnesses, or over-registration.
  6. “Pendency percentage” — with about 4.4 crore cases pending in the district judiciary (NJDG), the trial-stage figures lag the registration figures by years, so the two cannot be compared for the same year.
  7. State comparability. Different States police, register and classify differently; some offences exist in some States only (prohibition, cow slaughter, beggary). Sutherland’s and the standard Indian objection (§10, reason 5) applies directly.
  8. Categories that have changed. The NCRB has added heads over time — cyber crime, crimes against senior citizens, human trafficking, offences against the State, “crime against women” sub-heads after 2013. A new head always produces an apparent “increase”.

12. NCRB “Crime in India / Figures at a Glance 2022” — the data to quote

Use these figures precisely and always with a caveat. Quote the number, name the source and the year, and then say in one clause what the number does not prove. That combination — data plus epistemic caution — is what distinguishes a first-class statistics answer.

12.1 The headline national figures, 2022

Indicator2022Comparison / comment

Safest and least safe metro—Kolkata was the safest metropolis for the third consecutive year at 86.5 cognizable offences per lakh, followed by Pune (280.7) and Hyderabad (299.2). Delhi recorded the worst rate of crime against women and the largest number of juveniles in conflict with law (2,436 cases)
Kerala anomaly — a teaching point2,35,858 IPC casesCrime rate 661 per lakh, the highest in India, and the highest charge-sheeting rate. Kerala also has the highest literacy and human-development indices. The inference is not that Kerala is the most criminal State; it is that Kerala records the most. Use this example whenever you are asked whether crime statistics reflect reality

12.2 Prison statistics — the companion data set

The DU solved paper of 2022 (Q5) is built on this data, so keep it ready. As against a sanctioned capacity of about 4,03,739, Indian prisons held about 4,78,600 inmates, an occupancy of roughly 118–130%; roughly 75% of all prisoners are undertrials; women are about 4% of the prison population; and there are about 1,330–1,400 prisons. Read this with Rama Murthy v. State of Karnataka (1997) 2 SCC 642 (the nine problems), In Re Inhuman Conditions in 1382 Prisons (2016) 3 SCC 700, In Re Contagion of COVID-19 Virus in Prisons (2020), the s.479 BNSS undertrial-release provision and the Model Prisons and Correctional Services Act, 2023.

The single most important inferencePut the two data sets side by side. Economic offences: 1,93,385 registered cases in 2022. Prison population: about 4.8 lakh, three-quarters of them undertrials, overwhelmingly poor, illiterate or semi-literate, and disproportionately Dalit, Adivasi and Muslim (Prison Statistics India). The people who fill Indian prisons are not the people who commit the offences that cost India the most. That sentence, supported by both figures, is the thesis of Topic 3 and the bridge to head C (§18 onwards) and to privileged class deviance (§38–§40).

13. How to read and criticise Indian crime statistics

13.1 Six rules for handling a figure in the examination

  1. Always prefer the rate to the number, and say why.
  2. Never infer causation from a change in registration. An increase may mean more crime, better reporting, a new offence, a policy drive, or a change in counting.
  3. Name the counting rule. Mentioning the principal offence rule in one clause signals that you have read the source and not a newspaper summary.
  4. Distinguish the three rates — crime rate, charge-sheeting rate, conviction rate — and say what each measures.
  5. Anchor the figure to a legal provision. “Cruelty by husband constitutes 31.4% of crimes against women” becomes far stronger as “…31.4% — that is, s.498-A IPC, now s.85 BNS, is the single largest head of recorded crime against women in India, which is why the misuse debate in Arnesh Kumar and Rajesh Sharma has such practical importance.”
  6. End with the dark figure. Every statistics paragraph should close with a sentence on what is missing.

13.2 The standard criticisms of the NCRB — a consolidated list

Methodological
Principal offence rule; case-based counting; no victim counts for most heads; no incident-level micro-data published; projected population denominator; delayed publication (the 2022 volume appeared in December 2023).
Institutional
No statutory backing and no audit power; total dependence on State police who have an institutional interest in showing low crime; no independent victimisation survey to validate the police figures — India’s single largest data gap.
Definitional
“Economic offences” is confined to criminal breach of trust, forgery/ cheating/fraud and counterfeiting — it therefore excludes tax evasion, foreign-exchange violations, customs frauds, environmental crime, adulteration, corporate fraud and most regulatory breaches, which are dealt with by departments that do not report to the NCRB. The official “economic offences” figure therefore systematically understates socio-economic crime — exactly the blind spot the 47th Report identified in 1972.
Discontinued and contested series
The NCRB has at various times stopped publishing or altered heads (for instance the detailed break-up of lynching, mob violence and farmers’ suicides categories), each time attracting criticism for reducing transparency. Data on caste of prisoners and on custodial deaths are collected but sparsely disaggregated. Say this if the question invites criticism of official statistics.
Political
Rankings create incentives to suppress; the “safest city” competition drives non-registration; and selective use of the data in public debate. The NCRB itself now prints a caution that a rise in cases “does not necessarily indicate a rise in crime”.
The theoretical objection
Recall Topic 1: if crime is what the powerful define as crime (the conflict perspective), then official statistics are a record of that definitional power, not of harm. Sutherland: the statistics tell you “why people are caught and prosecuted rather than why they commit crimes”.
A model paragraph you can reuse. “According to the NCRB’s Crime in India 2022, 58,24,946 cognizable offences were registered, a decline of 4.5% over 2021, and the crime rate fell from 445.9 to 422.2 per lakh. These figures must, however, be read with three caveats. First, they record registration, not crime: as the standard criticism has it, statistics of crime are ‘the most deceptive of all statistics’, and Radzinowicz estimated that offences brought into the open and punished ‘represent not more than fifteen per cent of the great mass actually committed’. Second, the principal offence rule counts one FIR under the gravest head alone, so every subsidiary offence is undercounted. Third, the head ‘economic offences’ is confined to breach of trust, cheating and counterfeiting and therefore excludes tax, customs, environmental and corporate offending altogether — the very categories the 47th Law Commission Report described as harming ‘the whole society’. The recorded decline of 4.5% is therefore evidence about the criminal justice system before it is evidence about crime.”

14. Traditional crimes — the concept and the divide

Syllabus head B

14.1 What “traditional crime” means

The syllabus contrasts “Traditional Crimes” (head B) with “Social and Economic Offences” (head C). “Traditional” — also called conventional, ordinary, street or blue-collar crime — means the offences that have been recognised as crimes in every society and in every legal system for centuries: the offences against the person, against property and against public order that make up the bulk of the Penal Code. They are, almost exactly, the offences the Supreme Court in Bachan Singh called mala in se.

CharacteristicTRADITIONAL CRIMESOCIAL & ECONOMIC OFFENCE (47th Report)
MotiveLust, hate, anger, jealousy, revenge, want; often emotional“Avarice or rapaciousness (not lust or hate)”
Emotional backgroundPresent; there is usually a relationship or an encounter between victim and offender“Non-emotional… There is no emotional reaction as between the victim and the offender”
VictimAn identifiable individual who knows he has been wronged and complains“Usually the State or a section of the public”; often the victim does not know
ModeForce or stealth“Fraud, not force”
VisibilityHigh; often public and immediateLow; concealed in books of account, servers and shell companies
DetectionPolice; FIR on the victim’s complaintRegulator, audit, whistle-blower, data analytics; frequently no complaint at all
StigmaSevere and immediateSlight; “respectable” offender; often described as a “lapse” or an “irregularity”
Typical offenderYoung, male, poor, low education (NCRB and Prison Statistics India bear this out)Educated, propertied, professionally qualified, older, well-advised
Punishment in practiceImprisonment; long undertrial detentionPenalty, compounding, settlement, adjudication; imprisonment rare
Recorded volume35.6 lakh IPC crimes (2022)1.93 lakh “economic offences” (2022) — but the category itself excludes most of the field (§13.2)
The essential caution. The divide is analytic, not watertight. Much “traditional” crime is now committed by organised groups and through technology (vehicle-theft rings, ATM skimming, sextortion), and much socio-economic crime produces traditional harm (death from adulterated liquor or spurious drugs; deaths at Bhopal). The correct statement for an answer is: the two categories differ in their social organisation, their mode of commission, their victim structure and the State’s response to them — not in the gravity of the harm they cause.

14.2 Why traditional crimes still dominate the Indian crime picture

  1. They are the ones that get recorded — an individual victim exists and complains (§10).
  2. They are the ones the police are trained, equipped and rewarded for — the entire station-house machinery is built around them.
  3. They carry the political weight — public fear, media coverage and legislative response are all organised around them (Durkheim: the punishment of the visible offence reaffirms the collective conscience).
  4. They are theoretically well explained — Merton’s strain, Shaw and McKay’s delinquency areas, Cohen’s subculture and Sutherland’s differential association were all built on traditional offending.
  5. They are concentrated in a demographic that has no political voice — the young, urban, migrant poor, which is why the criminal justice response to them is the most punitive part of the system.

15. Traditional crimes I — offences affecting the human body

Chapter VI of the BNS, 2023 (ss.100–146); formerly Chapter XVI IPC. Give the definition, the essential ingredients, the punishment and one Indian case or example for each. The BNS numbering is given first with the IPC number in brackets.

Abduction (s.138) = compelling by force or inducing by deceitful means a person to go from any place — not an offence by itself, only when done with one of the specified intents7 years (s.139); ransom kidnapping: death or life (s.140(2)); kidnapping for begging (s.139): 10 years to life. Largest single head of “crime against children” in NCRB 2022 (45.7%)
Trafficking in personsss.143–144 (370, 370-A)See §31 — placed by the BNS in the chapter on offences affecting the human body7–10 years; aggravated forms up to life
Rape and sexual offencesss.63–79 (375–376E, 354 series, 509)s.63 definition with seven descriptions and two exceptions; s.64 punishment (10 years to life) and aggravated forms; s.65 rape of a woman under 16 and under 12; s.66 causing death or a persistent vegetative state (20 years to life or death); s.70 gang rape (20 years to life; death where the victim is under 18); s.69 (new) sexual intercourse by deceitful means or false promise of marriage; s.75 sexual harassment; s.77 voyeurism; s.78 stalking, including “monitoring… the use by a woman of the internet, email or any other form of electronic communication”; s.79 word, gesture or act intended to insult the modesty of a womanRead with POCSO 2012, the Criminal Law (Amendment) Acts 2013 and 2018, ss.183, 184, 193(3) BNSS (recording of the victim’s statement, medical examination, completion of investigation in 2 months), s.120 BSA (no evidence of character or of previous sexual experience), and the presumption of absence of consent. Tukaram v. State of Maharashtra (Mathura, 1979) → 84th Law Commission Report → the 1983 Amendment; State of Punjab v. Gurmit Singh (1996) 2 SCC 384; Mukesh v. State (NCT of Delhi) (2017) 6 SCC 1 (Nirbhaya)
Statistics anchored to the sectionNCRB 2022: 28,522 murders (s.103 BNS), motive “dispute” in 9,962 — i.e. the largest single cause of murder in India is not gain or gang rivalry but interpersonal quarrel over land, money or family. This is an empirical refutation of the popular image of the professional killer and a direct vindication of the criminological point that most traditional violence is expressive, not instrumental — which is why deterrence works so poorly on it (Beccaria and Becker, Topic 2, §31.5).

16. Traditional crimes II — offences against property

Chapter XVII of the BNS (ss.303–334); formerly Chapter XVII IPC. The property offences form the statistical bulk of the IPC head. The scheme is best learnt as a ladder of aggravation.

OffenceBNS (IPC)Distinguishing elementPunishment
Thefts.303 (378–379)Dishonestly taking movable property out of the possession of another without his consent, with a moving of it. No force, no entrustmentUp to 3 years or fine or both; s.303(2) proviso (new): for a first-time offender where the value is under ₹5,000 and the property is returned — community service
Snatching (new)s.304Theft is “snatching” if, in order to commit theft, the offender suddenly or quickly or forcibly seizes or secures or grabs or takes away from any person any movable property3 years + fine. A direct legislative response to the commonest urban street crime
Extortions.308 (383–389)Intentionally putting a person in fear of injury and thereby dishonestly inducing him to deliver property or a valuable security. Consent is obtained, but by fear7 years; aggravated forms up to 10 years/life

The workhorse provision for online fraud
