State of A.P. (2009) 5 SCC 212 (the Thomas and Nariman Committee guidelines on rioters’ liability)
Criminal trespass and house-breakingss.329–334 (441–462)Entering into or remaining on property with intent to commit an offence or to intimidate, insult or annoy; lurking house-trespass and house-breaking by the six described modes; aggravated by night3 months to 14 years depending on the form and the offence intended
Receiving stolen propertyss.317 (410–414)Dishonestly receiving or retaining stolen property knowing or having reason to believe it to be stolen; habitual dealing3 years; habitually dealing: life or 10 years — the provision that targets the fence, without whom property crime cannot function
Forgery and counterfeitingss.336–347 (463–477A); ss.178–181 (currency)Making a false document or false electronic record with intent to cause damage or injury, to support a claim, or to commit fraud2–7 years; forgery of a valuable security, will or authority: life or 10 years; s.336(4) forgery for the purpose of harming reputation — 3 years; counterfeiting currency (s.178): life or 10 years
Where property crime becomes economic crime. Trace the ladder: theft → cheating → criminal breach of trust by a banker or agent (s.316(5)) → forgery of valuable securities (s.338) → counterfeiting → offences under the Companies Act s.447, SEBI Act, PMLA. The BNS itself contains the transition: the same chapter that punishes the pickpocket punishes the banker. What differs is not the section but the enforcement. That observation, made with these section numbers, is a very strong answer point.

17. Traditional crimes III — women, children, public tranquillity, the State and religion

17.1 Offences against women and children — Chapter V BNS (ss.63–99)

The BNS makes a symbolic and structural change: for the first time, offences against women and children are grouped in a dedicated chapter placed before the general chapter on offences against the human body. Contents: sexual offences (ss.63–73), criminal force and assault on a woman (ss.74–79), dowry death (s.80), cohabitation by deceit (s.81), bigamy (ss.82–83), enticing a married woman (s.84), cruelty by husband or his relatives (ss.85–86), kidnapping or inducing a woman to compel marriage (s.87), causing miscarriage (ss.88–92), exposure and abandonment of a child (s.93), concealment of birth (s.94), hiring or employing a child to commit an offence (s.95), procuration of a child (s.96), kidnapping a child for begging (s.97), selling or buying a child for prostitution (ss.98–99).

Allied statutes that must be cited alongside: Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act, 2012 (ss.3–12, 19 mandatory reporting, 29 and 30 presumptions, 28 special courts); Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act, 2015; Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act, 2005; Dowry Prohibition Act, 1961; Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, 2006; Immoral Traffic (Prevention) Act, 1956; Commission of Sati (Prevention) Act, 1987; PCPNDT Act, 1994; POSH Act, 2013; Child and Adolescent Labour (Prohibition and Regulation) Act, 1986.

17.2 Offences against public tranquillity — Chapter XI BNS (ss.189–197)

OffenceBNS (IPC)Core
Unlawful assemblys.189 (141–145)Five or more persons with a common object falling within the five clauses (overawing government by criminal force; resisting execution of law; committing mischief/criminal trespass/other offence; taking property by force; compelling a person to do what he is not legally bound to do). s.190: every member liable for the offence committed in prosecution of the common object
Riotings.191 (146–148)Force or violence used by an unlawful assembly; armed rioting: 5 years
Affrays.194 (159–160)Two or more persons fighting in a public place and disturbing the public peace
Promoting enmitys.196 (153-A)Words or acts promoting disharmony or feelings of enmity between groups on grounds of religion, race, place of birth, residence, language, caste or community — now expressly including communication “through electronic means”
Imputations prejudicial to national integrations.197 (153-B)Includes s.197(1)(d) (new) — making or publishing false or misleading information jeopardising the sovereignty, unity, integrity or security of India

17.3 Offences against the State — Chapter VII BNS (ss.147–158)

Waging war (s.147 — death or life); conspiracy to wage war (s.148); collecting arms (s.149); concealing a design to wage war (s.150); assaulting the President or a Governor (s.151); s.152 — acts endangering the sovereignty, unity and integrity of India, the successor to sedition under s.124-A IPC. Note the examinable history: s.124-A was upheld in Kedar Nath Singh v. State of Bihar AIR 1962 SC 955 (only incitement to violence or public disorder is punishable), and its operation was suspended by the Supreme Court in S.G. Vombatkere v. Union of India (2022) 7 SCC 433; the BNS has not abolished the idea but has recast it, and the width of s.152 (“subversive activities”, “separatist activities”, “by words, signs, visible representation, electronic communication or financial means”) is a live constitutional controversy.

17.4 Other traditional heads

Offences relating to religion
ss.298–302 BNS (295–298 IPC) — injuring or defiling a place of worship; deliberate and malicious acts intended to outrage religious feelings; disturbing a religious assembly; trespassing on burial places; uttering words to wound religious feelings.
Offences by or relating to public servants
ss.198–205 BNS (166–171 IPC), including s.199 (public servant disobeying the law with intent to cause injury) and s.200 (non-treatment of a victim, and failure to record information relating to specified offences). Read with the Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988.
False evidence and offences against public justice
ss.227–269 BNS (191–229A IPC) — perjury, fabricating evidence, s.238 causing disappearance of evidence (old s.201), harbouring an offender, resistance to lawful apprehension, escape from custody. These are the offences that make the “fixing” of organised crime possible (§24).
Defamation and criminal intimidation
ss.356 (499–500) and 351 (503, 506) BNS. Criminal defamation was upheld in Subramanian Swamy v. Union of India (2016) 7 SCC 221 — a much-criticised decision, and the classic modern Indian example of a privileged-class use of the criminal law (§38).
How a “Traditional Crimes” question is usually set. Rarely as a bare “describe traditional crimes”. Usually as: (a) “Distinguish traditional crimes from socio-economic offences” — use the table at §14.1; (b) a problem on a specific offence — give ingredients, section and case; or (c) “Discuss the changing pattern of traditional crime in India in the light of NCRB data” — combine §12 (figures) with §15–§17 (sections) and close with the observation that traditional crime is increasingly committed with organised and technological means, which is why the BNS has had to create snatching (s.304), organised crime (s.111), petty organised crime (s.112) and mob lynching (s.103(2)).

18. Social and Economic Offences — the 47th Law Commission Report: the SIX characteristics

PRESCRIBED READING DULaw Commission of India, 47th Report (1972)DU Case Material, pp. 94–96

This is the single most important text in Topic 3. The Law Commission of India, under the chairmanship of Mr. Justice P.B. Gajendragadkar, submitted its Forty-Seventh Report on “The Trial and Punishment of Social and Economic Offences” in 1972. The DU material reproduces the key paragraphs. Reproduce para 1.4 verbatim in any question on this head.

VERBATIM — 47th Report, para 1.4 · DU Case Material p. 94 DU “Social and Economic Offences
1.4. By now, the concept of anti-social acts and economic offences has become familiar to those acquainted with the progress of the criminal law and its relationship to the achievement of social objectives. Still, it may not be out of place to draw attention to some of the salient features of these offences… Briefly, these may be thus summarized:

(1) Motive of the criminal is AVARICE or RAPACIOUSNESS (not lust or hate).
(2) Background of the crime is NON-EMOTIONAL (unlike murder, rape, defamation etc.). There is no emotional reaction as between the victim and the offender.
(3) The VICTIM is usually the STATE or a section of the public, particularly the consuming public (i.e. that portion which consumes goods or services, buys shares or securities or other intangibles). Even where there is an individual victim, the more important element of the offence is harm to society.
(4) Mode of operation of the offender is FRAUD, NOT FORCE.
(5) Usually, the act is DELIBERATE AND WILFUL.
(6) Interest protected is two-fold:
  (a) Social interest in the PRESERVATION of:
    (i) the property or wealth or health of its individual members, and national resources, and
    (ii) the general economic system as a whole, from
      (i) exploitation, or
      (ii) waste by individuals or groups.
  (b) Social interest in the AUGMENTATION of the wealth of the country by enforcing the laws relating to taxes and duties, foreign exchange, foreign commerce, industries and the like.”

18.1 Unpacking the six characteristics — the commentary the examiner wants

CharacteristicWhat it meansLegal and practical consequencesIndian illustration
1. Motive: avarice or rapaciousness, not lust or hateThe offence is committed for gain. It is instrumental, not expressive. This aligns the socio-economic offender with Beccaria’s and Becker’s rational calculator, and with Merton’s innovatorBecause the motive is gain, the rational deterrent works better here than for crimes of passion — which is why the correct penal response is confiscation, disgorgement and fine geared to the profit, not merely imprisonmentHoarding of essential commodities before a festival; over-invoicing of imports; tax evasion
2. Non-emotional background; no emotional reaction between victim and offenderThere is no encounter, no relationship, no confrontation. Often the offender never meets the victim and does not think of himself as harming anyoneThis is the precondition of neutralisation (Sykes and Matza — “denial of the victim”, “denial of injury”) and it is why these offenders suffer no loss of self-image or of social standingAn adulterating trader does not see the child who is poisoned; a tax evader believes he is “saving”, not stealing
3. Victim: the State or a section of the public, especially the consuming publicThe victim is diffuse. The loss is spread thinly across millions, so no single person has an incentive to prosecuteThe classic collective-action problem. It is why enforcement must be State-initiated and regulatory, and why class actions (s.37 Consumer Protection Act, 2019; s.245 Companies Act, 2013) and public interest litigation have become the structural answerA ₹1 per litre adulteration of milk across a State; a cartel raising cement prices; the 2G spectrum allocation
4. Mode: fraud, not forceThe instrument is deceit, concealment, falsification and abuse of position — not violenceInvestigation requires forensic accounting, audit, document analysis and digital forensics, not the traditional police skills of the thana. Hence the proliferation of specialist agencies: SFIO, ED, DRI, CBI, SEBI, CCISatyam’s falsified accounts (2009); the PNB letters of undertaking (2018); circular trading
5. Deliberate and wilfulThese are not offences of impulse or negligence; they are planned, often over years, with professional adviceMorally this makes them more, not less, culpable — which is the Commission’s foundation for recommending harsher treatment. Legally it is why the Commission recommended shifting the burden on mens rea: the mental element plainly exists but is impossible to prove from outsideShell-company structures created years before the fraud; layered hawala transfers
6. Twofold interest protected: preservation and augmentationThe law protects both the existing wealth, health and resources of the nation from exploitation and waste, and the growth of national wealth through tax, foreign exchange, commerce and industry lawsThis is the constitutional link: Arts. 38, 39(b) and (c), 41, 43, 47 and 48-A — the Directive Principles. Socio-economic offences are offences against the Directive Principles, which is why the 47th Report speaks of the “Welfare State”Illegal mining destroying a national resource (Goa Foundation v. Union of India (2014) 6 SCC 590); black money outside the country (Black Money Act, 2015)
The one-line memory device. A-N-V-F-D-T: Avarice · Non-emotional · Victim is the State/public · Fraud not force · Deliberate and wilful · Twofold interest (preservation + augmentation).

19. 47th Report, paras 1.5–1.9 — the victimless character, the categories, the intersecting circles

19.1 Para 1.5 — why special enforcement effort is needed (verbatim)

VERBATIM — 47th Report, para 1.5 · DU Case Material p. 94 DU “1.5. The most important feature of these offences is the fact that ordinarily they DO NOT INVOLVE AN INDIVIDUAL DIRECT VICTIM but are punished because they harm the WHOLE SOCIETY. This constitutes the primary reason why special efforts have to be made to enforce them. If a man or woman is robbed, assaulted or cheated, there is some person who is interested in getting the offender prosecuted, and because the act is a physical one having an immediate and direct impact, both individual and social vengeance are likely to be aroused. This element is, however, absent when, for example, essential commodities are hoarded, or foreign exchange is illegally taken out of the country, or prohibited goods are imported. No doubt, some social offences do involve a ‘victim’. For example, when adulterated food is sold, the immediate consumer is harmed. But the criminal act is potentially capable of harming a large number of persons and that is the principal object behind punishing it.”

Comment. This paragraph explains the entire enforcement architecture of socio-economic offences in India. Because no private person will complain, the State must (i) create specialist agencies with suo motu powers (ED, DRI, SFIO, NCB, CCI, SEBI, FIU-IND); (ii) impose reporting duties on intermediaries (s.12 PMLA — banks and reporting entities must file Cash and Suspicious Transaction Reports; s.143 Companies Act — auditor’s duty to report fraud); (iii) provide whistle-blower protection (the Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014, still largely unoperationalised — a fair criticism); and (iv) allow representative and public-interest proceedings. Where the victim cannot complain, the law must complain for him.

19.2 Para 1.6 — the eight categories of socio-economic offence (verbatim)

VERBATIM — 47th Report, para 1.6 · DU Case Material pp. 94–95 DU “Categories of Social and Economic Offences
1.6. The Law Commission had, in an earlier Report (29th Law Commission Report), occasion to deal with the following categories of offences, while considering the question whether provisions as to social and economic offences should be transferred to the Penal Code:
1) Offences calculated to prevent or obstruct the economic development of the country and endanger its economic health
2) Evasion of taxes
3) Misuse of position by public servants
4) Offences in the nature of breaches of contracts, resulting in the delivery of goods not according to specifications
5) Hoarding and Black-marketing
6) Adulteration of food and drugs
7) Theft and misappropriation of public property and funds
8) Trafficking in licences, permits etc.”
The fuller wording in the 29th Report (worth knowing, because the DU extract abbreviates items 2, 3 and 4): “(2) evasion and avoidance of taxes lawfully imposed; (3) misuse of their position by public servants in the making of contracts and disposal of public property, issue of licences and permits and similar other matters; (4) delivery by individuals and industrial and commercial undertakings of goods not in accordance with agreed specifications in fulfilment of contracts entered into with public authorities; (5) profiteering, black-marketing and hoarding.” Note also the constitutional question behind para 1.6: the 29th Report (1966) had recommended that these offences should not be transferred into the Penal Code, and the Law Commission reiterated that view — “such offences are better left to be dealt with by special and self-contained enactments which supplement the basic criminal law.” That single sentence explains why India has no “Chapter on White-Collar Crime” in the IPC or the BNS, notwithstanding the Santhanam Committee’s recommendation that one be inserted (§22).
#Category (47th Report)Indian statutes todayEnforcement agency
1Obstructing economic development / endangering economic healthFEMA 1999; COFEPOSA 1974; SAFEMA 1976; Competition Act 2002; IBC 2016; Black Money (Undisclosed Foreign Income and Assets) Act 2015; Fugitive Economic Offenders Act 2018Enforcement Directorate; CCI; SFIO
2Evasion of taxesIncome-tax Act 1961 ss.276C, 277, 278, 278E; CGST Act 2017 ss.132, 135; Customs Act 1962 ss.132, 135; Central Excise Act 1944 s.9CBDT; CBIC; DRI
3Misuse of position by public servantsPrevention of Corruption Act 1988 (as amended 2018) ss.7, 7A, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13, 14; BNS ss.198–205; Benami Transactions (Prohibition) Act 1988/2016; Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act 2013CBI; State ACBs/Vigilance; CVC; Lokpal
4Breach of contract with public authorities — supply of substandard goodsBNS ss.316, 318; PCA s.13(1)(d) (as it stood); the General Financial Rules; blacklisting jurisprudence — Erusian Equipment v. State of W.B. (1975) 1 SCC 70CBI; CAG audit; procurement authorities
5Hoarding and black-marketingEssential Commodities Act 1955 ss.3, 7, 10; Prevention of Black-marketing and Maintenance of Supplies of Essential Commodities Act 1980 (preventive detention); Legal Metrology Act 2009State civil supplies; police

19.3 Paras 1.8 and 1.9 — white-collar crime and the INTERSECTING CIRCLES (verbatim)

VERBATIM — 47th Report, paras 1.8–1.9 · DU Case Material p. 95 DU “Socio-economic Offences Form [an] Intersecting Circle with White-collar Crimes
1.8. …Without entering into details of the definition of ‘white-collar crime’, one may, for the purpose of the present Report, describe it as a crime committed in the course of one’s occupation by a member of the upper class of society. A manufacturer of drugs who deliberately supplies sub-standard drugs is, for example, a white collar criminal. So is a big corporation guilty of fraudulent evasion of tax. A person who illegally smuggles (for his personal use) costly television sets, is NOT a white-collar criminal in the above sense, there being no connection between his occupation and the crime committed by him. Nor is a pensioner who submits false returns of income. But all of them are guilty of social or economic offences. In short, SOCIAL OFFENCES are offences which affect the health or material welfare of the community as a whole, and not merely of the individual victim. Similarly, ECONOMIC OFFENCES are those which affect the country’s economy, and not merely the wealth of an individual victim.

1.9. Socio-economic offences and white-collar crimes could thus be INTERSECTING CIRCLES. Again, socio-economic offences and crimes of STRICT LIABILITY also could be represented by intersecting circles.”
The two examples in para 1.8 are traps — learn them. The Report deliberately gives negative examples: (i) the man who smuggles a costly television set for his own use is not a white-collar criminal, “there being no connection between his occupation and the crime”; (ii) nor is a pensioner who submits false returns of income. Both are nonetheless guilty of social or economic offences. A candidate who reproduces these two examples demonstrates that he has read the primary text and not a summary.

19.4 The definitional structure to memorise

White-collar crime
Defined by the offender and the occupation: “a crime committed in the course of one’s occupation by a member of the upper class of society” (47th Report, para 1.8); Sutherland: “a crime committed by a person of respectability and high social status in the course of his occupation” (1949).
Social offence
Defined by the interest harmed: “offences which affect the health or material welfare of the community as a whole, and not merely of the individual victim”. Examples: food and drug adulteration, spurious medicine, unsafe buildings, pollution, illicit liquor.
Economic offence
Also defined by the interest harmed: offences “which affect the country’s economy, and not merely the wealth of an individual victim”. Examples: tax evasion, foreign- exchange violations, smuggling, counterfeiting, bank fraud, money laundering.
Strict-liability offence
Defined by the mental element: mens rea modified or eliminated. Overlaps the other three but is not co-extensive with any of them (para 1.9).

20. 47th Report, paras 3.13–3.16 — stringent action and “public welfare offences”

VERBATIM — 47th Report, para 3.13 · DU Case Material p. 95 DU “Need for Stringent Action
3.13. These offences, affecting as they do the health and wealth of the entire community, require to be PUT DOWN WITH A HEAVY HAND at a time when the country has embarked upon a gigantic process of social and economic planning. With its vastness in size, its magnitude of problems and its long history of poverty and subjugation, our Welfare State needs weapons of attack on poverty, ill nourishment, and exploitation that are SHARP AND EFFECTIVE in contrast with the weapons intended to repress other evils. The legislative armoury for fighting socio-economic crimes, therefore, should be furnished with weapons which may not be needed for fighting ordinary crimes. The damage caused by socio-economic offences to a developing society could be treated on a level different from ordinary crimes. In a sense, anti-social activities in the nature of deliberate and persistent violations of economic laws could be described as EXTRA-HAZARDOUS ACTIVITIES, and it is in this light that we approach the problem [of offences which] really deserve the name of ‘public welfare’ offences.”

The crucial sentence for a constitutional-law answer comes a few lines later in the Report itself: “Hence, if legislation applicable to such offences, as a matter of policy, departs from legislation applicable to ordinary crimes in respect of the traditional requirements as to mens rea and the other substantive matters as well as on points of procedure, the departure would, we think, be JUSTIFIABLE.” That is the Law Commission’s authorisation, in 1972, for the entire modern architecture of reverse burdens and twin-condition bail in the NDPS Act, PMLA, UAPA and MCOCA. Whether that authorisation has been abused is one of the best essay questions available on this topic.

VERBATIM — 47th Report, paras 3.15–3.16 · DU Case Material pp. 95–96 DU “The Concept of Public Welfare Offences
3.15. Long ago, Sayre cited and classified a large number of cases of ‘public welfare offences’ and concluded that they fall roughly into subdivisions of (1) illegal sale of intoxicating liquor, (2) sales of impure or adulterated food or drugs, (3) sale of misbranded articles, (4) violations of anti-narcotic Acts, (5) criminal nuisances, (6) violations of traffic regulations, (7) violations of motor-vehicles laws, and (8) violations of general police regulations passed for the safety, health or well-being of the community.

3.16. The time has come when the concept of ‘public welfare offences’ should be given a NEW DIMENSION and extended to cover activities that affect national health or wealth ON A BIG SCALE. Demands of the economic prosperity of the nation have brought into being risks of a volume and variety unheard of, and if those concerned with the transactions and activities in this field were not to observe new standards of care and conduct, vital damage will be caused to the public welfare. In the field of health, for example, the wide distribution of goods has become an instrument of wide distribution of HARM. When those who disperse food, drink and drugs do not comply with the prescribed standard of quality, integrity, disclosure and care, public welfare receives a vital blow. In the economic field, again, freshly discovered sources of harm require the imposition of a higher type of precautions, without which there would be vital damage to the fabric of the country and even to its very survival.”
Sayre’s eight categories — note what they are
Francis Bowes Sayre’s classic 1933 article “Public Welfare Offenses” identified strict-liability regulatory offences. Notice that four of Sayre’s eight categories are squarely within Topic 3 head C: liquor, adulterated food and drugs, misbranding, and anti-narcotic Acts. The 47th Report’s move in para 3.16 is to expand the category from petty regulation to “activities that affect national health or wealth on a big scale”.
“The wide distribution of goods has become an instrument of wide distribution of harm”
This is the most quotable sentence in the Report and the best one-line justification for strict liability in consumer-protection and product-safety law. Modern Indian examples: the contaminated cough syrups linked to child deaths in The Gambia and Uzbekistan (2022–23) and the resulting revision of Schedule M of the Drugs and Cosmetics Rules; recalls of adulterated spices (2024); the Maggi noodles proceedings (2015).
“Extra-hazardous activities”
The Report’s analogy is to the law of strict liability in tort (Rylands v. Fletcher), and it anticipates by fifteen years the Supreme Court’s creation of absolute liability in M.C. Mehta v. Union of India (1987) 1 SCC 395 for enterprises engaged in hazardous activity — “an enterprise which is engaged in a hazardous or inherently dangerous industry… owes an absolute and non-delegable duty to the community”, with no exceptions, and damages correlated to the magnitude and capacity of the enterprise.
“New standards of care and conduct”
Implemented today through statutory compliance duties: s.134(5)(e) Companies Act 2013 (internal financial controls); Regulation 4 of the SEBI (LODR) Regulations; the RBI’s KYC Master Direction; s.12 PMLA; SA 240 on the auditor’s responsibility for fraud; the corporate offence in s.9 of the Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988 (commercial organisations giving bribes) with the “adequate procedures” defence.

21. 47th Report — the recommendations on trial and punishment

The DU extract stops at para 3.16, but a complete answer needs the Report’s recommendations, since the Report’s very title is “The Trial and Punishment of Social and Economic Offences”. The Commission examined some thirty central Acts and made both general and Act-specific recommendations.

21.1 On mens rea — the central recommendation

47th Report, paras 7.2–7.3 and 3.20 — substance “Traditional criminal jurisprudence requires that before criminal liability be imposed, a certain mental element of the offender must be proved… Necessities of the time have, however, witnessed the emergence of a category of offences — sometimes called ‘public welfare offences’ — where the element of mens rea has undergone modification or even suffered elimination… It is for these reasons that we have thought of a solution which, while PRESERVING the requirement of mens rea — a requirement which we would be loath to dispense with in any Act carrying serious punishment — THROWS THE BURDEN OF PROOF ON THE ACCUSED. Petty cases causing minor injuries are not worth the trouble of creating a special rule as to burden of proof. But acts causing substantial damage justify a departure, to the extent indicated above.

3.20. …we have recommended placing the burden of DISPROVING mens rea on the accused… the substantive provision would not, in its formulation, be so unreasonable as to attach culpability where there is no intention to evade its provisions and no want of reasonable care.”
This is a nuanced, examinable position. The Commission did not recommend abolishing mens rea; it recommended keeping the requirement and reversing the burden, with a saving for the person who had no intention to evade and was not careless. Compare what Parliament actually did: s.7 of the Essential Commodities Act — which the Commission itself said “goes beyond merely throwing the burden of proof on the accused and practically eliminates mens rea”, and suggested should be brought back into line. The gap between the recommendation and the legislation is a first-class critical point.

Where the recommendation was enacted: s.278E Income-tax Act, 1961; s.138A Customs Act, 1962; s.35 NDPS Act, 1985; s.20 Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988; s.24 PMLA, 2002; s.113-A and s.113-B Evidence Act (now ss.117–118 BSA) — each a statutory presumption of culpable mental state or of the fact in issue. The constitutional validity of such reverse burdens has been upheld, with safeguards, in Noor Aga v. State of Punjab (2008) 16 SCC 417 and Vijay Madanlal Choudhary v. Union of India (2022) SCC OnLine SC 929, on the footing that the foundational facts must first be proved by the prosecution.

21.2 The other general recommendations

RecommendationContentWhat Indian law did
Increase in maximum punishmentThe Commission recommended raising the maximum imprisonment in the Acts it examined (Central Excise s.9; Customs s.135; Gold (Control) Act s.85; Income-tax ss.276B, 276C, 277, 278, etc.)Largely accepted — see the Taxation Laws (Amendment) Act, 1975, which implemented several of the Report’s recommendations
MINIMUM imprisonment and MINIMUM fineMandatory minima, with a power in the court to award less “for special and adequate reasons to be recorded in writing”. Example given: Customs s.135(1) — minimum six months, maximum five years and fine, where the value of the goods exceeds ₹1 lakhNow the standard drafting technique: s.276C Income-tax; s.135 Customs; ss.15–25 NDPS; s.4 PMLA; s.3 Dowry Prohibition Act; s.376 IPC/s.64 BNS
Fine geared to the gain“To those who make a lot of money through infringement of laws, monetary penalties do not really serve as deterrents” unless calibrated to the profitSEBI Act s.15G (insider trading — penalty of ₹10 lakh or three times the profit, whichever is higher); Competition Act s.27(b) (up to three times the profit or 10% of turnover); Companies Act s.447; PMLA attachment and confiscation; s.13(2) PCA
Exclusion of the Probation of Offenders ActBenefit of probation and admonition should not be available for socio-economic offencesIshar Das v. State of Punjab (1973) 2 SCC 65 — the Supreme Court noted the recommendation and held that courts should not lightly extend the Probation Act to food-adulteration offences; see also s.18 of the Probation of Offenders Act, 1958 and Prem Ballab v. State (Delhi Admn.) (1977) 1 SCC 173
Special courts and speedy trialSpecial courts with subject-matter expertise; simplified procedure; appeal to lie directly to the High CourtNDPS Special Courts (s.36); PMLA Special Courts (s.43); CBI courts; Special Courts Act, 1979; the Companies Act Special Courts (s.435); SEBI Special Courts (s.26A SEBI Act); NIA Special Courts
Corporate liability — fine where imprisonment is impossibleWhere the offence is punishable with imprisonment, or with imprisonment and fine, and the offender is a corporation, the court should be empowered to sentence it to fine onlyNot enacted. The resulting difficulty produced Assistant Commissioner v. Velliappa Textiles (2003) 11 SCC 405 (a company could not be prosecuted where a minimum sentence was mandatory), which was overruled by the Constitution Bench in Standard Chartered Bank v. Directorate of Enforcement (2005) 4 SCC 530 — a company may be prosecuted and sentenced to fine even where imprisonment is mandatory; and by Iridium India Telecom v. Motorola (2011) 1 SCC 74 on attributing mens rea to a corporation. This is the best single illustration of the Report’s continuing influence and is frequently examinable.
Public censure and publication of names“Public censure” as an additional sanction — the loss of reputation being the sanction the white-collar offender actually fearss.287 Income-tax Act (publication of names of tax defaulters); SEBI debarment orders; the RBI wilful-defaulter list; blacklisting; the “name and shame” lists of the Serious Fraud Investigation Office
Preventive detention; stoppage of business; cancellation of licenceAdministrative disabling measures alongside punishmentCOFEPOSA, 1974; PBMSEC Act, 1980; PITNDPS Act, 1988; cancellation of licences under the FSSA and the Drugs and Cosmetics Act; disqualification of directors (s.164 Companies Act); s.29A IBC; the Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018 (confiscation and bar on civil claims)
Better investigation and expertiseThe Commission identified the causes of defective enforcement: absence of legislative provisions; absence of the relevant statutory notification; faulty investigation; lack of legal expertise; procedural drawbacks; want of evidence; administrative difficultiesCreation of the SFIO (2003, statutory under s.211 Companies Act 2013), the Directorate of Revenue Intelligence, the Economic Offences Wings of State police, the Central Economic Intelligence Bureau, and forensic-audit requirements
Proportioned “measures”Para 7.52: “If the punishable act has caused no harmful effects, punishment may be mild. If it has caused some harm but the offender can repair the damage done to society, probation would be appropriate. If the harm is serious, imprisonment would, of course, be required.”A graded, restorative approach that anticipates modern compounding, settlement and disgorgement regimes — and shows the Report was not merely punitive
How to answer “Critically examine the 47th Law Commission Report”. Praise: (i) the first systematic Indian analysis of socio-economic crime, eleven years before the topic entered university syllabi; (ii) the six characteristics remain the standard definition; (iii) the intersecting- circles insight; (iv) a nuanced position on mens rea that preserved the principle while shifting the burden; (v) a graded scheme of “measures”. Criticism: (i) it left socio-economic offences outside the Penal Code, so they remain a scattered mass of special statutes with no unifying principle and no place in the public moral imagination; (ii) its reverse-burden recommendation has been used far beyond what it contemplated, producing the twin-condition bail regimes of NDPS, PMLA and UAPA and mass undertrial detention; (iii) it assumed a “Welfare State… embarked upon a gigantic process of social and economic planning” — a 1972 premise that the post-1991 liberalised economy has displaced, and the Jan Vishwas Act, 2023, which decriminalised 183 provisions across 42 central Acts, moves in exactly the opposite direction; (iv) its corporate-liability recommendation was never enacted, leaving the courts to fix the problem in Standard Chartered Bank; (v) it said nothing about victim compensation, and nothing about the political and bureaucratic protection that is, as Pande shows (§38), the real reason these offences go unpunished.

22. Socio-economic offences in Indian law — the statute and enforcement-agency map

The 47th Report’s view that these offences are “better left to be dealt with by special and self-contained enactments which supplement the basic criminal law” has produced a very large, scattered body of law. The following table is the standard Indian “bird’s-eye view” of the field, giving the offence, the legislation and the enforcement authority. Reproducing even a dozen rows of it transforms an answer.

Benami transactionsBenami Transactions (Prohibition) Act, 1988, substantially amended in 2016Initiating Officer / Adjudicating Authority under the Act; Income-tax Department
Standard Indian commentary on the state of enforcement “Economic offences may either be cognizable or non-cognizable in nature. The local police deals with a considerable number of economic offences falling under the broad category of ‘cheating’, ‘counterfeiting’ and ‘criminal breach of trust’. The aforesaid special laws regulating customs, excise, taxes, foreign exchange, narcotic drugs, banking, insurance, trade and commerce… have been enacted and enforced by the respective departmental enforcement agencies created under the statutory provisions. The powers for investigation, adjudication, imposing of fines, penalties (and under special circumstances arrest and detention of persons) are derived from the concerned special legislation. The officers of the enforcement agencies are also vested with powers to summon witnesses, search and seize goods, documents and confiscate the proceeds. However, despite the special laws and independent enforcement agencies for handling economic crimes there is NO DECLINE in the crime rate; on the contrary, it is constantly rising. It is indeed a serious cause of concern for all those who are concerned with the administration of criminal justice.”
The structural criticism to make. Note what this table reveals. Every socio-economic offence is enforced by a specialist departmental authority that possesses powers of adjudication, penalty, compounding and settlement — that is, powers to resolve the matter without a criminal trial. The ordinary offender has no such avenue. This is Austin Turk’s “sophistication” variable (Topic 2, §28.4) built into the Indian statute book: the sophisticated offender has a regulatory exit, the unsophisticated has only the criminal court. Add the modern examples — SEBI consent/settlement orders (s.15JB SEBI Act and the Settlement Regulations, 2018), compounding under s.441 Companies Act and s.279(2) Income-tax Act, the GST compounding provision in s.138 CGST Act, customs compounding under s.137(3), and the Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Act, 2023, which converted 183 criminal provisions across 42 central Acts into civil penalties.

23. White-collar crime in India

23.1 The concept and its Indian setting

Standard Indian commentary “White collar criminality has become a global phenomenon with the advance of commerce and technology. Like any other country, India is equally in the grip of white collar criminality. The reason for [the] enormous increase in white collar crime in recent decades is to be found in the fast developing economy and industrial growth of this developing country.”

Trace the intellectual history in one paragraph: E.H. Sutherland’s presidential address to the American Sociological Society in 1939 and his book White Collar Crime (1949) — “a crime committed by a person of respectability and high social status in the course of his occupation”; his study of the 70 largest US corporations, which had 980 adverse decisions against them, of which only 158 were criminal convictions; his insistence that the conduct was criminal because it was legally punishable, even if dealt with by regulatory tribunals — Paul Tappan’s objection that only a person convicted by a criminal court is a criminal, and Sutherland’s reply that differential implementation of the law cannot change the nature of the conduct. Then bring it to India.

23.2 The Santhanam Committee on Prevention of Corruption (1964) — the classic Indian passage

Verbatim — Report of the Committee on Prevention of Corruption (Santhanam Committee), 1964, para 2.13, p. 11 “The advance of technological and scientific development is contributing to the emergence of ‘mass society’ with a large rank and file and a small controlling elite, encouraging the growth of monopolies, the rise of a managerial class and intricate institutional mechanisms. Strict adherence to [a] high standard of ethical behaviour is necessary for the even and honest functioning of the new social, political and economic processes. The inability of all sections of society to appreciate this need in full results in the emergence and growth of white collar and economic crimes, renders enforcement of the laws, themselves not sufficiently deterrent, more difficult. Tax evasion and avoidance, share-pushing, malpractices in the share market and administration of companies, monopolistic control, usury, under-invoicing or over-invoicing, hoarding, profiteering, sub-standard performance of contracts of construction and supply, evasion of economic laws, bribery and corruption, election offences and malpractices are some examples of white collar crime.”

What followed. “The Commission broadly classified white collar and socio-economic crimes into eight categories and suggested the insertion of a new chapter on white collar crimes in the Indian Penal Code. The matter was referred by the Government to the Law Commission of India for consideration. The Law Commission, however, disagreed with the proposal and observed that ‘such offences are better left to be dealt with by special and self-contained enactments which supplement the basic criminal law’.” That disagreement — the Santhanam Committee for codification, the Law Commission against it — is the single most examinable institutional fact in this part of the syllabus. It also explains the Santhanam Committee’s other legacy: the creation of the Central Vigilance Commission in 1964 (given statutory force by the CVC Act, 2003).

23.3 The classic Indian inquiries

Vivian Bose Commission of Inquiry (1963)
Inquiry into the affairs of the Dalmia–Jain group of companies. It “highlight[ed] how these big industries indulge in white collar crimes such as fraud, falsification of accounts, tampering with records for personal gains and tax evasion.” The first Indian judicial demonstration of Sutherland’s thesis.
Justice M.C. Chagla on Haridas Mundhra (1958)
Chagla J. said of the business magnate that he wanted to “build up an industrial empire by dubious means”. “There were as many as 124 prosecutions against this business tycoon and companies owned or controlled by him between 1958 and 1960, and as many as 113 of them resulted in conviction.” The LIC–Mundhra scandal, India’s first great post-Independence financial scandal, produced the resignation of the Finance Minister — a rare instance of accountability.
The scam sequence
Bofors (1987) and the Fairfax findings; the FERA inquiries of 1988 into a Delhi firm that received ₹6.5 crore in commission from ONGC; the Harshad Mehta securities scam of 1991–92 (JPC; Action Taken Report tabled 27 July 1994) “involving persons holding high positions in certain banks including… the State Bank of India”; the Ketan Parekh stock-market scam of 2001 (a 30-member JPC — 20 Lok Sabha and 10 Rajya Sabha — appointed in April 2001); the Telgi fake stamp-paper scam (2003); Satyam (2009); 2G and Coalgate (2010–12); NSEL (2013); Vyapam (2013); PNB/Nirav Modi ₹13,500 crore (2018); IL&FS (2018); PMC Bank (2019); ABG Shipyard ₹22,842 crore (2022).
Hoarding, black-marketing and adulteration
“The white collar crimes which are common to Indian trade and business world are hoarding, profiteering and black marketing. Violation of foreign exchange regulations and import and export laws are frequently resorted to for the sake of huge profits. That apart, adulteration of foodstuffs, edibles and drugs, which causes irreparable danger to public health, is yet another white collar crime common in India. The Law Commission has suggested drastic measures against such offenders. In the Commission’s observation the tedious prosecution process involved in the trial of such cases frustrates the cause of justice and often results in unjustified acquittal due to [a] defective report of the analyst or delay in examination of samples or lack of legal expertise” (47th Report, p. 83).
Tax evasion v. tax avoidance
“What constitutes crime is ‘tax evasion’ and not ‘tax avoidance’. Though both these terms appear to be synonymous, there is a fine distinction between the two. While the former implies non-payment of tax due to be paid, the latter signifies arranging the spread over of one’s income in such a way that it does not incur tax liability legally and lawfully” (Provident Investment Company v. CIT AIR 1954 Bom 95). On the Indian anti-avoidance response: McDowell & Co. v. CTO (1985) 3 SCC 230 (Chinnappa Reddy J.’s attack on “colourable devices”), Union of India v. Azadi Bachao Andolan (2004) 10 SCC 1 and Vodafone International Holdings v. Union of India (2012) 6 SCC 613 (form over substance), and the enactment of the General Anti-Avoidance Rules (GAAR), Chapter X-A of the Income-tax Act, in force from 1 April 2017.
Black money and disclosure schemes
“Tax of several crores of rupees is evaded annually by assessees in higher income groups, which results in circulation of black money. The Wanchoo Committee (1970) made important recommendations to unearth black money. The Government introduced the VDIS (Voluntary Disclosure of Income Scheme), 1997… [which] has not yielded the desired results. The main reason… seems to be that tax payers do not want to be identified as having evaded tax in the past and the fear of re-opening of their past assessments and facing roving enquiries also dissuades them.” In R.K. Garg v. Union of India (1981) 4 SCC 675 the Supreme Court, by majority, upheld the Special Bearer Bonds (Immunities and Exemptions) Act, 1981, holding that the Act was “not intended to encourage tax evasion in future [or to] condone such evasion committed in the past”, its real object being “to launch a nation-wide search to unearth undisclosed wealth” — a case worth citing on the tension between pragmatism and principle in economic legislation. Later schemes: the Income Declaration Scheme 2016 and the Pradhan Mantri Garib Kalyan Yojana 2016 after demonetisation.

23.4 White-collar crime in the professions

Standard Indian commentary “Some of the professions involving technical expertise and skill provide sufficient opportunities for white collar criminality. They include medical profession, engineering, legal practice, private educational institutions etc.”
ProfessionTypical white-collar offencesRegulatory and penal response
Medical“Issuance of false medical certificates, helping illegal abortions, secret service to dacoits by giving expert opinion leading to their acquittal, and selling sample drugs and medicines to patients or chemists. Dilatory tactics adopted… in [the] treatment of their patients with a view to extracting huge sums from them has become an accepted norm.” Also: unnecessary surgery and investigations, cuts and commissions for referrals, organ-trade participation, and the procurement of false sickness certificates by employees who cannot obtain leaveNational Medical Commission Act, 2019 and the Professional Conduct Regulations, 2002 (Reg. 6.4.1 bars rebates and commissions); Transplantation of Human Organs and Tissues Act, 1994; MTP Act, 1971; PCPNDT Act, 1994; s.336(4) BNS (forgery harming reputation); Jacob Mathew v. State of Punjab (2005) 6 SCC 1; Indian Medical Association v. V.P. Shantha (1995) 6 SCC 651 (services under the Consumer Protection Act)
Legal“There is a great divergence between the prescribed codes of ethics for the professional lawyers and their practice… It often involves resort to unethical practices such as touting, unfair bargaining with colleagues and other unfair tactics… Since the criminals often remain in close contact with their counsel, the former are often influenced by the latter’s behavioural pattern and thus tend to learn unethical practices” — a striking application of differential associationAdvocates Act, 1961 ss.35–38 (professional misconduct); Bar Council of India Rules, Part VI Ch. II (Rule 36 — no advertising); Ex-Capt. Harish Uppal v. Union of India (2003) 2 SCC 45 (no right to strike); R.D. Saxena v. Balram Prasad Sharma (2000) 7 SCC 264
Engineering and constructionUnder-specification in public contracts, use of substandard material, false completion certificates, collusive biddingPCA s.13; Competition Act s.3(3)(d) (bid rigging); blacklisting; the Commonwealth Games (2010) procurement scandal — “large-scale corruption in contracts for construction of overlays, sports grounds, technological equipments, accommodation arrangements, sponsorships, event-management… a glaring illustration [of] how organised crimes emerge out of an otherwise legitimate activity” (Shunglu Committee; CAG Report)
EducationCapitation fees, fake degrees and mark-sheets, paper leaks, ghost teachers, bogus institutions. “Rackets selling fake degrees and mark-sheets to needy persons are operating in several parts of India… in liaison with some of the University employees”Mohini Jain v. State of Karnataka (1992) 3 SCC 666 and Unni Krishnan v. State of A.P. (1993) 1 SCC 645 (capitation fee unconstitutional); Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024 (3–10 years and fine up to ₹1 crore for organised paper leaks); the Vyapam scam; the NEET-UG 2024 controversy
Business and advertising“Fake and misleading advertising is yet another area in which the white collar criminals operate. They make illegal and misleading claims of medical cure through advertisements in newspapers, magazines, radio and television, thus adding to human misery. Many patent medicines are not only worthless but harmful. Similar advertisements for cosmetics and adulterated food are also widespread”Drugs and Magic Remedies (Objectionable Advertisements) Act, 1954; s.89 Consumer Protection Act, 2019; the CCPA Guidelines for Prevention of Misleading Advertisements, 2022; ASCI Code; Indian Medical Association v. Union of India (the 2024 Patanjali contempt proceedings)
Chartered accountancy, audit and bankingFalsified audit certificates, window dressing, evergreening of loans, connivance in diversion of fundsChartered Accountants Act, 1949 Schedules I and II; National Financial Reporting Authority (s.132 Companies Act, 2013); s.143(12) auditor’s duty to report fraud; RBI Master Directions on Frauds; Deloitte Haskins & Sells LLP v. Union of India (2023) — SFIO proceedings against auditors of IL&FS upheld

23.5 Why white-collar crime persists — and the standard conclusion

Standard Indian commentary — the concluding passage “[I]n a developing country like India, where population is fast escalating, economic offences are increasing by leaps and bounds besides the traditional crimes. These are mostly associated with the middle and upper class of society and have added a new chapter to criminal jurisprudence. To a great extent, they are an outcome of industrial and commercial developments and the progress of science and new technology. With the growing materialism all around the world, acquisition of more and more wealth has become the final end of human activity. Consequently, moral values have either changed or [been] thrown to the winds, and frauds, misappropriation, misrepresentation, corruption, adulteration, evasion of tax etc. have become the TECHNIQUES OF TRADE, COMMERCE AND PROFESSION. …It is rather disappointing to note that though white collar crimes such as black market activities, evasive price violations, rent-ceiling violations, rationing-law violations, illegal financial manoeuvring etc. by businessmen are widespread in society, no effective programme for repressing them has so far been launched by the law enforcement agencies. Perhaps the reason for white collar crimes being carried on unabated is that these crimes are committed generally by INFLUENTIAL PERSONS who are SHREWD ENOUGH TO RESIST THE EFFORTS OF LAW ENFORCEMENT against them.

The economic offences which are often referred [to] as white collar crimes are master-minded and carried out in a planned manner by technocrats, highly qualified persons, well-to-do businessmen [and] corporate officials in the form of scams [and] frauds, facilitated by technological advancements. In these offences, not only [do] individuals get victimised with pecuniary loss but also such offences often damage the economy and the national defence. The offences such as smuggling of narcotic substances, counterfeiting of currency, financial scams [and] frauds are some of the white collar crimes which evoke serious concern and [have an] impact on national security and governance.”
Note the final sentence. The passage ends by linking white-collar crime to national security — smuggling, counterfeit currency, terror financing. That is exactly the bridge from head C(i) socio-economic offences to organised crime in the next section, and the reason the syllabus places them together. Modern India has institutionalised that link: the NIA Act, 2008 (as amended in 2019) gives the NIA jurisdiction over counterfeit currency and human trafficking; the PMLA Schedule includes NDPS, UAPA, Arms Act and corruption offences as predicate offences; and s.111 BNS expressly includes “economic offence” within the definition of organised crime.
Real-world example to close onNirav Modi / Punjab National Bank (2018). Roughly ₹13,500 crore was extracted through fraudulent Letters of Undertaking issued by two officials at a single Mumbai branch over seven years, routed through overseas branches of Indian banks and concealed by keeping the LoUs outside the bank’s core banking system. Every one of the 47th Report’s six characteristics is present: the motive was avarice; the background was entirely non-emotional; the victim was a public-sector bank and, through it, the taxpayer; the mode was fraud; the conduct was deliberate and sustained over years; and the interest injured was the economic system as a whole. The legal response — declaration as a fugitive economic offender, attachment and confiscation under the FEOA, 2018 and the PMLA, and extradition proceedings — is equally the modern socio-economic response: asset recovery rather than imprisonment. Use this case to show that the 1972 Report still describes Indian reality exactly.

24. Organised crime — definition and the classic statements

Syllabus head C(i)2017 Q2 concluded that differential association best explains organised crime in India

24.1 The setting

Standard Indian commentary “Criminality is a global phenomenon. With the advancement of time and development of knowledge and technology the complexities of life have multiplied, with the result that many anti-social elements think it profitable to embrace criminality as a PROFESSION to earn their livelihood. This has provided opportunities to criminals to organise themselves into criminal gangs. In the modern age of science and technology, new techniques of crime are used by the gangsters to perpetrate crimes.”

24.2 The definitions — give at least three

SourceDefinitionWhat it adds
General description“Organised crime is an act which is committed by two or more criminals as a joint venture in a systematically organised manner. It is an illegal act which the members of an unlawful association commit with mutual co-operation and adventure.”Plurality + system + joint venture
Dr. Walter Reckless, The Crime Problem, p. 319“An unlawful misadventure which is carried on by a BOSS, his LIEUTENANTS and OPERATORS who form a HIERARCHICAL STRUCTURE for a specific period.”Hierarchy and division of command — the feature that distinguishes a syndicate from a gang
Thorsten Sellin“Organised crime resembles those economic adventures or enterprises which are organised to carry on illegal activities.” It is “synonymous with economic enterprises organised for the purpose of conducting illegal activities and which, when they operate legitimate ventures, do so by illegal means.” The purpose is “to amass huge profits through illegal means. The largest share of the profits, however, goes to the manager and kingpin of the entire iniquitous enterprise, e.g. prostitution, smuggling, bootlegging, gambling, racketeering.”The enterprise model — organised crime is a business. This is the analytical key to the whole subject and the bridge to socio-economic offences
Donald Taft, Criminology (4th ed.) p. 183“The organisation of criminals introduced in the field of crime those factors of leadership, group-discipline, obedience, loyalty, division of labour, fellowmenship, sacrifice, co-operation and group planning which spell EFFICIENCY in the normal economic, political and social life.”Organised crime imports the virtues of legitimate enterprise into illegitimate activity — a Sutherland-style observation with a sting
Edwin Sutherland“There is at least one thing common among these criminal organisations. That is, they are bound together by a COMMON HOSTILITY TOWARDS LAW and therefore the members assist and protect each other from law-enforcing authorities. Since protection against arrest and detection is a necessary part of organised crimes, gangsters too often adopt the technique of ‘FIXING’ CRIMES, which is far more important than executing them. For this purpose they try to win over police officials by underhand tactics, or use threat, undue influence or coercion against the victim in order to refrain him from making a complaint to the police. That apart, organised criminals have also to seek co-operation and support from some parallel legitimate business organisations for disposing of their illegally earned money, property or commodity. Thus, organised crimes involve a number of accessory activities for the smooth conduct of their underworld profession.”The three defining features: solidarity against law; “fixing” more important than the offence; and the need for a legitimate front to launder the proceeds. This is the passage the DU 2017 model answer reproduces (§43).
UNTOC (Palermo Convention), 2000, Art. 2(a)“‘Organized criminal group’ shall mean a structured group of three or more persons, existing for a period of time and acting in concert with the aim of committing one or more serious crimes… in order to obtain, directly or indirectly, a financial or other material benefit.”The international legal definition. India ratified UNTOC and its three Protocols in May 2011
s.2(1)(e) MCOCA, 1999“‘Organised crime’ means any continuing unlawful activity by an individual, singly or jointly, either as a member of an organised crime syndicate or on behalf of such syndicate, by use of violence or threat of violence or intimidation or coercion, or other unlawful means, with the objective of gaining pecuniary benefits, or gaining undue economic or other advantage for himself or any other person or promoting insurgency.”The Indian statutory definition, reproduced almost verbatim in the Delhi, Karnataka, Gujarat, Rajasthan, Haryana and U.P. Acts
s.111(1) BNS, 2023“Any continuing unlawful activity including kidnapping, robbery, vehicle theft, extortion, land grabbing, contract killing, economic offence, cyber-crimes, trafficking of persons, drugs, weapons or illicit goods or services, human trafficking for prostitution or ransom, by any person or a group of persons acting in concert, singly or jointly, either as a member of an organised crime syndicate or on behalf of such syndicate, by use of violence, threat of violence, intimidation, coercion, or by any other unlawful means to obtain direct or indirect material benefit including a financial benefit, shall constitute organised crime.”For the first time organised crime is an offence under the general penal law of India, applicable throughout the country. Note that “economic offence” and “cyber-crimes” are expressly named — the syllabus heads of Topic 3 written into the Sanhita

24.3 How organised crime differs from other crime

Ordinary / casual crimeORGANISED crimeWhite-collar crimeTerrorism
StructureIndividual or ad hoc groupHierarchical, continuing, with division of labour and a codeAn individual within a legitimate organisationCell-based, ideological network
ObjectiveImmediate gain or emotionProfit — the enterprise modelProfit through occupationPolitical, religious or ideological; profit is instrumental
ContinuityEpisodicContinuing unlawful activity — a defined term in MCOCA and s.111 BNSOften continuing but within a lawful enterpriseCampaign-based
Relation to the StateAvoids itCorrupts and captures it — “fixing”, political graft (§25.4)Uses regulatory capture and lobbyingAttacks and seeks to overthrow it
Use of violenceSometimesInstrumental — to enforce monopoly and silence witnessesRareExpressive and communicative — violence is the message
Indian statuteBNS generallys.111 BNS; MCOCA 1999 and State ActsSpecial economic lawss.113 BNS; UAPA ss.15–23
Convergence. The categories overlap in practice — the “crime–terror nexus”. Indian examples: the 1993 Bombay blasts, prosecuted as terrorism but executed by a smuggling syndicate; narco-terrorism in Punjab and Jammu & Kashmir; the use of hawala for terror financing; counterfeit Indian currency notes (the NIA’s jurisdiction). The Vohra Committee (§26.3) was set up precisely because the boundary between organised crime, politics and insurgency had dissolved.

25. The four types of organised crime

The classic Indian classification divides “the different types of criminal organisations that may operate in the criminal world” into four heads. Learn the four and one Indian example of each.

25.1 Organised predatory crime

Standard Indian commentary “Crimes which do not involve any kind of service to the affected person or persons are called predatory crimes. It is therefore essentially a one-way transaction, inasmuch as the gangsters by committing such crime enjoy the entire benefit themselves without any apparent or actual service to the victim. There is no repentance among the gangsters although they are conscious of the opposition of law-abiding society. Juvenile delinquents and occasional offenders generally turn into professional gangsters in course of time. Commenting on this tendency, John Latuiesco observed that ‘in some delinquency areas the choice of a professional career in crime is as natural to the criminal as the choice of a legitimate career may be elsewhere’ [Taft and England, Criminology (4th ed.) p. 185]. The noted criminologist Sutherland opines that members of the professional criminal gangs require greater skill and planning than the occasional criminals. Their professionalisation involves not only the execution of crime but prior location of ‘spots’ and preparation for escape from punishment in case of detection.

Some of the predatory crimes which commonly occur are theft, dacoity, extortion, kidnapping, pick-pocketing etc. The peculiar characteristic of a predatory crime is that the victim of this crime is a TOTAL LOSER without any material gain or advantage or service from the offender. Thus, in a predatory crime the exploitation of the victim is so conspicuous that [the] entire society reacts to it.”

Terrorism as predatory crime. “Of late, the emergence of terrorism as an organised form of predatory crime has endangered peace and security. It is found in different forms such as political terrorism, religious terrorism, narco-terrorism. It basically involves violence and killing… The dissemination of new technology and weapons has facilitated the growth of terrorism. Though the roots of terrorism or extremism lie in ethnic or religious fundamentalism, it has proved a boon for professional criminals to carry on their criminal activities in a planned manner for dubious goals.” Legal response: the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 — under which, for instance, the Government declared the JKLF an unlawful association by notification S.O. 190(E) dated 18 February 1994. Illustrations given in the literature: the massacre of 61 Dalits at Laxmanpur Bathe, Jehanabad district, Bihar, on 2 December 1997 by the Ranvir Sena, “a well organised army of the wealthy landlords and upper castes”; and Naxalite/Maoist violence in Bihar, Jharkhand and Chhattisgarh.

25.2 Crime syndicate

Standard Indian commentary “The term ‘crime syndicate’ refers to a gang of criminals engaged in the business of providing some forbidden or illegal SERVICE to the customers who are desirous of having it and are willing to pay handsomely for that service. Crime syndicates operate because of the availability of [a] market for certain illegal prohibited services. Thus, gambling, bootlegging, commercialised prostitution, supply of narcotic drugs and other intoxicants are mostly carried on by the syndicates of criminals. Obviously, these crime syndicates exist because of the ILLEGITIMATE PUBLIC DEMANDS which cannot otherwise be legally met due to legal prohibitions. The possibility of enormous profits… is perhaps the main consideration… These monopolies are procured by persuasion, intimidation, violence and even murder. More often than not, these gangsters are extended protection and shelter by law-abiding persons…

Commenting on the working of crime syndicates, David Dressler observed that despite known arrest records and well documented criminal statistics, the leading gangsters remain for the most part of their career IMMUNE from prosecution and punishment, although underlinks of their gangs may at times be prosecuted and punished. The quasi-immunity of top-level mobsters can be ascribed to what is popularly known as the ‘FIX’. The ‘fix’ is not always [a] direct payment of money to law enforcement officials but may also come about through the ACQUISITION OF POLITICAL POWER by contributions to political organisations, or by creating ECONOMIC TIES with apparently respectable businessmen and lawyers, and by BUYING PUBLIC GOODWILL through charities, contributions and press relations.”
The economic insight — state it expressly. A syndicate is created by prohibition. Where the State forbids a service for which there is a large market, it does not abolish the market; it transfers it to criminals and confers on them a monopoly profit. This is why the standard Indian commentary goes on to suggest that “the service-crimes which are rampant in the form of syndicates and rackets can be repressed by legalising these services under heavy taxation. Gambling rackets and illicit liquor rackets can effectively be controlled in this manner.” It also notes the contrary view on prostitution — Smt. Suman Krishan Kant of the Mahila Dakshita Samiti opposed legalisation as “the worse form of human commodification… legitimising human depravity and degradation”. Present both sides. Indian evidence for the argument: hooch tragedies in prohibition States (Gujarat 2009 and 2022, Bihar 2016 and 2022, Tamil Nadu 2024); State lotteries and legalised betting revenue; the Law Commission’s 276th Report (2018) recommending regulated legalised gambling and sports betting; and the debate over online real-money gaming.

25.3 Criminal racket

Standard Indian commentary “Racketeering in [the] criminal world is the practice of systematic extortion under some kind of threat, usually of personal injury or property [Vold, Theoretical Criminology (1958) p. 228]. Donald Taft defines racketeering as ‘an organised crime in which the criminal elements perform a SERVICE to such members of society who are normally engaged in some LEGITIMATE business activity’. Racketeering differs from an organised predatory crime inasmuch as some kind of service is essentially involved in it and therefore it is not completely exploitative. It also differs from a criminal syndicate, as the service involved in a racket is rendered to those who are normally engaged in LEGITIMATE activities, while in the case of a syndicate the service is altogether illegal and prohibited. …opportunities for racketeering increase when business is not within the legal limits or is marginally within limits and legitimate recourse to police help for its protection is not possible. Persons who are exploited by the racketeers are sometimes convinced of the value of services taken by them even at the cost of their own exploitation.”
Business–labour racket
“The employers always try to make huge profits whereas the labour wants high wages… The employers resort to illegitimate means by utilising unorganised workers and racketeers to jeopardise the bargaining capacity of the labour… there are cases when organisers of even most reputed industrial establishments muster many non-existent and fictitious names on their payrolls as labourers and thus draw huge sums in their name for months through an organised racket. Such rackets frequently operate in the Engineering Department of the Railways and the Public Works Department of the States which employ a large number of workers as work-charge labour.” In the second form, “salary… is drawn in the name of certain fictitious persons who do not actually come for work but their attendance is marked by the interested parties”; the racketeers “in return offer protection to [the employers] against the workers’ strike… They are vigilant to make sure that they always remain indispensable to the employer and for this purpose they sometimes manipulate their election to the unions.”
Dockyard racket
“Cargoes arriving at the dockyard have to be unloaded in the shortest possible time because delay… would mean loss of profit… Therefore, in order to eliminate any possibility of delay… the employers often take the top leaders of the labour union into confidence and employ a large number of professional criminals and habitual offenders to organise themselves into [a] labour racket and retaliate threats from the dockyard workers… and in return extract huge sums of money from the employers. One such racket operated in America in the name of the New York Water Front Racket. Similar rackets are operative in various dockyards in India.”
Gambling racket
“Tendency of the people to take [a] chance and try their luck coupled with the hope of gaining something out of nothing is perhaps the main consideration which underlies the gambling rackets. Horse races, animal-combats and ball games are some of the common forms… Unfortunately, anti-gambling laws have totally failed to suppress this menace because of the inherent tendency of men to speculate and try their fortune. Moreover, in recent years the State lotteries run by State Governments sufficiently suggest that even the government accepts gambling as a potential source for raising revenue… Certain States have gone a step further and withdrawn all restrictions on speculation (satta)… these vices which are deep rooted in the society cannot be stopped merely by legislative measures unless public opinion mobilises in favour of uprooting them.” On cyber gambling: “In [the] United States, cyber casinos and internet gambling permitting non-sporting bets have been operating as an off-shore internet wagering racket… Illegal bettors are making huge profits through this cyber space gambling racket operating on [the] internet.” Indian law: the Public Gambling Act, 1867; State gaming Acts; the skill v. chance distinction — State of Andhra Pradesh v. K. Satyanarayana AIR 1968 SC 825 (rummy) and Dr. K.R. Lakshmanan v. State of T.N. (1996) 2 SCC 226 (horse racing is a game of skill); the IT Rules amendment of 2023 on online real-money games; and the Law Commission’s 276th Report.
Other rackets — the Indian illustrations
“A racket of fake recruiting personnel was unearthed by the Superintendent of Police, Meerut on 5 March 1975… [the ring leader] allegedly recruited young men to the army with bogus certificates of education and age and fictitious addresses… Several employees of the Meerut Recruiting Office were also involved.” “The Central Bureau of Investigation busted a transport department racket in Delhi on 22 August 1995 wherein six Motor Vehicle Inspectors along with four touts were caught red-handed dividing the day’s bribe amount of ₹1.27 lakh at the Burari office in North Delhi. In a subsequent search of the Inspectors’ houses, the Anti-Corruption Branch seized assets worth ₹1.5 crore. The bribe was collected for issuing licences and certificates regarding road-worthiness of vehicles.” Also: rackets in the medical profession — “illegal sale of alcohol and narcotics, abortion, illegal service to the underworld criminals, fraudulent reports and testimony in accident cases”; and rackets “selling fake degrees and mark-sheets… operating in several parts of India… in liaison with some of the University employees” — two such rackets were unearthed in Madhya Pradesh in 1990, at Rajpur and Ujjain.
Flesh-trade racket
“Criminal rackets involved in flesh trade and sale of women and girls for the purpose of prostitution are common news-items… Despite stringent legal measures such as the Immoral Traffic (Prevention) Act, 1956 and the provisions of the Indian Penal Code [ss.361, 362, 372, 373, 498 IPC], the repression of prostitution has not been possible. The problem being more of a social and economic nature, needs to be tackled through better social and economic planning rather than social legislation alone. The recent developments in information technology and Internet services have opened new opportunities for these rackets.” The Supreme Court, in Gaurav Jain v. Union of India AIR 1997 SC 3021, “issued direction[s] to the Social Welfare Department of the Government of India and the States to initiate adequate measures for prevention of induction of women in various forms of prostitution and [to] rescue them from the vile flesh-trade and to provide them dignity of person, means of livelihood and socio-economic empowerment.”
The modern Indian rackets
Add these to show currency: sand and stone mining mafias (Bihar, U.P., Rajasthan, Tamil Nadu — and the 2024 killings of officials); liquor mafias in prohibition States; land-grabbing mafias (expressly named in s.111 BNS); coal and iron-ore mafias (Goa Foundation, the Shah Commission); examination and recruitment rackets (Vyapam; the Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024); fake call-centre and loan-app rackets; and organ-trade rackets (the Gurgaon kidney racket, 2008).

25.4 Political graft

Standard Indian commentary “There is a general belief that persons of high status carrying on some legitimate business and professional criminals are inter-connected through POLITICAL GRAFTS. In order to assume political power and [ensure the] party’s victory at [the] polls, the politicians generally seek the support of notorious offenders and utilise them for illegal practices to accomplish their political ends. This utilisation of notorious criminals by the politicians for political gains is commonly known as ‘political graft’. These grafts resort to all kinds of legitimate or illegitimate methods to bring success to their employer at [the] polls. At times, these hired professional offenders do not even hesitate to resort to violence and threats to make voters cast their votes in favour of the candidate for whom they are working. Instances are not wanting when some professional voters have been found to vote at more than one place… Thus, ‘vote buying’ is a common example of political graft [Vold, Theoretical Criminology (1958) p. 231].”

The reverse flow — criminalisation of politics. Political graft has a second stage: the criminal, having served the politician, becomes the politician. “Some political leaders are found even to lead these armed gangs and criminal forces and manipulate their election to [the] State Legislature or the Parliament so that they can exert their political influence in accomplishing their anti-social designs.” The Indian legal response:

  • s.8, Representation of the People Act, 1951 — disqualification on conviction; Lily Thomas v. Union of India (2013) 7 SCC 653 struck down s.8(4), so a convicted legislator is disqualified immediately.
  • Union of India v. Association for Democratic Reforms (2002) 5 SCC 294 and PUCL v. Union of India (2003) 4 SCC 399 — the voter’s right to know the criminal antecedents, assets and qualifications of a candidate is part of Art. 19(1)(a); Form 26 affidavit.
  • Public Interest Foundation v. Union of India (2019) 3 SCC 224 — mandatory publication of a candidate’s criminal cases in newspapers and on TV; and Rambabu Singh Thakur v. Sunil Arora (2020) 3 SCC 733 — parties must publish reasons for selecting candidates with criminal cases.
  • Manoj Narula v. Union of India (2014) 9 SCC 1 — on the appointment of ministers with criminal antecedents; Ashwini Kumar Upadhyay v. Union of India — special courts for MPs/MLAs and the monitoring of pending cases.
  • Anukul Chandra Pradhan v. Union of India (1997) 6 SCC 1 — s.62(5) RPA (prisoners cannot vote) upheld.
  • ADR data: in the 18th Lok Sabha (2024), roughly 46% of the elected members declared criminal cases and about 31% declared serious criminal cases — the empirical measure of political graft in India today. Quote this figure.

26. Characteristics of organised criminals; the Vohra Committee

26.1 The characteristics

Standard Indian commentary “The sense of unity and solidarity among these criminals is perhaps the root cause of their success in their criminal ventures. It is primarily for this reason that detection of organised crimes has always been a difficult problem for the police… Organised criminals have their own leaders and the members of the gang work in perfect harmony and understanding. They have their own CODES OF ETHICS. There are instances when participants of such organisations have preferred DEATH rather than divulging the secret of their associates. That apart, a perfect DIVISION OF LABOUR is to be found in the gangs… Thus, the criminals associated with [a] liquor syndicate have a variety of activities to perform: some are entrusted the job of procuring liquor while others are to manipulate its transmission to the customers. A few… act as WATCH-DOGS and keep their fellow-criminals alert from the dangers of police… one or two members from the group assume the role of LEADERS to supervise the smooth working of the syndicate… The task of DISTRIBUTION OF BOOTY… is generally performed by the ring leader of the gang.

Besides perfect unity and division of labour, organised criminal gangs have to act in close liaison with other organised criminal groups. They cannot afford to be hostile or indifferent to other gangs… For example, those indulging in gambling dens have to remain in touch with the illicit liquor suppliers and perhaps also with the gang dealing with prostitution… The obvious reason for this mutual trust and co-operation… is their consciousness that the slightest confrontation with another gang might lead to their detection by the police, which would mean destruction, ruin and disbanding of the gang.

With the developing economy, industrial growth and modernisation, the scope of activities… has considerably widened. Today, criminality in an organised form is known to exist on [a] national as well as international plane. …There are inter-State gangs carrying on these organised crimes as a regular business… [This] amply reflect[s] upon their organisational skill, spirit of mutual trust and cooperation and loyalty to their fellowmen. At the same time, it casts aspersions on the efficacy of the police and detective agencies… It further makes it clear that even the professional criminals do have regard for the virtues of devotion, honesty, loyalty and fellowmenship, and if these noble qualities in them are properly channelised, there is no reason why they cannot be turned into law abiding members of the society.”
The last sentence is the reformative note — organised criminals display exactly the qualities society praises, only directed to the wrong ends. That is a direct restatement of Sutherland’s differential association (crime is learnt in intimate personal groups and expresses the same needs and values as lawful behaviour) and of Taft’s point that organised crime imports the efficiency-producing factors of legitimate enterprise. This is why the DU 2017 model answer concludes that differential association is the best explanation of organised crime in India.

26.2 Collective violence as organised crime
