“Collective violence may also be considered as a form of organised crime. Violent conflicts between nations and groups quite often lead to disastrous consequences in terms of loss of men, material and economic wealth. Collective violence in its multiple forms receives a high degree of public attention and media coverage. Violent conflicts between state and groups, terrorism, naxalite attacks, gang warfare and mass hooliganism are common occurrences [in] all parts of the world.” Definition: “violence by people who identify themselves as members of a group — whether such group is transitory or has a more permanent identity — against another group or set of individuals, for achieving [a] political, economic or social objective.” Indian legal responses: BNS ss.189–191 (unlawful assembly and rioting), s.103(2) (mob lynching), the Prevention of Damage to Public Property Act, 1984, and the directions in Tehseen Poonawalla and In Re: Destruction of Public and Private Properties (2009) 5 SCC 212.

26.3 The N.N. Vohra Committee Report (1993–95) — the central Indian document

Standard Indian commentary “The issue of links between crime syndicates and politicians as also the bureaucrats has been sufficiently highlighted in what purports to be the N.N. Vohra Committee Report, which was tabled before the House of Parliament on 3 August 1995. It has been alleged in the report that criminal gangs enjoy [the] patronage of ‘local level politicians’ and the criminal deeds of political high-ups are conveniently hushed up or ignored. This has resulted in [the] CRIMINALISATION OF INDIAN POLITICS and [in] making criminal actions respectable. The name of Iqbal ‘Mirchi’, one of the prime accused in the Bombay Blast case of January 1993, also finds place in the Vohra Report as an example of the growth of a small functionary into a major syndicate.

The Report refers to the proposals put forth by the Central Bureau of Investigation for enhancement in the power of preventive detention, to award punishment, tap telephones, carry out surveillance, establish [a] monitoring mechanism at State and Central levels, and simplification of trial procedures including review and amendments of existing laws, so that the guilty be punished irrespective of their class or cadre. Acting on this report, the Government of India appointed a high level committee under the chairmanship of the Home Secretary Shri Padmanabhaiah… Despite these measures, the nexus between politicians, government officials and criminals continues unabated…

[The] Director of the Central Bureau of Investigation has admitted before the Vohra Committee that criminal mafia gangs are receiving patronage of almost all the political parties, particularly in the States of Bihar, Uttar Pradesh and Haryana… These criminal rackets have become so powerful that they have succeeded in corrupting every wing of the governmental mechanism. …the ultimate goal of organised crimes being amassing huge profits through illegal means, it results in ruthless exploitation of the poor and at the same time has an adverse effect on the national economy.”
The Committee
Constituted on 9 July 1993 by the Government of India, a five-member committee chaired by the then Home Secretary Shri N.N. Vohra, to probe “the nexus between politicians, Government high officials and industrialists with the mafia gangs”. It reported in 1995 and was tabled in Parliament on 3 August 1995. It was set up in the aftermath of the March 1993 Bombay serial blasts.
The core finding
That the mafia network has virtually established a parallel government, with the nexus between criminal gangs, police, bureaucracy and politicians having come out clearly in various parts of the country; that these gangs enjoy the patronage of local-level politicians cutting across party lines; and that no single agency had the mandate or capacity to deal with the problem.
The recommendation
The establishment of a nodal agency to collate and act on intelligence about the nexus. The standard commentary echoes it: “it may further be suggested that a high level secret cell be established in the Home Ministry of the Government of India to break the links between politicians, bureaucrats and the criminal gangs and rackets, as they pose a direct threat to the internal as well as external security of the country.” This eventually produced the Multi Agency Centre (MAC) in the Intelligence Bureau (strengthened after 26/11), the National Investigation Agency (2008) and the National Intelligence Grid.
The criticism
The Report has never been published in full — only a summary was tabled. In Dinesh Trivedi, M.P. v. Union of India (1997) 4 SCC 306 the Supreme Court considered a petition seeking disclosure of the annexures and the background papers; it declined full disclosure on grounds of national security and the need to protect sources, but appointed a high-level committee to examine the material. The non-publication of the Vohra Report is itself the best single illustration of privileged class deviance (§38–§40): a report on elite criminality was withheld in the interest of the elite.
The follow-up committees
Padmanabhaiah Committee on Police Reforms (2000); Malimath Committee on Reforms of the Criminal Justice System (2003), which recommended a federal/national law on organised crime and a witness protection programme; the Second Administrative Reforms Commission, especially its 4th Report on Ethics in Governance (2007) and 5th Report on Public Order (2007); and the Law Commission’s 239th Report (2012) on expeditious trial of cases against public representatives.
The political-graft cases to cite
Vineet Narain v. Union of India (1998) 1 SCC 226 (the Jain Hawala case) — the CVC given statutory supervision over the CBI, the “single directive” struck down, and the continuing mandamus device created; Prakash Singh v. Union of India (2006) 8 SCC 1 — seven directions to insulate the police from political control; Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI (2014) 8 SCC 682 — s.6A DSPE Act (prior approval for investigating senior officials) struck down as violative of Art. 14.

27. The legal framework against organised crime in India

27.1 Why the ordinary law failed

  1. The IPC punished the act, not the enterprise. Each robbery, each extortion, each murder was tried separately; the syndicate itself was not an offence. Criminal conspiracy (s.120-B IPC → s.61 BNS) and the law of common object (s.149 IPC → s.190 BNS) were the only tools, and both require proof tied to a specific offence.
  2. Witnesses do not testify. Intimidation and “fixing” destroy the prosecution; hostile witnesses are the commonest cause of acquittal in gang cases. Until 2018 India had no witness protection scheme at all.
  3. Bail and proceeds. The kingpin obtained bail and continued to operate; the proceeds were never traced.
  4. Federalism. Syndicates operate across State boundaries; “police” and “public order” are State subjects (Entries 1 and 2, List II), and there was no central law.

27.2 s.111 and s.112 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 — the new general law

ProvisionContentPunishment
s.111(1) — definition“Continuing unlawful activity” including kidnapping, robbery, vehicle theft, extortion, land grabbing, contract killing, economic offence, cyber-crimes, trafficking of persons, drugs, weapons or illicit goods or services, human trafficking for prostitution or ransom, by a person or group acting in concert, as a member of or on behalf of an organised crime syndicate, by violence, threat, intimidation, coercion “or by any other unlawful means”, to obtain direct or indirect material benefit including a financial benefit—
Explanations to s.111“Organised crime syndicate” = a group of two or more persons who, singly or jointly, as a syndicate or gang, indulge in any continuing unlawful activity. “Continuing unlawful activity” = an activity prohibited by law which is a cognizable offence punishable with three years or more, undertaken by a person singly or jointly as a member of a syndicate, in respect of which more than one charge-sheet has been filed before a competent court within the preceding period of ten years and the court has taken cognizance. “Economic offence” = criminal breach of trust, forgery, counterfeiting of currency/valuable securities, financial scams, running of Ponzi schemes, mass-marketing fraud or multi-level marketing schemes to defraud the public, or any other offence involving a large-scale organised betting—
s.111(2)(a) Where the offence has resulted in the death of any person — death or imprisonment for life and fine not less than ₹10 lakh.
(b) In any other case — 5 years to life and fine not less than ₹5 lakh		as stated
s.111(3)–(7)Abetting, attempting, conspiring, or knowingly facilitating (5 years–life + ₹5 lakh); harbouring a member (3 years–life + ₹5 lakh); being a member of an organised crime syndicate (5 years–life + ₹5 lakh); intentionally possessing property derived from organised crime or property of a syndicate (3 years–life + ₹2 lakh)as stated
s.112 — PETTY ORGANISED CRIME (entirely new)Theft, snatching, cheating, unauthorised selling of tickets, unauthorised betting or gambling, selling of public examination question papers, “or any other similar criminal act”, committed by any member of a group or gang, either singly or jointly, so as to cause general feelings of insecurity among citizens. Sub-s.(1) Explanation: “theft” includes pickpocketing, theft through card skimming, shoplifting, and theft of automated teller machines1 to 7 years and fine
s.113 — terrorist actSubstantially the UAPA s.15 definition brought into the general penal code, with the officer not below the rank of Superintendent of Police deciding whether to register the case under s.113 BNS or the UAPADeath or life where death is caused; otherwise 5 years to life
Critique of s.111 — the points to make. (i) The definition of “continuing unlawful activity” requires more than one charge-sheet in ten years, which imports a criminal-record requirement reminiscent of the habitual-offender and Criminal Tribes logic (Topic 2). (ii) “Or by any other unlawful means” and “any other similar criminal act” (s.112) are vague, raising Art. 14 and Art. 21 “void for vagueness” concerns (Shreya Singhal v. Union of India (2015) 5 SCC 1). (iii) Overlap with MCOCA and the State Acts, with UAPA and with the PMLA creates the risk of multiple prosecutions for the same conduct. (iv) Unlike MCOCA, s.111 BNS carries no special procedural safeguards of its own — no prior sanction of a senior officer, no special court, no confession regime — so the safeguards that justified MCOCA’s severity are absent while its severity is generalised. These are exactly the criticisms a good answer makes.

27.3 MCOCA, 1999 — the model State statute

SectionProvision
s.2(1)(d)“Continuing unlawful activity” — a cognizable offence punishable with three years or more, undertaken singly or jointly as a member of or on behalf of a syndicate, in respect of which more than one charge-sheet has been filed in the preceding ten years and cognizance has been taken
s.2(1)(e)“Organised crime” — see §24.2
s.2(1)(f)“Organised crime syndicate” — a group of two or more persons indulging in continuing unlawful activity
s.3Punishment: death or life and fine of not less than ₹1 lakh where death is caused; otherwise 5 years to life and fine of not less than ₹5 lakh; separate punishment for conspiracy, abetment, harbouring, membership and possession of unaccounted property
s.4Punishment for possession of unaccountable wealth on behalf of a member of a syndicate — 3 to 10 years and fine, plus forfeiture
ss.5–10Special Courts; power to transfer; procedure and powers
s.14Interception of wire, electronic or oral communication — with the authorisation of a competent authority; s.16 makes the intercepted material admissible
s.18Confession made to a police officer not below the rank of Superintendent of Police is admissible — a radical departure from ss.25–26 of the Evidence Act. Upheld subject to strict safeguards; contrast Tofan Singh v. State of T.N. (2021) 4 SCC 1 on s.67 NDPS
s.21(4)Twin conditions for bail — the court must be satisfied that there are reasonable grounds for believing the accused is not guilty and that he is not likely to commit any offence on bail; s.21(5) bars bail to a person out on bail in another such offence
s.23(1)(a)No information about an offence under the Act shall be recorded without the prior approval of an officer not below the rank of Deputy Inspector General; s.23(2) — no cognizance without the previous sanction of an officer not below the rank of Additional Director General of Police. These safeguards are strictly construed — a case registered without prior approval is vitiated
Leading casesState of Maharashtra v. Bharat Shanti Lal Shah (2008) 13 SCC 5 — constitutionality of MCOCA and of s.14 (interception) upheld; the State legislature is competent under Entries 1 and 2 of List II and Entry 12 of List III. Ranjitsing Brahmajeetsing Sharma v. State of Maharashtra (2005) 5 SCC 294 — the twin conditions in s.21(4) read down: the court must be satisfied only on a prima facie view, not on the material as at trial. Prasad Shrikant Purohit v. State of Maharashtra (2015) 7 SCC 440 — “continuing unlawful activity” and the requirement of prior charge-sheets. Zameer Ahmed Latifur Rehman Sheikh v. State of Maharashtra (2010) 5 SCC 246 — repugnancy with UAPA considered; MCOCA held valid.
State versionsDelhi (MCOCA extended in 2002), Karnataka (KCOCA, 2000), Andhra Pradesh/Telangana (APCOCA, 2001), Gujarat (GCTOC, 2015 — assented in 2019), Rajasthan (2023), Haryana (2019), Uttar Pradesh (UPCOCA, 2018)

27.4 The wider statutory arsenal

Terror and insurgency
UAPA, 1967 — s.15 terrorist act, s.16 punishment, s.17 raising funds, s.18 conspiracy, s.20 membership of a terrorist gang or organisation, s.23 enhanced penalties, s.43-D(5) bail bar, s.43-D(2) 180-day detention; Chapter VI (terrorist organisations) and the 2019 Amendment allowing an individual to be designated a terrorist. Union of India v. K.A. Najeeb (2021) 3 SCC 713 — the bail bar does not oust the constitutional court’s power to grant bail where trial is unlikely to conclude in reasonable time (Art. 21). NIA v. Zahoor Ahmad Shah Watali (2019) 5 SCC 1 — the standard for “prima facie true”.
Proceeds and preventive detention
PMLA, 2002 — attachment, confiscation and the Schedule of predicate offences (§37); COFEPOSA, 1974; PITNDPS Act, 1988; National Security Act, 1980; SAFEMA, 1976; the Prevention of Black-marketing Act, 1980; the Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018. Preventive detention is constitutionally permitted by Art. 22(3)–(7), but is strictly scrutinised — Rekha v. State of T.N. (2011) 5 SCC 244 (“preventive detention is a draconian measure… an exception to Art. 21”).
Investigation and forum
NIA Act, 2008 (amended 2019) — Scheduled Offences now include human trafficking, counterfeit currency, manufacture or sale of prohibited arms, and cyber-terrorism, and the NIA may investigate offences committed outside India; DSPE Act, 1946 (CBI); State Economic Offences Wings and Anti-Terrorism Squads; s.24 BNSS (Special Executive Magistrates); the Criminal Procedure (Identification) Act, 2022.
Witness protection — the missing piece, now supplied
Mahender Chawla v. Union of India (2019) 14 SCC 615 — the Supreme Court approved the Witness Protection Scheme, 2018 and directed all States and Union Territories to implement it as law under Art. 141/142 until Parliament legislates. Categories A, B and C of threat perception; protection measures; and the Witness Protection Fund. Also s.398 BNSS — every State Government shall prepare and notify a Witness Protection Scheme: for the first time a statutory obligation. This is the single most important reform for organised-crime prosecution, because the whole apparatus of the “fix” operates through witnesses.
International cooperation
UNTOC (Palermo), 2000 and its three Protocols — trafficking in persons, smuggling of migrants, and illicit manufacturing of firearms — ratified by India in May 2011; the UN Convention against Corruption, 2003 (ratified 2011); mutual legal assistance under ss.105A–105L CrPC → ss.112–122 BNSS; the Extradition Act, 1962; Interpol red notices routed through the CBI as the National Central Bureau; FATF and the Egmont Group.
What is still missing
(i) India has no comprehensive central organised-crime statute of the RICO type; s.111 BNS is a start but has no dedicated procedure. (ii) No plea-bargaining or approver regime designed for syndicates (s.343 BNSS pardon to an accomplice is narrow). (iii) Weak asset-tracing capacity outside the ED. (iv) Police reform remains unimplemented — the Prakash Singh directions of 2006 are still only partially complied with, which leaves the “fix” intact. (v) No national witness protection authority with a dedicated budget.
The examiner’s favourite question here: “Which criminological theory best explains organised crime in India?” The DU model answer (2017 Q2) says Sutherland’s differential association, and gives Sutherland’s own reasoning on common hostility to law and the technique of “fixing”. Support it with: (a) recruitment and training within an intimate group; (b) the transmission of techniques, motives and rationalisations; (c) the modalities of frequency, duration, priority and intensity; and then acknowledge the rivals — Merton’s innovation (blocked legitimate means), Cloward and Ohlin’s illegitimate opportunity structure (you cannot join a syndicate unless one exists in your neighbourhood), Sellin’s enterprise model and rational choice (the syndicate is a firm maximising profit), and conflict theory (the syndicate survives because it has captured the State). Conclude that no single theory suffices but that differential association best explains recruitment and persistence, while the enterprise model best explains structure.

28. CYBER CRIME I — definition, causes and classification

Syllabus head C(i)NCRB 2022: 65,893 cases, +24.4%

28.1 The definitional problem

Standard Indian commentary “Some authorities feel that the term ‘cyber crime’ is a misnomer, as this term is nowhere defined in any statute or Act enacted by the Parliament. In a sense, it is not radically different from the concept of conventional crime, insofar as both include conduct — whether act or omission — which causes breach of law and therefore is punishable by the state.

A cyber crime may be defined as ANY CRIMINAL ACTIVITY THAT USES A COMPUTER EITHER AS AN INSTRUMENTALITY, TARGET OR MEANS OF PERPETRATING FURTHER CRIME. In other words, cyber crime is an unlawful act wherein the computer is either a tool or a target or both.

The distinction between cyber crime and conventional crime lies in the involvement of the MEDIUM… there should be involvement, at any stage, of the virtual cyber space medium in [the] case of a cyber crime.”

Add the standard descriptive definition: “criminal activity where a computer or network is the source, tool, target, or place of a crime”. And note the legal position: the Information Technology Act, 2000 does not use the expression “cyber crime”; it creates specific offences in Chapter XI (ss.65–74) and specific contraventions in Chapter IX (ss.43–45).

28.2 Why cyber crime has grown — the five reasons (memorise)

Standard Indian commentary — the five reasons
  1. “The computer has the unique characteristic of storing data in a relatively very small space. This affords [an opportunity] to derive and remove information either through physical or virtual medium more easily.”
  2. “Computers are easy to access and therefore unauthorised access by the use of complex cyber space technology is easily possible, by-passing [the] security system.”
  3. “The computers work on operating systems which are complex and are composed of millions of codes. The cyber offenders take advantage of the fallibility of [the] human mind and penetrate into the computer system.”
  4. “One of the significant features of [a] computer system is that evidence is destroyed in no time. The criminals find it easy to destroy the evidence soon after the crime is committed, which makes it difficult for the investigating agencies to collect relevant material evidence.”
  5. “Slightest negligence on the part of [the] computer user in ensuring security… may lead to catastrophic consequences, as the cyber criminal may gain illegal access and unauthorised control over the computer system.”

Supplement with the six modern reasons that follow from the nature of the medium: (i) anonymity and pseudonymity — VPNs, the dark web, crypto-wallets; (ii) distance and transnationality — the offender, the server and the victim may be in three different jurisdictions, so territorial jurisdiction and evidence-gathering collapse; (iii) scale — one act can victimise millions at near-zero marginal cost; (iv) low skill threshold — “crime-as-a-service”, ransomware kits and phishing templates are sold online; (v) under-reporting — embarrassment and the belief that recovery is impossible; (vi) capacity deficit — few trained investigators, a shortage of accredited forensic laboratories and a chronic backlog in obtaining data from foreign platforms under Mutual Legal Assistance Treaties.

28.3 The classifications

ClassificationCategoriesContent
By the role of the computer (the primary classification)1. Computer as TARGET“(i) sabotage of computer systems or computer networks; (ii) sabotage of operating systems and programmes; (iii) theft of data/information; (iv) theft of intellectual property, such as computer software; (v) theft of marketing information; and (vi) blackmailing based on information gained from computerised files such as personal history, sexual preferences, financial data, medical information”
2. Computer as INSTRUMENT“The terrorists and criminals are using Internet methods such as e-mail to flesh out encrypted messages around the world… computer programs are manipulated to facilitate the offence. For example, fraudulent use of Automated Teller Machine (ATM) cards and accounts, frauds related to e-banking or e-commerce, electronic data-interchange… Cyber pornography, software piracy, on-line gambling, copyright infringement, trademark violations are some other illustrations”
Sieber’s traditional classification (The International Handbook on Computer Crime, p. 38)(1) Economic cyber crime; (2) cyber crime against privacy“In the first category, the perpetration of some impairment of resources is relevant, while in the second, the cyber crime affects the privacy of persons… The economic type… include[s] frauds committed by manipulation of computer systems, illegal copy of software and computer spying, computer sabotage or illegal use of computer systems belonging to others. The only aim of the offender… is to surpass the access barrier and make illegal use of computer systems without authorization. Cyber crime involving violation of [the] right to privacy adversely affects the right to privacy of a person without affecting any of the proprietary rights of the victim”
The general classification(1) Against persons; (2) against property; (3) against the State or societyAgainst persons: “harassment via e-mail, stalking, defamation, unauthorised access to computer systems, indecent exposure, e-mail spoofing, fraud, cheating and pornography”. Against property: “computer vandalism, transmission of virus, denial of service attack, unauthorised access over [a] computer system, intellectual property rights violations, Internet time-theft, sale of illegal articles”. Against the State or society: “possession of unauthorised information, cyber terrorism, distribution of pirated software, polluting youth through indecent exposure, trafficking, financial scams, forgery, online gambling”
Council of Europe, Budapest Convention on Cybercrime, 2001Four titles(i) Offences against the confidentiality, integrity and availability of computer data and systems; (ii) computer-related offences (forgery, fraud); (iii) content-related offences (child pornography); (iv) offences related to infringements of copyright. India is not a signatory, on the ground that it did not participate in the drafting and that Art. 32(b) (trans-border access to data) affects sovereignty — an examinable policy point
NCRB 2022 — the data to quote65,893 cyber-crime cases, an increase of 24.4% over 2021 (52,974) — the fastest-growing crime head in India. Motive: fraud in 64.8% of cases (42,710), extortion 5.5%, sexual exploitation 5.2%. Crime rate 4.8 per lakh. Note the implication: Indian cyber crime is overwhelmingly economic, not ideological — Sieber’s first category. Add that the National Cybercrime Reporting Portal and helpline 1930 record financial-fraud complaints running into lakhs of crores of attempted loss annually, figures far larger than the FIR-based NCRB count, which is a clean demonstration of the dark figure (§10) in a modern setting.

29. CYBER CRIME II — the catalogue of offences

Learn these as short definitions with the Indian penal provision attached. The quoted passages are from the standard Indian commentary.

29.1 Malicious code

Virus
“A program or code that replicates and infects another programme, sector or document by inserting itself or attaching itself to that medium. The effect of a virus is that it destroys or alters the data files and other programs. Except in rare cases, the virus does not damage the computer hardware.” Two classes: file infectors (which attach to ordinary program files, and are either direct-action, selecting programs to infect each time the host is executed, or resident, hiding in memory — Vienna is direct-action, Jerusalem resident) and boot-record infectors (Brain, Sonde, Azusa, Michelangelo), which are always resident; some are “boot and file” viruses. Others named: stealth, polymorphic, fast and slow infectors, sparse infector, companion, armoured and macro viruses. Historic example: the “Love Bug” virus of May 2000, which caused severe damage to working internet sites.
Virus hoax
“Generally appears [as] an e-mail message that describes a particular virus that does not in fact exist. Such messages are intended to create panic… The message then spreads like a chain letter… For example, ‘Good Times’ virus hoax was written in 1994 and since then has circled the globe many times. It is always advisable to ignore or delete such hoax virus [messages] rather than acting upon [them].” The modern equivalents are WhatsApp forwards and misinformation — now touched by s.197(1)(d) BNS (false and misleading information jeopardising sovereignty) and Rule 3(1)(b) of the IT (Intermediary Guidelines) Rules, 2021.
Worms, trojans, ransomware, spyware
A worm self-propagates without a host file; a trojan is “an unauthorised programme which gains control over another’s system by representing itself as an authorised programme”; ransomware encrypts data and demands payment (WannaCry, 2017, which affected systems in India including some hospital and State networks; the AIIMS Delhi ransomware attack of November 2022, which crippled hospital servers for weeks); spyware and keyloggers capture keystrokes and credentials. Indian provisions: s.43(c) and (e) (introducing a computer contaminant or virus; damaging data) with s.66 IT Act; s.70 (protected systems); CERT-In directions of 28 April 2022 requiring reporting of cyber incidents within six hours.

29.2 Offences against the person

OffenceDescription (standard commentary)Indian law
Cyber stalking“Persistent messages are sent to unwilling recipients, thus causing them annoyance, worry and mental torture. Sending of unsolicited e-mails or spamming is an infringement of [the] right of privacy… Cyber stalking usually occurs with women who are stalked by men, adolescents or adult pedophiles. A cyber stalker does not have to leave his home to harass his targets and has no fear of physical avenge since he cannot be physically touched in cyber space. A cyber stalker generally collects all the personal information about the victim — name, age, family background, telephone or mobile numbers, workplace — from internet resources such as the various profiles the victim may have filled in… The menace of cyber stalking has spread like wild-fire in India and many innocent women, girls and children are being targeted.”s.78 BNS (s.354-D IPC) — stalking, expressly including monitoring “the use by a woman of the internet, email or any other form of electronic communication”: up to 3 years for a first offence, 5 years for a subsequent one; s.79 BNS (s.509 IPC); s.67 IT Act
Cyber defamation and harassmentPublication of defamatory imputations online; trolling; doxxings.356 BNS (499–500 IPC) — Subramanian Swamy v. Union of India (2016) 7 SCC 221; note that s.66-A IT Act was struck down as unconstitutional in Shreya Singhal v. Union of India (2015) 5 SCC 1 for vagueness and overbreadth under Art. 19(2) — the single most important Indian cyber-law case. Police continue to register cases under the dead section, which the Supreme Court condemned in PUCL v. Union of India (2021)
Obscenity, pornography and child sexual abuse materialPublishing or transmitting obscene material; sexually explicit material; child pornographyss.67, 67A and 67B IT Act (3/5/5 years and fine; s.67B specifically targets CSAM, including browsing and collecting); s.294 BNS (obscene books); ss.13–15 POCSO Act, 2012 — s.15 (storage/possession of CSAM) substantially strengthened in 2019; Just Rights for Children Alliance v. S. Harish (2024) — the Supreme Court held that viewing, storing and failing to delete CSAM is an offence under s.15 POCSO and recommended replacing the term “child pornography” with “child sexual exploitative and abuse material (CSEAM)”
Voyeurism and non-consensual imagesCapturing or disseminating images of a private act; “revenge porn”s.77 BNS (s.354-C IPC); s.66E IT Act (violation of privacy — capturing/publishing the image of a private area without consent, 3 years or ₹2 lakh)
Sextortion and “digital arrest” fraudsThreatening to publish intimate material, or impersonating law-enforcement officers over video call to extort moneyss.308, 319, 351 BNS; s.66D IT Act; a 2023–25 epidemic on which the Prime Minister and the MHA have issued public warnings and I4C has issued advisories
Deepfakes and AI-generated impersonationSynthetic audio/video used for fraud, defamation or election manipulations.66D IT Act (cheating by personation using a computer resource); s.319 BNS; Rule 3(1)(b)(vii) of the IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021 and the MeitY advisories of November 2023 and March 2024; Anil Kapoor v. Simply Life India (2023, Delhi HC) — personality-rights injunction against AI deepfakes

29.3 Offences against property and the economy

OffenceDescriptionIndian law
Hacking“Hacking is the most common form of cyber crime… The reason why hackers indulge in this crime may vary from monetary gain to political interest, or it may even be for the sake of sheer thrill. Hacking may be of different forms such as web-spoofing, e-mail bombing, trojan attacks, virus attacks, password cracking… In simple words hacking means seeking unauthorised access through [a] computer network”s.43 (civil liability, unlimited compensation) read with s.66 IT Act — 3 years or fine up to ₹5 lakh or both; s.70 — unauthorised access to a protected system: 10 years. (The older text’s reference to a fine of ₹1 crore reflects s.43 as it stood before the 2008 amendment, when s.43 capped compensation at ₹1 crore; the cap was then removed)
Web-jacking“Forcefully taking over control of a website of someone else or [of] the victim. The motive is usually ransom or attainment of some illegal political purpose”ss.43, 65, 66 IT Act; s.308 BNS (extortion)
E-mail bombing“Sending [a] large number of mails to the victim, which may be an individual or a company, to cause confusion and harassment”; the modern equivalent is the DDoS attacks.43(f) (denial of access) with s.66 IT Act
E-mail spoofing / phishing“A spoofed e-mail may be said to be one which misrepresents its origin.” Phishing extends this to the harvesting of credentials through cloned websites; vishing (voice), smishing (SMS)s.66C (identity theft — 3 years and ₹1 lakh) and s.66D IT Act (cheating by personation — 3 years and ₹1 lakh); ss.318, 319 BNS; NASSCOM v. Ajay Sood (2005) 119 DLT 596 (Del) — the first Indian judgment to define phishing as a misrepresentation and an actionable wrong, damages of ₹1.6 lakh awarded
Data diddling“Changing or erasing of data in subtle ways which makes it difficult to put the data back or be certain of its accuracy. This is resorted to for the purpose of illegal monetary gains or for committing a fraud or financial scam”s.43(d), (i) and (j) with s.66 IT Act; s.316 BNS. The classic Indian instance is manipulation of electricity billing data
Money laundering online“A kind of cyber crime in which money is illegally downloaded in transit. There is a phenomenal increase in the incidence of this cyber offence. Out of 146 seizures made by the Enforcement Directorate in money laundering cases in the year 2005, recoveries were made in 106 cases involving seizure of about ₹9.5 crore”PMLA, 2002 (§36–§37); crypto-laundering now covered by the notification of 7 March 2023 bringing virtual digital asset service providers within s.2(1)(sa) PMLA as “reporting entities”
Salami attack; internet time theft; cyber squattingSlicing negligible amounts from many accounts; unauthorised use of another’s paid internet hours; registering a domain name corresponding to another’s trade mark to extract paymentss.43, 66 IT Act; Trade Marks Act, 1999; the .IN Domain Name Dispute Resolution Policy
Intellectual property crimes“Intellectual property consists of a bundle of rights which may be violated by committing software piracy, copyright infringement, trade-mark and service-mark violations, theft of computer source code… Computer pirates steal away valuable intellectual property when they copy software, music, graphics/pictures, books, movies etc.”s.65 IT Act (tampering with computer source documents — 3 years or ₹2 lakh); s.63 Copyright Act, 1957 — 6 months to 3 years and fine of ₹50,000 to ₹2 lakh, with enhanced penalty for a second conviction (s.63A); s.63B — knowing use of an infringing copy of a computer programme: not less than 7 days and up to 3 years, fine ₹50,000 to ₹2 lakh. s.2(o) of the Copyright Act was amended in 1994 and 1999 (effective 13 January 2000) so that “literary work” includes a computer programme (source and object code) and database, and s.14 was amended to give the owner the exclusive right to reproduce or rent a computer database or programme

Domain names as trade marks — the three cases in the commentary

Rediff Communications Ltd. v. Cyberbooth & Ramesh NahataAIR 2000 Bom 27
Holding
“A domain name is more than an internet address and [is] therefore entitled to protection under the Trade Marks Act.” The defendant’s “radiff.com” was restrained as deceptively similar to “rediff.com”.
Satyam Infoway Ltd. v. Sifynet Solutions (P) Ltd.(2004) 6 SCC 145
Facts
The appellants, incorporated in 1995, had traded under the domain name “Sifynet” since June 1999 and had registered it with ICANN, claiming wide reputation and goodwill in “Sify”. The respondent began internet-marketing business under the domain name “Siffynet” from June 2001. The City Civil Court granted an injunction; the High Court reversed; the appellants appealed to the Supreme Court.
Issue
Whether a domain name has the characteristics of a trade mark, so that a passing-off action lies.
Arguments
Appellant: the domain name is a business identifier in which goodwill subsists; adoption of a phonetically identical name is calculated to deceive. Respondent: a domain name is a mere address; registration with ICANN confers no statutory right; the businesses were different.
Decision
“With the increase of commercial activities on the internet, a domain name is also used as a business identifier. It not only serves as an address for internet communication but also identifies the specific internet site for a specific business or its goods or services. It therefore has all the characteristics of a trade mark and a passing-off action can be based [on] infringement of [a] domain name right.” By adopting the phonetically similar “Siffy”, the respondent had “tried to cash in on the appellant’s reputation”. Appeal allowed; the High Court’s decision set aside and the City Civil Court’s order affirmed.
Principle
Domain names are protected by the law of passing off; the internet is not a law-free zone.
Yahoo! Inc. v. Akash Arora1999 PTC (19) 201 (Del)
Facts and issue
The defendant used “YahooIndia.com” for internet-related services. He contended that the provisions of the Trade Marks Act were not attracted, as service marks were not then recognised in India.
Decision
The Delhi High Court granted an injunction, holding that “though service marks are not recognised in India, the services rendered are to be recognised for ‘passing-off’ actions”. India’s first cyber-squatting judgment.

29.4 Cyber terrorism

Standard Indian commentary “With the emergence of new communication technologies, the nature and mode of operation of terrorism has undergone a radical change, giving rise to a new variety of terrorism called cyber-terrorism. According to the U.S. National Infrastructure Protection Center, cyber terrorism is defined as ‘a criminal act perpetrated by the use of computer and telecommunication capabilities, resulting in violence, destruction and/or disruption of services to create fear by causing confusion and uncertainty within a given population, with the goal of influencing a government [or] population to conform to a particular political, social or ideological agenda’.

Thus, cyber terrorism may be said to be a convergence of terrorism and cyber space, involving unlawful attacks or threats of attacks against computers, networks and the information stored therein, in order to coerce or intimidate a government or its people in furtherance of political, social or ideological objectives. Cyber terrorism is resorted to either by attacking the critical infrastructure via cyber attacks or by misusing the internet. …It may be defined as the premeditated use of disruptive activities, or the threat thereof, in cyber space, with the intention to further social, ideological, religious, political or similar objectives, or to intimidate any person in furtherance of such objectives.”

s.66F of the Information Technology Act, 2000 (inserted in 2008) is the Indian provision. Cyber terrorism is committed by (a) denying access to an authorised person, attempting to penetrate or access a computer resource without authorisation, or introducing a computer contaminant, with intent to threaten the unity, integrity, security or sovereignty of India or to strike terror, and thereby causing or likely to causing death or injuries, damage to property, disruption of supplies or services essential to the life of the community, or adversely affecting critical information infrastructure; or (b) knowingly or intentionally accessing restricted information with reason to believe that it may be used to injure the interests of India or its relations with a foreign State. Punishment: imprisonment for life. Allied provisions: s.70 (protected system — declared by notification; 10 years), s.70A (National Critical Information Infrastructure Protection Centre, NCIIPC), s.70B (CERT-In), and s.113 BNS / s.15 UAPA where the act also constitutes a terrorist act. Illustrations given in the commentary: the terrorist attack on the Indian Parliament on 13 December 2001; the misuse of computer technology in the attacks of 11 September 2001; and the 26 November 2008 Mumbai attacks. Modern Indian instances: the Kudankulam nuclear plant network intrusion (2019) and the AIIMS ransomware attack (2022).

30. CYBER CRIME III — the statutory framework and the response

30.1 The Information Technology Act, 2000 — the map

ProvisionContentPunishment
s.43Penalty and compensation for damage to a computer or computer system — unauthorised access, downloading, introducing a contaminant, damaging, disrupting, denying access, facilitating unauthorised access, tampering, destroying or stealing source codeCivil — compensation to the person affected (the ₹1 crore cap was removed in 2008); adjudicated by the Adjudicating Officer (s.46) up to ₹5 crore, above which the civil court has jurisdiction
s.43AFailure of a body corporate handling sensitive personal data to maintain reasonable security practicesCompensation; the SPDI Rules, 2011; largely superseded by the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 (penalties up to ₹250 crore), which also repeals s.43A
s.65Tampering with computer source documents3 years or ₹2 lakh or both
s.66Computer-related offences — doing any act referred to in s.43 dishonestly or fraudulently3 years or ₹5 lakh or both
s.66BDishonestly receiving a stolen computer resource or communication device3 years or ₹1 lakh
s.66CIdentity theft — fraudulent use of another’s electronic signature, password or unique identification feature3 years and ₹1 lakh
s.66DCheating by personation by using a computer resource3 years and ₹1 lakh
s.66EViolation of privacy — capturing, publishing or transmitting the image of a private area of a person without consent3 years or ₹2 lakh
