s.67CIntermediary’s duty to preserve and retain information3 years and fine
s.69Power to issue directions for interception, monitoring or decryption; s.69A blocking of public access; s.69B monitoring traffic dataNon-compliance: 7 years (s.69), 7 years (s.69A), 3 years (s.69B). Shreya Singhal upheld s.69A with its procedural safeguards while striking down s.66A and reading down s.79(3)(b); the IT (Procedure and Safeguards for Blocking) Rules, 2009
s.70, 70A, 70BProtected system; NCIIPC; CERT-In — s.70B(7): failure to furnish information to CERT-In is punishable with 1 year or ₹1 lakhs.70: 10 years
ss.71–74Misrepresentation; breach of confidentiality and privacy (s.72); disclosure of information in breach of lawful contract (s.72A); publishing a false electronic signature certificate2–3 years and fine
s.75Extra-territorial application — the Act applies to an offence committed outside India by any person if the act involves a computer, computer system or computer network located in IndiaThe jurisdictional key to cyber-crime prosecution
s.79 and the Intermediary Rules, 2021Safe harbour for intermediaries subject to due diligence; s.79(3)(b) read down in Shreya Singhal to require a court order or a government notification before takedown. The IT (Intermediary Guidelines and Digital Media Ethics Code) Rules, 2021 impose grievance officers, traceability of the “first originator” for significant social-media intermediaries (Rule 4(2) — challenged as violating privacy), and a 36-hour takedown timelineLoss of safe harbour
ss.65B BSA / 65B Evidence ActAdmissibility of electronic records — s.63 of the Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam, 2023 re-enacts s.65B of the Evidence Act with an expanded certificate. Anvar P.V. v. P.K. Basheer (2014) 10 SCC 473; Arjun Panditrao Khotkar v. Kailash Kushanrao Gorantyal (2020) 7 SCC 1 — the s.65B certificate is mandatory for secondary electronic evidence, resolving the conflict with Shafhi Mohammad. This is the provision on which most cyber prosecutions succeed or fail.—

30.2 Allied statutes, institutions and procedure

The BNS side
Most cyber offences are charged together with BNS provisions: s.318 (cheating), s.319 (cheating by personation), s.303 (theft of data as movable property — contested), s.336(3) and s.340 (forgery of an electronic record, forged document), s.356 (defamation), s.351 (criminal intimidation), s.78 (stalking), s.77 (voyeurism), s.196 (promoting enmity by electronic means), s.111 (organised crime — “cyber-crimes” expressly named) and s.112 (petty organised crime — card skimming and ATM theft expressly named).
Data protection
Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 — consent, purpose limitation, the duties of a Data Fiduciary, the rights of the Data Principal, the Data Protection Board, and penalties up to ₹250 crore for failure to prevent a data breach. It repeals s.43A of the IT Act. Read with K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India (2017) 10 SCC 1 — informational privacy is part of Art. 21, and any invasion must satisfy legality, legitimate aim, proportionality and procedural safeguards.
Institutions
I4C (Indian Cyber Crime Coordination Centre, 2020) with its seven verticals; the National Cybercrime Reporting Portal and helpline 1930; the Citizen Financial Cyber Fraud Reporting and Management System (the “golden hour” freeze of fraudulent transfers); CERT-In; NCIIPC; CCPWC scheme; State cyber police stations and cyber forensic labs; the RBI’s limited-liability circular of 6 July 2017 on unauthorised electronic banking transactions (zero liability where the customer reports within three working days) and the RBI Ombudsman Scheme, 2021.
Procedure
s.78 IT Act — investigation by an officer not below the rank of Inspector; s.80 — power of entry, search and arrest in a public place; s.173(1) BNSS Zero FIR and mandatory e-FIR for cognizable offences; s.94 BNSS (production of documents including electronic records); s.185 BNSS (search); Mutual Legal Assistance Treaties under ss.112–122 BNSS for evidence held abroad. Practical difficulty: most platforms are foreign, so MLAT requests take months — the single biggest operational obstacle in Indian cyber-crime investigation.

30.3 Prevention — “Preventive Legal Measures Against Cyber Crime”

  1. Technical. Firewalls, intrusion detection, encryption, multi-factor authentication, regular patching, backups, and CERT-In’s mandatory logging (the April 2022 directions: six-hour incident reporting and 180-day log retention within India).
  2. Institutional. Specialised cyber police stations, forensic laboratories accredited under s.79A IT Act as “Examiner of Electronic Evidence”, and trained prosecutors.
  3. Legal. Keeping the definitions technology-neutral; bringing virtual digital assets within the PMLA (done, March 2023); and India’s continuing decision not to accede to the Budapest Convention while participating in the negotiation of the UN Convention against Cybercrime (adopted by the General Assembly on 24 December 2024).
  4. Educational. Cyber hygiene campaigns; the commentary’s own point that “slightest negligence on the part of [the] computer user… may lead to catastrophic consequences”.
  5. Comparative note in the commentary: Sri Lanka’s Computer Crime Act is cited as a regional example; add the EU’s GDPR and NIS2 Directive, Singapore’s Computer Misuse Act and the UK’s Computer Misuse Act 1990.
Real-world Indian examples to cite State of Tamil Nadu v. Suhas Katti (2004, CMM Egmore) — the first conviction in India under s.67 of the IT Act, for posting obscene and annoying messages about a divorced woman in a Yahoo message group; conviction within about seven months of the FIR. Bank NSP case — misuse of a company’s e-mail system by a management trainee, establishing the employer’s exposure. SONY.SAMBANDH.COM case (2002) — conviction for online credit-card fraud under s.418/420 IPC; the court released the accused on probation, noting the novelty of the offence. Pune Citibank MphasiS BPO fraud (2005) — ₹1.5 crore siphoned by call-centre employees using customers’ credentials. Ritu Kohli (2001) — India’s first reported cyber-stalking complaint, which exposed the absence of a specific provision and led eventually to s.354-D IPC in 2013. Cosmos Bank (2018) — ₹94 crore stolen through malware-driven ATM cloning. AIIMS Delhi ransomware (2022). Use two or three of these; they show currency and specificity.

31. TRAFFICKING I — concept, the international definition and s.143 BNS

Syllabus head C(i)DU PYQ 2017 Q1(c) — “Human trafficking”Suggested reading: Prof. Usha Razdan (2012)

31.1 The concept — the DU model answer’s own definition

Verbatim — DU solved paper, 2017, Answer 1(c) PYQ “Human trafficking is the practice of people being hacked [trapped], lured, coerced or otherwise removed from their home or country, and then forced to work with no or low payments or on terms which are highly exploitative. Trafficking in people has been facilitated by porous borders and advanced communication technologies; it has become increasingly transnational in scope and highly lucrative.

Human Trafficking and Sexual Exploitation: The term encompasses the organized movement of people, usually women, between countries and within countries for sex work with the use of physical coercion, deception and bondage through forced debts. It includes coercing a migrant into a sexual act as a condition of allowing or arranging the migration. They are often promised work in [the] domestic or service industry, but instead are usually taken to brothels where their passport and other identification papers are confiscated.

Trafficking of Children: Trafficking and commercial sexual exploitation of children can take many forms and include forcing a child into prostitution or other forms of sexual activity or child pornography.

Trafficking in organs: In many countries waiting lists for transplants are very long and criminals have seized this opportunity to exploit the desperation of patients and potential donors.

In India it has become an ORGANIZED CRIME generating millions. The root cause of the problem is POVERTY, UNDER-DEVELOPMENT, UNEMPLOYMENT among others. Therefore we have to address the problem both SOCIALLY and LEGALLY.”

31.2 The three-element international definition — the analytical key

Article 3(a), UN Protocol to Prevent, Suppress and Punish Trafficking in Persons, Especially Women and Children, 2000 (the Palermo Protocol, supplementing UNTOC; ratified by India in May 2011): trafficking in persons means the recruitment, transportation, transfer, harbouring or receipt of persons, by means of the threat or use of force or other forms of coercion, of abduction, of fraud, of deception, of the abuse of power or of a position of vulnerability or of the giving or receiving of payments or benefits to achieve the consent of a person having control over another person, for the purpose of exploitation.

ElementContentNote
1. The ACTRecruitment · transportation · transfer · harbouring · receiptMovement across a border is not required — internal trafficking is trafficking
2. The MEANSThreat or use of force · coercion · abduction · fraud · deception · abuse of power or of a position of vulnerability · giving or receiving payments to a person in controlArt. 3(c): where the victim is a child (under 18), the “means” element is not required at all
3. The PURPOSE — exploitation“At a minimum, the exploitation of the prostitution of others or other forms of sexual exploitation, forced labour or services, slavery or practices similar to slavery, servitude or the removal of organs”Exploitation need not have occurred; the purpose suffices — trafficking is complete on the act done with the means for the purpose
CONSENTArt. 3(b): the consent of the victim is irrelevant where any of the means has been usedReproduced in India in Explanation 2 to s.143 BNS: “The consent of the victim is immaterial in [the] determination of the offence of trafficking” — exactly as the DU model answer records
Distinguish trafficking from three neighbouring concepts. (i) Smuggling of migrants (the second Palermo Protocol) — the “victim” consents, the relationship ends on arrival, and the offence is against the State’s immigration control; in trafficking the relationship begins on arrival and the offence is against the person. (ii) Prostitution — not all sex work is trafficking; the Supreme Court in Budhadev Karmaskar v. State of W.B. (2022) held that voluntary adult sex work is a profession, that consenting adult sex workers are entitled to equal protection of the criminal law, and that the police must not harass them or arrest them merely because the brothel is raided. (iii) Bonded labour — a form of exploitation which trafficking often supplies, prohibited by Art. 23 and the Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act, 1976.

31.3 s.143 BNS (s.370 IPC) — the Indian offence, as reproduced in the DU model answer

The provision, as set out in the DU 2017 solved paper PYQ “As per [the] Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2013, Section 370 of [the] IPC says: Whoever, for the purpose of exploitation, (a) recruits, (b) transports, (c) harbours, (d) transfers, or (e) receives a person or persons, by — (a) threat; (b) using force or any other form of coercion; (c) abduction; (d) practising fraud or deception; (e) by abuse of power; (f) by inducement — commits the offence of human trafficking.

Explanation 2 states that consent of the victim is immaterial in [the] determination of [the] offence.”
ProvisionContentPunishment
s.143(1) BNS (s.370(1) IPC)The definition above. Explanation 1: “exploitation” shall include any act of physical exploitation or any form of sexual exploitation, slavery or practices similar to slavery, servitude, or the forced removal of organs. Explanation 2: the consent of the victim is immaterial—
s.143(2)Trafficking simpliciter7 to 10 years RI and fine
s.143(3)Trafficking of more than one person10 years to life and fine
s.143(4)Trafficking of a child10 years to life and fine
s.143(5)Trafficking of more than one child14 years to life and fine
s.143(6)Person convicted of trafficking of a child on more than one occasionImprisonment for life, meaning the remainder of natural life, and fine
s.143(7)Where a public servant or a police officer is involved in the traffickingImprisonment for life (natural life) and fine
s.144 BNS (s.370A IPC)Exploitation of a trafficked person — engaging a trafficked child for sexual exploitation: 5 to 10 years; engaging a trafficked person: 3 to 7 yearsas stated
s.145 BNS (s.371 IPC)Habitual dealing in slavesLife or up to 10 years and fine
s.146 BNS (s.374 IPC)Unlawful compulsory labourUp to 1 year, or fine, or both, or community service
ss.98–99 BNS (ss.372–373 IPC)Selling and buying a child for purposes of prostitutionUp to 10 years and fine
s.139 BNS (s.363A IPC)Kidnapping or maiming a child for begging10 years to life; maiming: life
s.96 BNS (s.366A IPC)Procuration of a child for illicit intercourseUp to 10 years and fine

Constitutional foundation — always cite it first. Article 23(1): “Traffic in human beings and begar and other similar forms of forced labour are prohibited and any contravention of this provision shall be an offence punishable in accordance with law.” It binds not only the State but private persons — PUDR v. Union of India (1982) 3 SCC 235 held that payment below the minimum wage amounts to “forced labour” within Art. 23. See also Art. 24 (no child below 14 in hazardous employment), Arts. 39(e) and (f), Art. 21.

32. TRAFFICKING II — the Indian statutory scheme and the case law

32.1 The Immoral Traffic (Prevention) Act, 1956

Originally the Suppression of Immoral Traffic in Women and Girls Act, 1956 (SITA), enacted to give effect to the International Convention for the Suppression of the Traffic in Persons and of the Exploitation of the Prostitution of Others, 1950, which India signed on 9 May 1950; renamed and substantially amended in 1986.

SectionContentPunishment
s.2(f)“Prostitution” means the sexual exploitation or abuse of persons for commercial purposes. Note: the Act does not criminalise prostitution as such — it criminalises its commercialised organisation—
s.3Keeping a brothel or allowing premises to be used as a brothel1–3 years and ₹2,000 (first conviction); 2–5 years on a subsequent conviction. The standard criticism is that “the punishment for keeping a brothel or allowing premises to be used as a brothel is TOO LENIENT”
s.4Living on the earnings of prostitutionUp to 2 years; 7–10 years where the earnings relate to a child or minor
s.5Procuring, inducing or taking a person for the sake of prostitution — the core anti-trafficking provision3–7 years; 7 years to life where the person is a child
s.6Detaining a person in premises where prostitution is carried onNot less than 7 years, up to life; presumption where a child or minor is found in a brothel
s.7Prostitution in or in the vicinity of public placesUp to 3 months; aggravated where the person is a child
s.8Seducing or soliciting for the purpose of prostitutionUp to 6 months or ₹500 (first offence). This is the provision under which the trafficked woman herself is prosecuted — the single most criticised section in the Act
s.9Seduction of a person in custody7 years to life
ss.10A, 13, 14, 15, 16–19Detention in a corrective institution; Special Police Officer (s.13) and trafficking police officers; offences cognizable (s.14); search without warrant (s.15); rescue (s.16); intermediate custody (s.17); removal of prostitutes from any place (s.18); application by the woman to be kept in a protective home (s.19)—
s.21Protective homes — “no person or authority shall establish or maintain any protective home except under [a] licence by the State Government”, which may impose the condition that the Home be entrusted to women. “This is a salutary provision to check misuse of ‘homes’ as ‘dens’ of prostitution”—
ss.22A, 22AA, 23AEstablishment of Special Courts; s.22B allows summary trial, but the sentence in such a trial cannot exceed one year—
Advisory body“The State Government may associate with [the] Special Police Officer a non-official advisory body consisting of not more than five leading social welfare workers of the area, including [a] woman social welfare worker” — Delhi Administration v. Ram Singh AIR 1962 SC 63—
Shama Bai v. State of Uttar PradeshAIR 1959 All 57
Facts and issue
The constitutional validity of the Suppression of Immoral Traffic Act, 1956 was challenged, in particular ss.4(2) and 20, as violating the fundamental right to carry on a trade or profession under Art. 19(1)(g) and the right to life and liberty.
Arguments
Petitioner: the Act in substance prohibits a woman from pursuing her means of livelihood and is therefore an unreasonable restriction; s.20 (removal of a prostitute from a place) confers arbitrary power. State: the Act does not prohibit the profession; it regulates its commercialised and exploitative forms in the interest of public morality and health, as permitted by Art. 19(6).
Decision
The Court held that “Sections 4(2) and 20 of the Act were constitutional and not violative of fundamental rights, as none of the provisions of the Act are intended to stop [the] profession or trade of a prostitute altogether. Only restrictions can be imposed reasonably, as Section 3 only punishes running of a brothel.”
Principle
The ITPA targets the commercial exploitation of prostitution, not the prostitute. It is the doctrinal foundation of the position later confirmed in Budhadev Karmaskar. Note also that Article 23 of the Constitution “prohibits immoral traffic in human beings and makes it punishable under the law”.

32.2 The other forms of exploitation and the statutes that reach them

Bonded and forced labour
Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act, 1976 — s.4 abolishes the bonded labour system, s.6 extinguishes the debt, ss.16–17 penalise enforcement of bonded labour (3 years and ₹2,000), s.13 provides for Vigilance Committees. Bandhua Mukti Morcha v. Union of India (1984) 3 SCC 161 — a letter treated as a writ petition; directions for identification, release and rehabilitation of bonded labourers in the Faridabad stone quarries; the Court held that a person found working for less than the minimum wage is presumed to be a bonded labourer. PUDR v. Union of India (Asiad Workers) (1982) 3 SCC 235 — payment below the minimum wage is “forced labour” under Art. 23. Central Sector Scheme for Rehabilitation of Bonded Labourers, 2016 (₹1–3 lakh per released labourer).
Child labour and child trafficking
Child and Adolescent Labour (Prohibition and Regulation) Act, 1986 (as amended 2016) — s.3 total prohibition for children under 14 (with a family-enterprise exception, criticised), s.3A prohibition of adolescents (14–18) in hazardous occupations, s.14 penalty (6 months to 2 years and/or ₹20,000–₹50,000). Art. 24. JJ Act, 2015 ss.75–79 — cruelty to a child, employment for begging, giving intoxicating substances, and s.79 exploitation of a child employee. M.C. Mehta v. State of Tamil Nadu (1996) 6 SCC 756 (Sivakasi) — ₹20,000 per child into a Child Labour Rehabilitation-cum-Welfare Fund from every offending employer, plus ₹5,000 from the appropriate Government. Bachpan Bachao Andolan v. Union of India (2011) 5 SCC 1 — directions on children in circuses; (2011) 5 SCC 1 and the 2013 order on missing children: registration of an FIR is mandatory in every case of a missing child, with a presumption of trafficking.
Organ trade
Transplantation of Human Organs and Tissues Act, 1994 (amended 2011) — s.19 punishes commercial dealings in human organs (5–10 years and ₹20 lakh–₹1 crore after the 2011 amendment), s.18 removal without authority, s.20 contravention generally; Authorisation Committees to screen unrelated donors. Indian instances: the Gurgaon kidney racket (2008) and periodic rackets in Delhi, Chennai and Kolkata. The DU model answer’s observation is exactly right: “in many countries waiting lists for transplants are very long and criminals have seized this opportunity to exploit the desperation of patients and potential donors.”
Surrogacy, adoption and marriage trafficking
Surrogacy (Regulation) Act, 2021 — commercial surrogacy prohibited; only altruistic surrogacy permitted; penalties up to 10 years and ₹10 lakh. Assisted Reproductive Technology (Regulation) Act, 2021. Illegal adoption: s.80 and s.81 JJ Act, 2015 (giving or receiving a child otherwise than in accordance with the Act: 3–5 years or ₹1 lakh). Bride trafficking from Assam, West Bengal and Jharkhand into Haryana and Rajasthan — prosecuted under s.143 BNS with the Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, 2006 and s.87 BNS.
Trafficking for begging and for crime
s.139 BNS (s.363A IPC) — kidnapping or maiming a child for begging; s.95 BNS (s.83 IPC) — hiring or employing a child to commit an offence; s.76 JJ Act. The Bombay Prevention of Begging Act, 1959 is the wrong instrument, and 25 of its sections were struck down in Harsh Mander v. Union of India 2018 SCC OnLine Del 10427: the trafficked beggar was being punished instead of the trafficker.
Enforcement machinery
Anti-Human Trafficking Units (AHTUs) in districts (MHA scheme); the Anti-Trafficking Cell in the MHA; NIA jurisdiction over human trafficking since the NIA (Amendment) Act, 2019; s.3 of the ITPA special police officers; the Ujjawala scheme (prevention, rescue, rehabilitation, reintegration) and Swadhar Greh; Childline 1098; the Missing Child Alert / Khoya-Paya portals; victim compensation under s.396 BNSS (s.357-A CrPC) and NALSA’s Compensation Scheme for Women Victims/Survivors of Sexual Assault, 2018.

32.3 The leading Indian cases

Vishal Jeet v. Union of India(1990) 3 SCC 318trafficking
Facts
A public interest petition under Art. 32 complaining of (i) forced prostitution of girls and children, (ii) the Devadasi and Jogin systems under which girls are dedicated to temple deities and then sexually exploited, and (iii) the connivance of police and other officials.
Issues
Whether the Court should issue directions to the Central and State Governments for the eradication of forced prostitution, the Devadasi system and child prostitution, and for the rescue and rehabilitation of the victims.
Arguments
Petitioner: Articles 23, 39(e) and (f) and 21 are violated; the existing machinery under SITA and the IPC is a dead letter because of official connivance; a CBI inquiry and directions are needed. Union and States: the subject is one for the executive; laws already exist; the Court should not assume administrative functions.
Decision
The Supreme Court declined a CBI inquiry but issued extensive directions: State Governments and Union Territories to direct their law-enforcement authorities to take appropriate and speedy action under the existing laws; to set up Advisory Committees to make suggestions for eradicating child prostitution and for the care, protection, rehabilitation and vocational training of the rescued victims; to provide rehabilitative homes manned by well-qualified trained social workers, psychiatrists and doctors; and to “take steps in providing adequate and rehabilitative homes” for the devadasis and jogins. The Court observed that the malady is “neither a religious nor a social problem but a SOCIO-ECONOMIC problem” and therefore cannot be eradicated by punitive measures alone.
Principle
The State’s obligation under Art. 23 is positive: not merely to refrain from trafficking but to rescue, rehabilitate and reintegrate. The Court also made the enduring observation that the victim of trafficking must not be treated as an offender.
Gaurav Jain v. Union of IndiaAIR 1997 SC 3021; (1997) 8 SCC 114trafficking
Facts
A PIL arising out of an article titled “A Red Light Trap: Society Gives No Chance to Prostitutes’ Offspring”, concerning the plight of children of prostitutes and of the women themselves in red-light areas.
Issues
(i) Whether the children of prostitutes should be segregated from their mothers and housed in separate institutions. (ii) What directions should issue for the rescue, rehabilitation and socio-economic empowerment of women in prostitution.
Arguments
Petitioner: children of prostitutes are condemned to the trade by their environment and should be provided separate hostels and schooling; the State has failed in its duty under Arts. 21, 23, 39(f) and 45. Respondents and amici: segregation would violate the child’s right to a family and stigmatise both mother and child; the answer lies in rehabilitation of the mother, not removal of the child.
Decision
The Court declined to order segregation, holding that children should not be separated from their mothers merely because of the mother’s occupation, and instead issued directions to the Social Welfare Department of the Government of India and the States “to initiate adequate measures for prevention of induction of women in various forms of prostitution and [to] rescue them from the vile flesh-trade and to provide them dignity of person, means of livelihood and SOCIO-ECONOMIC EMPOWERMENT”. It directed the constitution of a Committee to evolve a scheme, juvenile homes for the children, and the implementation of Art. 39(f) and Art. 45.
Principle
Rehabilitation, not segregation; the remedy for trafficking is economic empowerment, which places the judgment squarely within Bonger’s economic explanation of crime. Note the later procedural history: a review bench in Gaurav Jain (1998) clarified that directions in a PIL must be within the scope of the pleadings.
Budhadev Karmaskar v. State of West Bengal(2011) 11 SCC 538; orders of 19 and 26 May 2022sex work
Facts
A criminal appeal against conviction for the brutal murder of a sex worker. The Supreme Court, while dismissing the appeal, converted the matter into a suo motu public interest proceeding on the conditions of sex workers.
Issue
What rights do adult, voluntary sex workers have under Art. 21, and how must the police and the State treat them?
Decision
“Sex workers are entitled to equal protection of the law… They have a right to live with dignity under Article 21.” In May 2022, exercising its power under Art. 142, the Court issued directions including: sex work is a “profession” and voluntary adult sex workers must not be “arrested, penalised, harassed or victimised” when a brothel is raided, since “voluntary sex work is not illegal and only running the brothel is unlawful”; the police must not discriminate in registering FIRs where a sex worker is the victim of a crime, particularly a sexual offence; media must not reveal identities; the child of a sex worker shall not be separated merely on that ground; and Aadhaar and ration cards must be issued without disclosure of occupation.
Principle
The law must distinguish trafficking and forced prostitution, which are crimes, from voluntary adult sex work, which is not. This is the modern answer to the criticism of s.8 ITPA and the key case to cite on the victim-versus-offender problem.
Prajwala v. Union of India(2015) and subsequent orders; see also In Re: Prajwala Letter (2018)trafficking
Facts and issue
An NGO’s letter enclosing videos of sexual assault circulating on the internet; and a wider petition on the rescue and rehabilitation of trafficking victims. The issue was how to prevent the circulation of rape and child sexual abuse videos, and how to institutionalise victim rehabilitation.
Decision
The Court directed the constitution of an expert committee with MeitY, CBI and intermediaries to devise technical solutions (hash-matching, auto-blocking, proactive monitoring); directed the CBI to investigate; and, on the trafficking side, directed the creation of an Organised Crime Investigative Agency proposal and pressed the Union to finalise a comprehensive anti-trafficking law and a victim rehabilitation protocol.
Principle
Platform responsibility for CSAM and rape videos; and the recognition that rescue without rehabilitation returns the victim to the trafficker. It is the origin of the current push for the Trafficking in Persons (Prevention, Care and Rehabilitation) Bill.
The legislative gap. India still has no single comprehensive anti-trafficking statute. The Trafficking of Persons (Prevention, Protection and Rehabilitation) Bill, 2018 was passed by the Lok Sabha but lapsed; a redrafted Trafficking in Persons (Prevention, Care and Rehabilitation) Bill, 2021 was circulated but not enacted. The field is therefore governed by a patchwork: s.143 BNS + ITPA + POCSO + JJ Act + Bonded Labour Act + CALPRA + THOTA. Say this — it is the single best “reform needed” point.

33. TRAFFICKING III — the SAARC dimension and the international framework

Suggested reading: Prof. Usha Razdan, “Combating Trafficking of Women and Children in SAARC Countries: Issues and Intricacies”, Journal of Criminology and Criminal Justice (2012)

33.1 Why South Asia is the epicentre

Standard Indian commentary “The trafficking of women, young girls and children at [the] international level continues to be a global problem which is generating seven billion dollars [of] business every year. It is the THIRD LARGEST illegal transnational business after DRUGS and ARMS. The merchandise for this lucrative trade is mostly women and children from marginal communities who are looking for a viable employment and means of survival. The traffickers use the bait of better life opportunities, to deceive the victims and exploit their innate human need to migrate for better opportunities. Women and young girls are primarily trafficked to satiate the demand of the global sex trade. The principle underlying illegal human trafficking is the SHEER POWERLESSNESS of the victims arising out of GENDER DISPARITIES and the LACK OF ECONOMIC CHOICES. Although each nation state has laws and policies against illegal human trafficking, they LACK EFFECTIVE IMPLEMENTATION.

The global community is constantly responding… by formulating numerous regional and International Conventions… Some of these are the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), 1979 and the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC), 1989. The Beijing + 5 Conference and [a] recent SAARC Conference at Male also took up this issue for deliberation and discussion.”
The push factors (source)
Poverty and landlessness; unemployment and agrarian distress; illiteracy; gender discrimination and son preference; child marriage; caste and tribal marginalisation; natural disasters and displacement (the Kosi floods, the 2015 Nepal earthquake, cyclones in the Sundarbans); armed conflict and insurgency; dysfunctional families and domestic violence; the collapse of traditional livelihoods. The three most trafficked-from Indian States are consistently West Bengal, Jharkhand and Assam, with Bihar, Odisha and the North-East close behind.
The pull factors (destination)
Demand for commercial sex; demand for cheap and docile domestic, construction and agricultural labour; demand for brides in States with skewed sex ratios (Haryana, Punjab, Rajasthan — the “paro” or “molki” bride phenomenon); demand for organs; demand for children for adoption, begging and entertainment. Destination States: Delhi, Maharashtra, Karnataka, Tamil Nadu, Goa, Haryana and Punjab, and internationally the Gulf States and South-East Asia.
The facilitating factors
Porous and open borders — the India–Nepal border is open under the Treaty of Peace and Friendship, 1950, so movement requires no passport at all; the India–Bangladesh border is long and permeable. Add: a large informal labour market; unregulated placement agencies; the internet and social media; and, critically, corruption and police complicity — which is why s.143(7) BNS prescribes imprisonment for life where a public servant or police officer is involved.
Why prosecution fails
Victims will not depose (fear, stigma, dependence, the absence of an alternative livelihood); the victim is often prosecuted herself under s.8 ITPA; age determination is difficult; the offence spans several States and countries, creating jurisdictional gaps; investigating officers are untrained; there is no effective witness protection; and rescue without rehabilitation returns the victim to the trafficker. Conviction rates under the ITPA and s.370 IPC are among the lowest of any offence in the NCRB tables.

33.2 The SAARC Convention, 2002

The SAARC Convention on Preventing and Combating Trafficking in Women and Children for Prostitution, signed at Kathmandu on 5 January 2002, is the regional instrument. Its salient features:

  • Art. I(3) defines “trafficking” as “the moving, selling or buying of women and children for prostitution within and outside a country for monetary or other considerations with or without the consent of the person subjected to trafficking”.
  • Art. III requires each State Party to make trafficking, and the keeping of a brothel for the purpose, a punishable offence, with aggravating circumstances where the offender is a public servant, or the victim is a child, or organised crime is involved.
  • Art. IV — aggravated punishment for repeat offenders and for members of an organised criminal group.
  • Art. V — jurisdiction; Art. VI — extradition and mutual legal assistance; the offence is deemed to be an extraditable offence in existing treaties.
  • Art. VIII — mutual assistance, a Regional Task Force, and cooperation between police, judicial and prosecuting authorities.
  • Art. IX — care, treatment, rehabilitation and repatriation of victims; establishment of protective homes; and — importantly — “Member States shall establish a Regional Task Force to facilitate implementation”.
The standard criticisms of the SAARC Convention — this is what Prof. Razdan’s title means by “Issues and Intricacies”.
  1. It is confined to trafficking for PROSTITUTION. Trafficking for labour, domestic servitude, organ removal, begging, bride purchase and adoption — quantitatively the larger part of South Asian trafficking — falls outside it. Contrast the Palermo Protocol, which covers all forms of exploitation.
  2. It is confined to WOMEN AND CHILDREN. Trafficked men and boys are excluded.
  3. “With or without the consent” is welcome, but the definition uses “moving, selling or buying”, which is narrower than the Palermo triad of act–means–purpose and does not expressly cover harbouring or receipt.
  4. No individual complaint or monitoring mechanism; the Regional Task Force has met infrequently and SAARC itself has been largely dormant since 2014.
  5. Implementation is left entirely to domestic law, and domestic laws differ sharply — Nepal’s Human Trafficking and Transportation (Control) Act, 2007; Bangladesh’s Prevention and Suppression of Human Trafficking Act, 2012; Pakistan’s Prevention of Trafficking in Persons Act, 2018; Sri Lanka’s Penal Code amendments. The resulting mismatch of definitions defeats extradition, because double criminality cannot be established.
  6. No provision for the repatriated victim’s rehabilitation in the country of origin beyond an exhortation, and no funding mechanism.
  7. Victim protection is subordinated to law enforcement — the Convention’s centre of gravity is criminalisation, not the rights of the trafficked person.
The recommended reforms: harmonise definitions on the Palermo model; extend to all forms of exploitation and to all persons; create bilateral standard operating procedures for cross-border rescue and repatriation (India and Bangladesh signed a Memorandum of Understanding on bi-lateral cooperation on trafficking in 2015, and India–Nepal cooperation runs through the border police and NGOs such as Maiti Nepal); a regional victim database; and a non-punishment principle for trafficked persons.

33.3 The international instruments in one place Treaty

InstrumentYearRelevance · India’s position
Slavery Convention; Supplementary Convention on the Abolition of Slavery1926; 1956India is a party; the source of the “practices similar to slavery” language in s.143 BNS
ILO Forced Labour Convention No. 29; Abolition of Forced Labour Convention No. 1051930; 1957India ratified No. 29 (1954) and No. 105 (2000)
Convention for the Suppression of the Traffic in Persons and of the Exploitation of the Prostitution of Others1950Signed by India on 9 May 1950; the immediate source of SITA 1956 → ITPA
CEDAW — Art. 6 requires States to suppress all forms of traffic in women and exploitation of prostitution1979India ratified in 1993 with declarations; the basis of Vishaka-style incorporation of treaty norms
Convention on the Rights of the Child and its Optional Protocol on the Sale of Children, Child Prostitution and Child Pornography1989; 2000India ratified the CRC in 1992 and the Optional Protocol in 2005; the foundation of the JJ Act and POCSO
ILO Convention No. 182 on the Worst Forms of Child Labour1999Ratified by India in 2017, along with Convention No. 138 on minimum age — the reason for the 2016 amendment to the child-labour Act
UNTOC and the Palermo Protocol on trafficking (and the Protocols on smuggling of migrants and firearms)2000Ratified by India in May 2011 — the direct cause of the Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2013 substituting s.370 IPC
SAARC Convention on trafficking in women and children for prostitution2002Signed at Kathmandu, 5 January 2002
Sustainable Development Goals — Targets 5.2, 8.7 and 16.22015Elimination of trafficking, forced labour and child labour by 2030
US State Department Trafficking in Persons (TIP) ReportannualIndia has been placed in Tier 2 — a useful comparative datum, though its methodology is contested
Model structure for “Human trafficking” (2017 Q1(c) — a short note of about 250–300 words). (1) Definition — quote the DU answer’s definition and then the Palermo three-element test. (2) Forms — sexual exploitation, forced/bonded labour, child labour and begging, organ removal, bride trafficking. (3) Scale — “the third largest illegal transnational business after drugs and arms”, about US$7 billion a year; NCRB India data. (4) Causes — “poverty, under-development, unemployment”, gender disparity, porous borders, technology. (5) Indian law — Art. 23; s.143–144 BNS (s.370–370A IPC) with consent immaterial; ITPA 1956 ss.3–9; POCSO; JJ Act; Bonded Labour Act; THOTA. (6) Case law — Vishal Jeet, Gaurav Jain, Bachpan Bachao Andolan, Budhadev Karmaskar. (7) Criticism — s.8 ITPA punishes the victim; no comprehensive statute; low conviction rate; SAARC Convention limited to prostitution. (8) Conclusion — the DU answer’s own words: “we have to address the problem both socially and legally.”

34. NARCOTIC TRADE I — the drug problem and the illicit traffic

34.1 Why illicit drug trafficking is the paradigm organised crime

Standard Indian commentary “Illicit drug trafficking is so complex in nature that it involves a large variety of drugs from many sources throughout the world. It not only violates national drug laws and international conventions but also involves several other allied activities such as RACKETEERING, CONSPIRACY, BRIBERY AND CORRUPTION, TAX-EVASION, ILLEGAL MONEY TRANSACTIONS, VIOLATION OF IMPORT AND EXPORT LAWS, CRIMES OF VIOLENCE AND TERRORISM. The wide range of illegal and criminal activities associated with illegal drug trafficking poses a threat to law-enforcement agencies throughout the globe.

Considering the ever-increasing demand and consumption of liquor, opium, heroin etc. and [the] huge profit in their illicit trafficking, the legislative control measures have not been able to countenance the menace, particularly because big tycoons and drug syndicates having INTERNATIONAL LINKAGE are involved in this illegal trade.”

Note the list in the first sentence. Drug trafficking is not one offence; it is a portfolio — it necessarily generates corruption, money laundering, tax evasion, customs violations, violence and, in the Indian context, terrorism. That is precisely why the NDPS Act is scheduled as a predicate offence under the PMLA and why “narco-terrorism” is a recognised category.

34.2 India’s geography — the reason the problem is structural

Standard Indian commentary “In the Indian context, the geographical location of this country is most suited to the unabated inflow of drugs and illicit drug products by sea and land routes from all sides. Iran, Afghanistan and Pakistan, which are the largest producers of opium, manufacture heroin from it and the same is smuggled into India. So [is] also the case with [the] North-East neighbouring countries, namely, Burma, Thailand and Laos. The State of Bihar provides [an] entry-route for ganja from Nepal. Quite a large quantity of opium and cannabis [is] being illicitly cultivated within India itself, besides the licensed cultivation for medical purposes. All these factors cumulatively provide a fertile ground for illicit drug trafficking both within and outside the country.”
The Golden Crescent
Iran – Afghanistan – Pakistan, to India’s north-west. The world’s largest source of illicit opium and heroin. Routes: overland through Punjab and Rajasthan (the “Punjab drug crisis”), and by sea into Gujarat and the Arabian Sea — hence the very large maritime seizures off the Gujarat and Kerala coasts by the Narcotics Control Bureau and the Indian Navy in recent years.
The Golden Triangle
Myanmar – Thailand – Laos, to India’s north-east. Source of heroin and, increasingly, of methamphetamine (“yaba”) and precursor chemicals. Routes through Manipur, Mizoram and Nagaland; the drug-and-insurgency nexus in the North-East.
Domestic cultivation and diversion
India is the world’s largest licensed producer of opium for medical purposes, grown under licence in Madhya Pradesh, Rajasthan and Uttar Pradesh under the Central Bureau of Narcotics — with the corresponding risk of diversion. Illicit cannabis and poppy cultivation in the Himalayan belt, Odisha, Andhra Pradesh and the North-East; and diversion of pharmaceutical opioids (tramadol, codeine cough syrup) and precursor chemicals such as ephedrine and pseudoephedrine.
The new frontier — darknet and courier
The International Narcotics Control Board (INCB), in its annual report released on 20 February 2001, observed that “[the] internet is fast becoming a growing source of on-line trafficking, since it provides an easy access to controlled substances including narcotic drugs and psychotropic substances like benzodiazepines, barbiturates and various amphetamine-type stimulants”; that “with the expansion of internet shopping… some companies are openly advertising on [the] internet that they provide controlled drugs without prescription”; and that “countries should introduce legislative changes to allow prosecution of illicit internet drugstores”. Shri C. Chakraborty of the INCB suggested that governments “take preventive measures to restrict the ‘excessive availability’ of controlled drugs [through] on-line shopping, as over-medication leads to untold physical and mental suffering.” Modern Indian instances: darknet LSD and MDMA networks dismantled by the NCB (2020–2023), and the use of couriers and cryptocurrency.

34.3 The classification of drugs

Standard Indian commentary “The International Convention on Drugs, to which India is a signatory, has classified drugs under two categories: (a) Narcotic Drugs; and (b) Psychotropic Substances.
(a) Narcotic Drugs — the main drugs covered under this head are: (i) opium and its derivatives like brown sugar, heroin and codeine; (ii) coca leaf, cocaine; (iii) cannabis, cannabis resin, extracts and tinctures; (iv) methadone, pethidine, thebaine.
(b) Psychotropic Substances include valium, diazepam, tidigesic, morphine etc.”
[Opium is taken from the opium poppy, “papaver somniferum”, and is generally used as a pain-killer and hypnotic in certain regions of India.]
The statutory definitions — use these, they are more precise. Under the NDPS Act, 1985: s.2(xiv) “narcotic drug” means coca leaf, cannabis (hemp), opium, poppy straw and all manufactured drugs; s.2(xxiii) “psychotropic substance” means any substance, natural or synthetic, or any salt or preparation included in the Schedule to the Act. s.2(iii) “cannabis (hemp)” means charas (the separated resin), ganja (the flowering or fruiting tops), and any mixture or drink prepared from them — note the famous exclusion: the seeds and leaves, when not accompanied by the tops (i.e. bhang), are outside the definition. The international framework is the Single Convention on Narcotic Drugs, 1961 (as amended by the 1972 Protocol), the Convention on Psychotropic Substances, 1971, and the UN Convention against Illicit Traffic in Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances, 1988 (the Vienna Convention) — it was to give effect to these three that the NDPS Act was enacted, as its Preamble records.

34.4 Drugs, alcohol and crime — the criminological link

Standard Indian commentary “Like drug-addiction, alcoholism also causes disillusionment, unhappiness and troubles in family life. An alcoholic finds it difficult to adjust with other people… The mental faculty of an alcoholic does not function in a normal way, with the result [that] he loses self-control and often behaves improperly, at times picking up quarrels, accusing, abusing or insulting others or committing acts of aggression and violence. An alcoholic, while he is drunk, is an irresponsible person… and prone to criminality at any moment. The acts of indecency or assault are usually committed by [persons] under the influence of liquor or narcotic drugs. According to sources used for the National Survey on the Extent, Pattern and Trends of Drug Abuse in India (2004), alcohol is the most commonly used substance…”

The three-fold link (Goldstein’s tripartite framework) — state it in an answer:

  1. Psychopharmacological — the effect of the substance itself produces disinhibition, aggression or paranoia. Indian evidence: alcohol is implicated in a very large proportion of road fatalities, domestic violence and assault. s.23–24 BNS (voluntary intoxication no defence), s.185 Motor Vehicles Act, 1988 (drunken driving).
  2. Economic-compulsive — the addict commits acquisitive crime to finance the habit. Bonger and Merton’s innovation apply directly.
  3. Systemic — the violence intrinsic to an illegal market: territorial disputes, enforcement of debts, punishment of informers. This is the violence of prohibition, not of the drug.

Add the Indian data: the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment’s “Magnitude of Substance Use in India” (AIIMS–NDDTC, 2019) found that alcohol is the most widely used psychoactive substance (about 14.6% of those aged 10–75, roughly 16 crore persons), followed by cannabis (about 3.1 crore) and opioids (about 2.26 crore, of whom over 77 lakh need help) — with Punjab, Haryana, Delhi, Manipur, Mizoram, Nagaland, Sikkim and Uttar Pradesh showing the highest opioid prevalence. NCRB records the number of NDPS cases registered annually and deaths by drug abuse in the ADSI volume.

35. NARCOTIC TRADE II — the NDPS Act, 1985

35.1 Scheme and object

The Narcotic Drugs and Psychotropic Substances Act, 1985 replaced three colonial statutes — the Opium Act, 1857, the Opium Act, 1878 and the Dangerous Drugs Act, 1930 — and was enacted to give effect to India’s obligations under the 1961, 1971 and 1988 UN Conventions and to the mandate of Article 47 of the Constitution (“the State shall endeavour to bring about prohibition of the consumption except for medicinal purposes of intoxicating drinks and of drugs which are injurious to health”). It has been amended in 1988 (mandatory minimum sentences, death penalty for repeat offenders), 2001 (the quantity-based sentencing structure), 2014 (“essential narcotic drugs” for medical use) and 2021 (a drafting correction to s.27A).

35.2 The provisions, section by section

SectionContentPunishment / note
s.2Definitions — (iii) cannabis (hemp) = charas, ganja and mixtures (excluding seeds and leaves not accompanied by the tops, i.e. bhang); (xiv) narcotic drug; (xxiii) psychotropic substance; (vii-a) commercial quantity and (xxiii-a) small quantity as notified; (viii-b) illicit traffic—
s.4, s.5Central Government’s power and duty to take measures; the Narcotics Control Bureau constituted under s.4(3) in 1986—
s.8The core prohibition: no person shall cultivate coca, opium poppy or cannabis, or produce, manufacture, possess, sell, purchase, transport, warehouse, use, consume, import inter-State, export inter-State, import into India, export from India or tranship any narcotic drug or psychotropic substance — except for medical or scientific purposes and in the manner prescribed—
ss.15–23Offences in respect of poppy straw (15), coca plant (16), prepared opium (17), opium poppy and opium (18), embezzlement by a cultivator (19), cannabis (20), manufactured drugs (21), psychotropic substances (22), and illegal import/export/transhipment (23)THE QUANTITY-BASED LADDER (2001 Amendment):
• small quantity — up to 1 year rigorous imprisonment or fine up to ₹10,000 or both;
• more than small but less than commercial — up to 10 years and fine up to ₹1 lakh;
• commercial quantity — not less than 10 years, extendable to 20, and fine of not less than ₹1 lakh.
This graded structure is the single most important feature of the Act and the point most frequently examined
s.24External dealings in NDPS without previous authorisation10–20 years and ₹1–2 lakh
