s.27CONSUMPTION of a narcotic drug or psychotropic substanceCocaine, morphine, diacetylmorphine and notified drugs: up to 1 year or ₹20,000; any other drug: up to 6 months or ₹10,000. The lowest punishment in the Act — a legislative recognition that the addict is a patient, not a criminal
s.27AFinancing illicit traffic and harbouring offenders — the “kingpin” provision10–20 years and fine ₹1–2 lakh. A scheduled offence under the PMLA
s.28, s.29, s.30Attempt; abetment and criminal conspiracy (same punishment as the offence itself, even if committed outside India); preparations.29 is the provision most used against syndicate members
s.31Enhanced punishment for repeat offenders — one and a half times the maximum—
s.31ADeath penalty for certain repeat offences involving specified quantitiesMade discretionary (“may be punished with death”) by the 2014 Amendment, after Indian Harm Reduction Network v. Union of India 2011 SCC OnLine Bom 921, in which the Bombay High Court held the mandatory death sentence under s.31A unconstitutional and read it down as directory
s.32BFactors to be taken into account for imposing a punishment higher than the minimum — use of violence or arms, holding public office, involving a minor, organised group, etc.The Act’s own sentencing guideline
s.35Presumption of culpable mental state — “in any prosecution for an offence under this Act which requires a culpable mental state of the accused, the court shall presume the existence of such mental state”, and the accused must prove its absence beyond reasonable doubtA direct enactment of the 47th Report’s recommendation (§21.1)
s.37Offences to be cognizable and NON-BAILABLE; for offences involving commercial quantity, the twin conditions: bail only if the Public Prosecutor has had an opportunity to oppose and the court is satisfied that there are reasonable grounds for believing that the accused is not guilty and is not likely to commit any offence while on bailThe provision responsible for the very high proportion of NDPS undertrials. Union of India v. Rattan Mallik (2009) 2 SCC 624; Union of India v. Niyazuddin Sk (2018) 13 SCC 738; but see Rabi Prakash v. State of Odisha (2023) — prolonged incarceration without trial defeats s.37, Art. 21 prevailing
s.41–43Power of entry, search, seizure and arrest — with warrant (s.41) and without warrant (s.42, between sunrise and sunset in a building/conveyance/enclosed place; s.43 in a public place)Karnail Singh v. State of Haryana (2009) 8 SCC 539 (Constitution Bench) — substantial compliance with s.42 (recording of information and sending it to the superior) suffices; total non-compliance is impermissible. Distinguish public places (s.43), where s.42 does not apply: State of Punjab v. Baldev Singh (1999) 6 SCC 172
s.50Search of a person — the person to be searched must be informed of his right to be searched in the presence of a Gazetted Officer or a MagistrateState of Punjab v. Baldev Singh (1999) 6 SCC 172 (Constitution Bench) — failure to inform vitiates the trial as regards recovery from the person; Vijaysinh Chandubha Jadeja v. State of Gujarat (2011) 1 SCC 609 — the requirement is mandatory and substantial compliance is not enough; State of Himachal Pradesh v. Pawan Kumar (2005) 4 SCC 350 — s.50 applies to the search of the person, not of a bag carried by him
s.52ADisposal of seized narcotic drugs; inventory, photographs and samples certified by a MagistrateUnion of India v. Mohanlal (2016) 3 SCC 379 — non-compliance with s.52A is a serious lapse; the certified inventory is primary evidence. Yusuf @ Asif v. State (2023) and Simarnjit Singh v. State of Punjab (2023) — failure to draw samples in the Magistrate’s presence is fatal
s.54Presumption from possession of illicit articles — the accused must account satisfactorilyRead with s.35; but conscious possession must first be proved by the prosecution: Mohan Lal v. State of Rajasthan (2015) 6 SCC 222
s.64AImmunity from prosecution for an addict charged under s.27 (or with a small quantity) who volunteers for de-addiction treatment at a recognised centreThe reformative provision — under-used in practice, and a good “reform” point
s.67Power to call for information and examine personsTofan Singh v. State of Tamil Nadu (2021) 4 SCC 1 (3-Judge Bench, 2:1) — officers of the NCB, DRI etc. under s.53 are “police officers” within s.25 of the Evidence Act, and a confessional statement recorded under s.67 is NOT admissible. This overruled Kanhaiyalal v. Union of India (2008) 4 SCC 668 and is the most important recent NDPS decision
s.68A–68ZForfeiture of illegally acquired property of persons convicted under the Act or detained under PITNDPSThe asset-recovery arm, parallel to the PMLA
s.36–36DSpecial Courts for offences punishable with more than three years; s.36A(4) — the period of investigation may be extended up to 180 days (and to one year for commercial quantity) on the report of the Public ProsecutorFailure to file the charge-sheet within the period gives an indefeasible right to default bail under s.187 BNSS (s.167(2) CrPC): Rakesh Kumar Paul v. State of Assam (2017) 15 SCC 67; Bikramjit Singh v. State of Punjab (2020) 10 SCC 616
s.71Government power to establish identification, treatment, rehabilitation and social re-integration centres for addictsThe Nasha Mukt Bharat Abhiyaan (2020) and the National Action Plan for Drug Demand Reduction (NAPDDR)
PITNDPS Act, 1988Preventive detention of persons engaged in illicit traffic — up to two yearsThe companion preventive statute, like COFEPOSA for smuggling

35.3 Criticism of the NDPS regime — the points that earn marks

The consumer is criminalised
Although s.27 prescribes the lowest punishment and s.64A offers immunity on volunteering for treatment, the overwhelming majority of NDPS arrests are of consumers and small carriers, not of financiers under s.27A. The kingpin is almost never prosecuted — the classic “fix”. Proposals: the Ministry of Social Justice’s 2021 proposal to decriminalise possession of small quantities for personal consumption and to treat addiction as a health issue; the Portuguese and Netherlands models; and the Supreme Court’s observation in Mohan Lal that the Act’s object is “to curb the menace, not to fill the prisons with addicts”.
Reverse burdens and bail
ss.35, 54 and 37 together create the harshest regime in Indian criminal law after UAPA. The result is mass pre-trial detention: NDPS cases are one of the largest categories of undertrials in Punjab, Delhi and Maharashtra. Noor Aga upheld the reverse burden but insisted the prosecution must first prove the foundational facts and that a stringent standard of proof applies to them.
Procedural safeguards are the real battleground
Because the reverse burden is so heavy, the accused’s only realistic defence is non-compliance with ss.42, 50, 52A and 55. This has produced an enormous jurisprudence and a high acquittal rate on technical grounds — an outcome that satisfies nobody. A better answer is investigative capacity: bodycams, proper sampling, and independent witnesses.
The mixture/quantity problem
Whether the “quantity” means the weight of the pure drug or of the whole mixture was resolved against the accused in Hira Singh v. Union of India (2020) 20 SCC 272, which overruled E. Micheal Raj v. Intelligence Officer, NCB (2008) 5 SCC 161 and held that the entire weight of the mixture including neutral substance is to be counted. The practical effect is that a tiny quantity of heroin mixed in a large quantity of powder becomes a “commercial quantity”, attracting a ten-year minimum and the s.37 bail bar. This is now the single most criticised NDPS holding.
Death penalty
s.31A was a mandatory death sentence until the Bombay High Court read it down in Indian Harm Reduction Network (2011) and Parliament made it discretionary in 2014. The Law Commission’s 262nd Report (2015) recommended abolition of the death penalty for all offences except terrorism and waging war. No execution has taken place under s.31A.
Federal and institutional gaps
Excise is a State subject and the State excise departments, NCB, DRI, customs, police and paramilitary all operate with overlapping jurisdictions; forensic laboratories are chronically backlogged; and de-addiction infrastructure under s.71 is grossly inadequate relative to the AIIMS 2019 estimate of over 77 lakh persons needing help for opioid use.
Real-world examplesPunjab — the “udta Punjab” crisis; the Punjab & Haryana High Court’s monitoring of the drug-trade investigation and the prosecution of a serving police officer in the Jagdish Bhola case (2013) exposed the police–politician–trafficker nexus precisely as the Vohra Committee described. Gujarat coast — repeated multi-thousand-crore heroin seizures, including about 3,000 kg at Mundra port (September 2021), one of the largest seizures in the world. North-East — methamphetamine and heroin from Myanmar financing insurgent groups (“narco-terrorism”). Darknet — NCB operations against LSD networks in Delhi and Karnataka (2020–23) paid for in cryptocurrency, connecting the narcotic trade to cyber crime and money laundering in a single chain.

36. MONEY LAUNDERING I — concept, stages and typologies

Syllabus head C(i)

36.1 The concept

The UN Vienna Convention, 1988, Art. 3(1)(b) — the classic definition “The conversion or transfer of property, knowing that such property is derived from any offence(s), for the purpose of concealing or disguising the illicit origin of the property, or of assisting any person who is involved in such offence(s) to evade the legal consequences of his actions.”

The plain statement: money laundering is the process by which the proceeds of crime are made to appear lawful — “washing” dirty money. The standard commentary’s own formulation for the cyber context is: “a kind of cyber crime in which money is illegally downloaded in transit”; and more generally, “any action taken to conceal, arrange, use or possess the proceeds of any criminal conduct”.

Why it is a separate offence at all. Three reasons, each worth a sentence. (i) It completes the crime. Predicate crime is worthless unless the proceeds can be spent; attacking the laundering attacks the motive, which is “avarice or rapaciousness” (47th Report, characteristic 1). (ii) It is the point of greatest visibility. The drug shipment is invisible; the ₹50 crore that appears in a shell company’s books is not. (iii) It corrupts the legitimate economy. Laundered money distorts asset prices, funds terrorism, and buys political protection — the Vohra Committee nexus in financial form.

36.2 The three stages — draw the flowchart

36.3 The three stages explained

StageWhat happensTechniquesWhere the law bites
1. PLACEMENTThe cash proceeds of crime are introduced into the formal financial system. This is the launderer’s point of greatest risk, because cash is bulky and banks are watchedStructuring / “smurfing” — breaking deposits into amounts below the reporting threshold; use of cash-intensive businesses (restaurants, cinemas, car washes, scrap dealing); money mules; casinos and betting; purchase of bullion, jewellery or bearer instruments; blending with legitimate takingss.12 PMLA — Cash Transaction Reports for cash above ₹10 lakh and all Suspicious Transaction Reports; KYC under the RBI Master Direction; s.269SS and 269ST Income-tax Act (bar on cash receipts of ₹2 lakh or more)
2. LAYERINGA series of complex transactions designed to break the audit trail and separate the money from its sourceWire transfers through multiple jurisdictions; shell companies and nominee directors; trade-based laundering (over- and under-invoicing, phantom shipments, multiple invoicing); back-to-back loans; round-tripping through tax havens; participatory notes; cryptocurrency mixers; hawalas.3 PMLA (“any process or activity connected with the proceeds of crime”); FEMA, 1999; the Benami Transactions (Prohibition) Act, 1988/2016; s.447 Companies Act; the notification of 7 March 2023 bringing virtual digital asset service providers within the PMLA
3. INTEGRATIONThe money returns to the offender as apparently legitimate wealth, which can be spent and invested openlyPurchase of real estate; investment in businesses; false salary or consultancy income; “loans” to oneself from an offshore entity; sale of overvalued art or shares; film financingAttachment and confiscation under ss.5 and 8 PMLA; forfeiture under ss.68A–68Z NDPS; the Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018; the Black Money Act, 2015

36.4 Hawala and the Indian context

Hawala (also hundi) is an informal value-transfer system based entirely on trust: a person hands cash to a broker in one country; the broker instructs a counterpart in another country, who pays out the equivalent in local currency, less a commission; the two brokers settle their accounts later, often by offsetting future transactions or by manipulating trade invoices. No money crosses a border, no record is created and no audit trail exists. It is fast, cheap and anonymous, which makes it attractive both to migrant workers remitting wages and to criminals. In India it is unlawful under s.3 of FEMA, 1999 (dealing in foreign exchange otherwise than through an authorised person) and, where the funds are proceeds of crime, under the PMLA; it is also the principal channel for terror financing (UAPA ss.17 and 40).

The Jain Hawala case — the classic Indian illustrationIn Vineet Narain v. Union of India (1998) 1 SCC 226, diaries seized from the Jain brothers recorded payments running into crores to a large number of politicians and bureaucrats across parties. The CBI’s failure to investigate led to a PIL. The Supreme Court (Verma C.J.) developed the technique of “continuing mandamus”, held that the “Single Directive” requiring prior permission before investigating senior officers was invalid, and issued binding directions to insulate the CBI and the ED — statutory status and supervisory power for the Central Vigilance Commission, a fixed two-year tenure for the Director of the CBI and of the ED, and a selection process through a committee. It is simultaneously a money-laundering case, a political-graft case and a privileged-class-deviance case, and can be cited under all three heads.

37. MONEY LAUNDERING II — the PMLA, 2002, and Vijay Madanlal Choudhary

37.1 Background and object

The Prevention of Money-Laundering Act, 2002 came into force on 1 July 2005. Its Preamble records that it was enacted to prevent money laundering and to provide for confiscation of property derived from or involved in money laundering, and to give effect to the Political Declaration and Global Programme of Action adopted by the UN General Assembly in 1990, the Vienna Convention, 1988, the Palermo Convention, 2000, and the recommendations of the Financial Action Task Force. It has been amended in 2005, 2009, 2012, 2013, 2015, 2018 and 2019 (the last three largely through Finance Acts, a technique challenged as a money-bill circumvention of the Rajya Sabha and referred to a larger Bench in Vijay Madanlal).

37.2 The Act, section by section

SectionContent
s.2(1)(u) — “proceeds of crime”Any property derived or obtained, directly or indirectly, by any person as a result of criminal activity relating to a scheduled offence, or the value of any such property, or, where such property is taken or held outside India, the property equivalent in value held within the country or abroad. The Explanation added in 2019 makes clear that proceeds of crime include property “not only derived or obtained from the scheduled offence but also any property which may directly or indirectly be derived or obtained as a result of any criminal activity relatable to the scheduled offence”
s.2(1)(y) — “scheduled offence”Offences in Part A (no monetary threshold — BNS offences including murder, kidnapping, extortion, forgery and counterfeiting; PCA; NDPS; Arms Act; Wild Life Act; Immoral Traffic Act; Copyright and Trade Marks Acts; UAPA; Companies Act s.447; SEBI Act; Explosives; Antiquities; and many more), Part B (offences under the Customs Act where the value is ₹1 crore or more) and Part C (trans-border crimes)
s.3 — the offence“Whosoever directly or indirectly attempts to indulge or knowingly assists or knowingly is a party or is actually involved in any process or activity connected with the proceeds of crime, including its concealment, possession, acquisition or use, and projecting or claiming it as untainted property, shall be guilty of the offence of money-laundering.” The Explanation inserted in 2019 makes the process “a continuing activity” and clarifies that the listed activities are independent, not cumulative
s.4 — punishmentRigorous imprisonment for 3 to 7 years and fine; where the proceeds relate to an offence under Part A paragraph 2 (the NDPS Act), up to 10 years
s.5 — provisional attachmentA Director or authorised officer may provisionally attach property believed to be proceeds of crime for 180 days, on recording reasons in writing, subject to a report under s.173 CrPC/s.193 BNSS having been forwarded or a complaint filed
s.8 — adjudicationThe Adjudicating Authority issues notice, decides whether the property is involved in money laundering, and may confirm the attachment; confiscation on conclusion of the trial (s.8(5)–(8)); the property vests absolutely in the Central Government
ss.16–18 — survey, search, seizureSearch of premises and persons; s.17 search and seizure; s.18 search of persons; s.20 retention of property; s.21 retention of records
s.19 — arrestPower to arrest on the basis of material in possession and reasons to believe recorded in writing; the grounds of arrest must be informed “as soon as may be”. Pankaj Bansal v. Union of India (2024) 7 SCC 576 — the grounds of arrest must be furnished in writing; Prabir Purkayastha v. State (NCT of Delhi) (2024) applied the same rule to UAPA; Arvind Kejriwal v. Directorate of Enforcement (2024) — the “reasons to believe” must be tested against s.19(1)
s.24 — burden of proofWhere a person is charged with money laundering, the Authority or Court shall presume, unless the contrary is proved, that the proceeds of crime are involved in money laundering; and in any other proceeding it may presume
s.44Trial of the scheduled offence and of the money-laundering offence by the same Special Court; the ED’s complaint is deemed a complaint under s.200 CrPC/s.223 BNSS
s.45 — bailTwin conditions: bail only if the Public Prosecutor has been given an opportunity to oppose and the court is satisfied that there are reasonable grounds for believing the accused is not guilty and is unlikely to commit an offence on bail; a proviso relaxes the rule for persons under 16, women, the sick or infirm, and those accused of laundering less than ₹1 crore. s.45 also makes offences cognizable and non-bailable. History: struck down in Nikesh Tarachand Shah v. Union of India (2018) 11 SCC 1 for arbitrariness (the classification by reference to the punishment for the scheduled offence); re-enacted in 2018 and upheld in Vijay Madanlal
s.50 — summons and statementsThe Director and other authorities have the powers of a civil court to summon persons and to require them to state the truth; statements recorded are admissible, and ED officers are not “police officers”, so ss.25–26 of the Evidence Act do not apply. Contrast Tofan Singh under the NDPS Act
s.63Punishment for false information or failure to give information; s.66 disclosure of information to other agencies
InstitutionsDirectorate of Enforcement (investigation and prosecution); FIU-IND (2004) — receiving and analysing CTRs/STRs; the Adjudicating Authority; the Appellate Tribunal (now merged with the SAFEMA Appellate Tribunal); appeal to the High Court under s.42

37.3 Vijay Madanlal Choudhary v. Union of India — the leading case

Vijay Madanlal Choudhary v. Union of India(2022) SCC OnLine SC 929 (27 July 2022); 3-Judge Bench, A.M. Khanwilkar J.money laundering
Facts
Over 240 petitions were heard together, challenging the constitutional validity of substantially the whole of the PMLA — the definition of “proceeds of crime” and of money laundering in s.3, the powers of attachment (s.5), search and seizure (s.17), search of persons (s.18), arrest (s.19), the presumptions (ss.22 and 24), the admissibility of statements to ED officers (s.50) and the twin conditions for bail (s.45) — as well as the legislative technique of amending the Act through Finance Acts.
Issues
(i) Is money laundering under s.3 a stand-alone offence or is it dependent on the scheduled offence? (ii) Is the width of “proceeds of crime” after the 2019 Explanation constitutional? (iii) Do the ED’s powers under ss.17, 18 and 19 violate Arts. 14, 20(3) and 21? (iv) Is a statement recorded under s.50 hit by Art. 20(3) and by ss.25–26 of the Evidence Act? (v) Are the twin conditions in s.45 valid after Nikesh Tarachand Shah? (vi) Must the ECIR (Enforcement Case Information Report) be supplied to the accused like an FIR? (vii) Was it permissible to amend the PMLA by Finance Acts?
Arguments for the petitioners
(i) The PMLA has been converted from a statute aimed at organised laundering into a general instrument of coercion; the 2019 Explanations expand “proceeds of crime” beyond anything the predicate offence yields. (ii) ED officers exercise police powers but are denied the label, so the accused loses the protection of ss.25–26 Evidence Act and of s.161(2) CrPC — a “constitutional fraud”. (iii) s.50 compels self-incriminating testimony, violating Art. 20(3) and the rule in Nandini Satpathy v. P.L. Dani (1978) 2 SCC 424. (iv) Withholding the ECIR denies the accused the basis of his arrest, contrary to Art. 22(1) and Youth Bar Association v. Union of India (2016). (v) s.45’s twin conditions were struck down in Nikesh Tarachand Shah and their re-enactment without curing the vice is impermissible. (vi) Amendment by Finance Act bypassed the Rajya Sabha.
Arguments for the Union
(i) Money laundering is a distinct and continuing offence against the economy and sovereignty of the nation; India’s FATF obligations require a stringent regime. (ii) The ED is not a police agency: it does not register an FIR, cannot file a police report under s.173 CrPC, and its officers are revenue officials; statements under s.50 are therefore admissible, as under s.108 Customs Act and s.14 Central Excise Act. (iii) The ECIR is an internal departmental document, not a statutory FIR. (iv) The twin conditions, as re-enacted in 2018, cure the defect identified in Nikesh because the classification is no longer tied to the punishment for the scheduled offence. (v) The 47th Report’s logic — socio-economic offences may justly depart from ordinary criminal procedure — applies with full force.
Decision
The Court upheld the Act almost in its entirety:
  • s.3 is wide: any of the listed activities — concealment, possession, acquisition, use, projecting or claiming as untainted — is independently sufficient; “projecting as untainted” is not an additional ingredient in every case. But money laundering is dependent on the predicate offence: if the accused is discharged or acquitted of the scheduled offence, or the proceedings are quashed, there can be no offence of money laundering and the attached property must be released.
  • ss.5, 8, 15, 17, 18, 19, 44 and 45 upheld. Attachment, search, seizure and arrest are constitutionally valid given the inbuilt safeguards of recorded reasons and adjudication.
  • s.50 upheld. ED officers are not police officers; a statement under s.50 is not hit by Art. 20(3) because the person summoned is not, at that stage, an “accused of an offence”.
  • The ECIR is not an FIR and need not be supplied to the accused; it is enough that he is informed of the grounds of arrest at the time of arrest.
  • s.24 (reverse burden) upheld as having a reasonable nexus with the object of the Act.
  • s.45 twin conditions upheld; the defect identified in Nikesh Tarachand Shah was held to have been cured by the 2018 amendment.
  • The Finance Act question — whether the amendments could validly be passed as a money bill — was expressly left open and referred to a larger Bench, following Rojer Mathew v. South Indian Bank (2020) 6 SCC 1.
Principle evolved
Money laundering is an independent, continuing offence against the economic sovereignty of the country, justifying a departure from ordinary criminal procedure — the 47th Report’s thesis at its high-water mark. But the offence has no existence without a scheduled offence, which is the single most important practical protection for the accused.
Aftermath and criticism
A review petition was admitted on 25 August 2022 (Kaul J.) limited to the non-supply of the ECIR and the reverse burden under s.24; the matter is pending. Criticism: the conviction rate under the PMLA is extremely low relative to the number of ECIRs, attachments and arrests — which has led to the charge that the process itself has become the punishment. The Supreme Court itself has since tightened practice: Pankaj Bansal (written grounds of arrest), Manish Sisodia v. Directorate of Enforcement (2024) (prolonged incarceration without trial violates Art. 21; “bail is the rule, jail is the exception” applies to PMLA too), and Prem Prakash v. Union of India (2024) (a statement under s.50 made by a person already in custody in the same case cannot be used against him).

37.4 FATF, the international architecture, and criticism

FATF
The Financial Action Task Force, established by the G-7 in 1989, sets the global standards — the 40 Recommendations on money laundering and the 9 Special Recommendations on terrorist financing (now consolidated as 40). It conducts mutual evaluations and maintains the “grey” and “black” lists. India became a full member in 2010; the FATF’s Mutual Evaluation Report on India (September 2024) placed India in the “regular follow-up” category — the best available outcome — while noting the need to improve prosecution outcomes and to supervise the non-financial sectors (real estate, precious metals, lawyers and accountants).
The other instruments
Vienna Convention, 1988; Palermo Convention, 2000, Art. 6; UN Convention against Corruption, 2003, Art. 23; UNSC Resolutions 1267 and 1373 on terror financing; the Egmont Group of Financial Intelligence Units; the Asia/Pacific Group on Money Laundering; and bilateral Tax Information Exchange Agreements and the OECD Common Reporting Standard for automatic exchange of financial account information.
Indian allied statutes
FEMA, 1999 (civil, not criminal — the deliberate de-criminalisation of FERA); Black Money (Undisclosed Foreign Income and Assets) and Imposition of Tax Act, 2015; Benami Transactions (Prohibition) Act, 1988/2016; Fugitive Economic Offenders Act, 2018 (declaration where the offence involves ₹100 crore or more and the person has left India, with confiscation of even untainted property and a bar on defending civil claims); UAPA ss.17, 40 (terror financing); Companies Act, 2013 ss.89 and 90 (significant beneficial ownership); the notification of 7 March 2023 bringing virtual digital assets within the PMLA.
Criticism — the answer points
(i) Very low conviction rate against a very large number of ECIRs and attachments; the sanction in practice is pre-trial detention and attachment, not conviction. (ii) Width of the Schedule — including offences such as copyright and trade-mark infringement brings ordinary commercial disputes within an extraordinary regime. (iii) Absence of an FIR equivalent and of the safeguards of Chapter XII BNSS. (iv) Selective enforcement and the allegation of use against political opponents — the classic conflict-criminology objection, and a live public controversy. (v) Amendment by money bill. (vi) The attachment of the innocent third party’s property and the delay in adjudication. Balance these against the genuine difficulty of proving laundering and India’s international obligations.

38. PRIVILEGED CLASS DEVIANCE I — B.B. Pande: the concept

Syllabus head C(ii)Suggested reading: B.B. Pande, “Privileged Class Deviance — Nature and Dimensions”, The Other Side of Development (1987)
A note on sources. The DU Case Material does not reproduce Prof. B.B. Pande’s essay; it is listed as a suggested reading. What follows is the standard account of the concept as it is taught, supported by the Indian material in the project folder and by the statutory and case-law apparatus. Where a proposition is Pande’s own, it is attributed; where it is an illustration, it is marked as such.

38.1 What “privileged class deviance” means, and why a new term was needed

Prof. B.B. Pande, of the Faculty of Law, University of Delhi, coined the expression to describe a category of harmful conduct that the existing vocabulary of criminology could not capture. His starting point is that the three available concepts are each inadequate for Indian conditions:

Existing conceptWhat it capturesWhy it is inadequate
CrimeConduct declared punishable by lawMuch of the most damaging elite conduct is not criminalised at all — it is lawful, or it falls in a regulatory grey zone, or it is protected by immunity. A “crime”-based criminology therefore cannot see it
White-collar crime (Sutherland)Crime committed by a person of respectability and high social status in the course of his occupationTied to occupation and to an advanced corporate-industrial economy. It misses elite deviance exercised through political office, caste dominance, bureaucratic discretion, religious authority and social status, which is the characteristic Indian form. As the 47th Report itself notes, the smuggler and the pensioner filing a false return are not white-collar criminals
Socio-economic offence (47th Report)Offences harming the health, welfare or economy of the communityDefined by the interest harmed, not by the status of the actor; and again confined to what the statute book has criminalised
PRIVILEGED CLASS DEVIANCE (Pande)Deviant behaviour of persons who occupy positions of privilege — economic, political, bureaucratic, professional, social or religious — which causes serious harm to society, and which the legal system either does not define as crime at all, or, having defined it, systematically fails to enforceThe advantage of the concept: it is organised around power, not around the statute book. It therefore includes both criminalised elite conduct that goes unpunished and uncriminalised elite conduct that ought to be condemned

38.2 The nature of privileged class deviance — its defining features

  1. It is defined by the STATUS of the actor, not by the nature of the act. The same act — occupying land, evading a rule, using force — is “crime” when done by the powerless and “irregularity”, “lapse”, “policy decision” or “custom” when done by the powerful.
  2. Its principal feature is IMPUNITY. The deviance survives not because it is undetected but because the system contains structural devices that prevent punishment (§40).
  3. It is often LAWFUL or quasi-lawful. The elite participate in making the very rules that define deviance; hence they can define their own conduct out of the criminal law. Pande’s point converges exactly with Quinney’s second proposition (Topic 1 and Topic 2, §28.5): “criminal definitions describe behaviours that conflict with the interests of the segments of society that have the power to shape public policy.”
  4. Its victims are diffuse, poor and unorganised, and frequently do not know that they are victims. This is the 47th Report’s characteristic (3) generalised beyond the economic sphere.
  5. It is SELF-PROTECTIVE and self-reproducing. Elite groups cover for each other (Sutherland’s “common hostility towards law” transposed upward); investigation requires permission from the elite; and adverse findings are buried in unpublished reports (the Vohra Report itself, §26.3).
  6. It sets the pattern for the rest of society. This is the point the standard Indian commentary makes directly: “Pattern setting by the privileged and influential groups of society such as the politicians, industrialists, lawyers, engineers, doctors, bankers, businessmen etc. play[s] an important role in [the] appraisal of criminal behaviour by the persons belonging to [the] under-privileged class. It is common knowledge that Indian society is fairly tolerant about the exploitative tendencies of top-ranking businessmen and industrialists who quite often resort to white collar crimes and other illegal methods for their personal gain.”
  7. It is DEVELOPMENT-RELATED. Pande’s essay appears in a volume titled The Other Side of Development, and that title is the thesis: the processes of planned development — land acquisition, dam construction, mining leases, industrial licensing, subsidies, contracts, public distribution — create the discretionary power that privileged deviance exploits. Development is not merely disturbed by elite deviance; it generates it.
Standard Indian commentary — the “pattern setting” passage, continued “Bribery, corruption, tax evasion, black-mailing and speculation are common among the persons of high social status. It is well known that there is a great divergence between the prescribed codes of ethics for the professional lawyers and their practice… These methods adopted by this prestiged class of society indirectly set [a] pattern for the normal tune of the society.

As regards the politicians who claim themselves to be custodians of society, less said the better. They do not even hesitate to make use of their political influence and contacts with high officials for their personal gain and are at times tempted to indulge in nefarious activities which are offensive and even anti-social in nature. More often than not, they resort to corrupt practices for their personal advantage. At times they also seek the help of notorious offenders and anti-social elements to accomplish their political ends. Politicians very often violate the codes and ethics of their party, particularly at the time of polls… Obviously, such conduct on the part of political leaders has an ADVERSE EFFECT ON YOUNGSTERS who tend to follow the same course of conduct to achieve success in their pursuits.”
The theoretical placement — say this expressly. Privileged class deviance is the Indian synthesis of four strands from Topic 2: Sutherland (status and differential implementation); Sellin, Vold, Turk and Quinney (the conflict perspective — who has the power to define and to enforce); Bonger (the economic structure that generates elite egoism); and Chambliss and Seidman (“the more economically and politically powerful a group is, the less likely it is that its members will be dealt with by the legal system for violating the law”). Pande’s contribution is to make the concept fit a post-colonial, developmental, caste-stratified society rather than an advanced industrial one.

39. PRIVILEGED CLASS DEVIANCE II — the dimensions

Pande’s essay is titled “Nature and Dimensions”, and it is the dimensions that make the concept usable. The following classification, with Indian illustrations, is what an answer should reproduce.

39.1 Political deviance

Forms
Corruption in the award of contracts, licences, leases and permits; misuse of discretionary funds and quotas; political graft (§25.4) — the use of criminals for electoral purposes and the reverse entry of criminals into legislatures; defection and horse-trading; electoral offences — booth capturing (s.169 BNS, old s.171E–171F IPC; s.135A RP Act), bribery and undue influence (ss.170–172 BNS); communal mobilisation and hate speech; abuse of emergency and preventive-detention powers; opaque political funding.
Indian illustrations
The Vohra Committee finding of a politician–bureaucrat–mafia nexus amounting to a “parallel government”; Bofors; the Jain Hawala diaries (Vineet Narain); 2G spectrum and Coalgate; the fodder scam; Vyapam; the Commonwealth Games (2010) contracts; and the ADR finding that roughly 46% of members of the 18th Lok Sabha (2024) declared criminal cases, about 31% serious ones.
The legal response and its limits
Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988 (amended 2018); Lokpal and Lokayuktas Act, 2013; RTI Act, 2005; Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014 (passed but substantially unoperationalised — a major criticism); Lily Thomas; ADR and Public Interest Foundation; the Supreme Court’s special courts for MPs and MLAs. On funding: Association for Democratic Reforms v. Union of India (2024) 5 SCC 1 struck down the Electoral Bonds Scheme as violative of Art. 19(1)(a), holding that the voter’s right to information about political funding outweighed the donor’s claim to privacy — a landmark and highly citable instance of the judiciary dismantling a legal device that protected privileged-class interests.

39.2 Bureaucratic and administrative deviance

Forms
Bribery and extortion at the point of discretion (licences, permits, clearances, mutations, pensions, transfers, postings); “speed money”; collusive procurement; falsification of records; non-feasance — the deliberate failure to act (the most invisible and most damaging form); custodial violence and fake encounters by the police; diversion of welfare entitlements (PDS, MGNREGA, mid-day meal); harassment of the poor under petty regulatory laws.
Indian illustrations
The CBI’s transport department racket in Delhi (22 August 1995) — six Motor Vehicle Inspectors and four touts caught dividing a day’s bribe of ₹1.27 lakh, with assets worth ₹1.5 crore recovered; PDS diversion; the Sivakasi and mining cases; custodial deaths — NCRB and NHRC data, and the Sathankulam case (2020).
The protective devices
s.218 BNSS (old s.197 CrPC) — no court shall take cognizance of an offence alleged to have been committed by a public servant while acting in the discharge of official duty except with the previous sanction of the appropriate Government; s.17A PCA (inserted 2018) — no police officer shall conduct any enquiry or investigation into an offence relating to a recommendation made or decision taken by a public servant in the discharge of official functions without prior approval; Art. 311 — the two-stage protection in dismissal; departmental inquiries that outlast the officer’s service; and the long delays in sanction, which the Supreme Court sought to control in Vineet Narain and Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI (2014) 8 SCC 682, which struck down s.6A of the DSPE Act (prior approval to investigate officers of the rank of Joint Secretary and above) as violative of Art. 14 — “the law does not classify offenders differently for treatment thereunder, including investigation of offences and prosecution for offences, according to their status in life.” That sentence is the single best judicial refutation of privileged class deviance in Indian law. Quote it.

39.3 Corporate and economic deviance

Forms
Tax evasion and aggressive avoidance; bank fraud and wilful default; accounting fraud; insider trading and market manipulation; cartelisation; violation of environmental, labour and safety norms; sale of unsafe products; misleading advertisement; land-grabbing and benami holdings; unpaid wages and denial of statutory benefits; industrial disaster.
Indian illustrations
Bhopal (1984) — thousands dead, a maximum sentence of two years under s.304-A; Harshad Mehta, Ketan Parekh, Satyam, NSEL, PNB/Nirav Modi, IL&FS, ABG Shipyard; the Uphaar cinema fire (1997) and the long dilution of liability; illegal mining in Goa, Karnataka and Odisha; contaminated cough syrup exports (2022–23).
The protective devices
The corporate veil and the difficulty of attributing mens rea (answered in Standard Chartered Bank (2005) and Iridium (2011)); limited liability; compounding (s.441 Companies Act; s.279(2) Income-tax Act; s.137(3) Customs Act; s.138 CGST Act); settlement without admission of guilt (SEBI Settlement Regulations, 2018); adjudication instead of prosecution; the Jan Vishwas Act, 2023, which decriminalised 183 provisions across 42 central Acts; the sheer cost and duration of litigation; and — as the standard commentary puts it — the fact that these offences “are committed generally by influential persons who are shrewd enough to resist the efforts of law enforcement against them.”

39.4 Professional deviance

Set out at §23.4 in detail. The structural feature to emphasise here is self-regulation: doctors are disciplined by the National Medical Commission, advocates by the Bar Councils under ss.35–37 of the Advocates Act, chartered accountants by the ICAI and the NFRA, and academics by their own universities. Peer bodies adjudicating on peers produce very low rates of serious sanction. Add the standard commentary’s striking differential-association point about lawyers: “since the criminals often remain in close contact with their counsel, the former are often influenced by the latter’s behavioural pattern and thus tend to learn unethical practices”, and its observation on medical certificates: the practice of procuring false sickness certificates “though a white collar crime, has proved a boon and a workable alternative to employees who have difficulty in obtaining leave from the employers” — an unusually candid statement of how elite deviance becomes normalised.

39.5 Social, caste, religious and patriarchal deviance — the distinctively Indian dimension

This is the dimension that Sutherland’s concept cannot reach at all, and it is the strongest justification for Pande’s new term. Here the privilege is not economic or official but ascriptive.

FormIllustrationLaw, and why it under-performs
Caste dominanceUntouchability; denial of entry, water and burial grounds; social and economic boycott; atrocity and massacre — the Ranvir Sena killing of 61 Dalits at Laxmanpur Bathe (2 December 1997); manual scavenging and sewer deathsArt. 17; Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955; SC/ST (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989 ss.3, 4 (wilful neglect by a public servant), 14 (special courts), 15A (rights of victims and witnesses), 18 (no anticipatory bail — and see Subhash Kashinath Mahajan v. State of Maharashtra (2018) 6 SCC 454, the 2018 Amendment restoring s.18, and Prathvi Raj Chauhan v. Union of India (2020) 4 SCC 727 upholding it); Prohibition of Employment as Manual Scavengers Act, 2013. Why it under-performs: the investigating officer, the prosecutor and often the judge belong to the dominant group; acquittal rates are high; Sukanya Shantha v. Union of India (2024) showed caste-based labour written into eleven State prison manuals
Khap and community authorityDecrees against inter-caste and sagotra marriage; ostracism; honour killingLata Singh v. State of U.P. (2006) 5 SCC 475; Shakti Vahini v. Union of India (2018) 7 SCC 192 — preventive, remedial and punitive directions, and the declaration that a khap panchayat has no authority to interfere with the choice of an adult; s.103(2) BNS (mob lynching); Law Commission’s 242nd Report (2012) proposing a Prohibition of Unlawful Assembly (Interference with the Freedom of Matrimonial Alliances) Bill — still not enacted
Patriarchal privilegeDowry demand and dowry death; domestic violence; denial of inheritance; sexual harassment at the workplace; witch-hunting of widows and single women to seize landss.80, 85–86 BNS; Dowry Prohibition Act, 1961; PWDVA, 2005; POSH Act, 2013 (following Vishaka); Hindu Succession (Amendment) Act, 2005 and Vineeta Sharma v. Rakesh Sharma (2020) 9 SCC 1; the State witch-hunting Acts (Bihar 1999, Jharkhand 2001, Chhattisgarh 2005, Odisha 2013, Rajasthan 2015, Assam 2015)
Religious and institutional authorityMisappropriation in temple, waqf and church trusts; sexual abuse by persons in spiritual authority; superstition-based exploitation and human sacrifices.74–79 BNS; POCSO s.5(f) (aggravated assault by a person in a position of trust or authority) — see the conviction of a bishop and of self-styled godmen; State endowment Acts; the Maharashtra Prevention and Eradication of Human Sacrifice and other Inhuman, Evil and Aghori Practices and Black Magic Act, 2013 and the Karnataka Act of 2017
Landlord and creditor privilegeBonded labour; illegal eviction; usurious lending; land alienation from tribalsArt. 23; Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act, 1976 (Bandhua Mukti Morcha); State Debt Relief Acts; PESA 1996 and the Forest Rights Act, 2006; the Fifth Schedule — and Samatha v. State of A.P. (1997) 8 SCC 191
Real-world example to close the sectionSukanya Shantha v. Union of India (2024). A journalist’s investigation revealed that the prison manuals of at least eleven States allocated work inside prison by caste — sweeping and scavenging to prisoners from Scheduled Castes and denotified tribes, cooking to “higher” castes — and segregated barracks accordingly. The Supreme Court struck the provisions down as violating Arts. 14, 15, 17, 21 and 23, ordered all States to revise their manuals within three months, directed deletion of the caste column from prisoner registers, and read down the “habitual offender” provisions so far as they target denotified tribes, observing that “discrimination against the marginalised castes cannot continue behind the prison walls”. No better illustration exists of privileged class deviance: the discrimination was not concealed — it was printed in the State’s own rule book and had survived unnoticed for decades, because those subjected to it had no voice.

40. PRIVILEGED CLASS DEVIANCE III — why it escapes: the architecture of impunity

The most examinable part of this head is not the catalogue of forms but the mechanism. Organise it in five stages, following the life of a case.

40.1 Stage 1 — DEFINITION: the conduct is never made criminal

  • Lawful but harmful conduct. Tax avoidance (as distinct from evasion); “policy decisions” allocating natural resources below value; opaque political funding; misleading but non-actionable advertising; regulatory arbitrage.
  • Deliberate de-criminalisation. The Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Act, 2023 converted 183 criminal provisions across 42 central Acts into civil penalties, in the interest of “ease of doing business”. Whatever its merits, its effect is that conduct by business that was criminal is now merely penalised — the exact inverse of the 47th Report’s prescription that these offences “require to be put down with a heavy hand”. This is the single best contemporary illustration of definitional privilege, and it should be argued both ways.
  • Exemptions written into the offence. The family-enterprise exception in s.3 of the child-labour Act; the “adequate procedures” defence for commercial organisations in s.9(3) PCA; thresholds below which conduct is not an offence.
  • Marital rape. Exception 2 to s.375 IPC, retained as the exception to s.63 BNS — the clearest surviving instance of a status-based immunity in Indian substantive criminal law; the split verdict in RIT Foundation v. Union of India (Delhi HC, 2022) and the pending Supreme Court proceedings.

40.2 Stage 2 — DETECTION and INVESTIGATION: permission is required

DeviceProvisionEffect
Absolute immunity of the Head of StateArt. 361 — the President and Governors are not answerable to any court for the exercise of their powers; no criminal proceedings whatsoever shall be instituted or continued against them during their termComplete bar during tenure; see Rameshwar Prasad v. Union of India (2006) 2 SCC 1 on the limits of the protection
Parliamentary privilegeArts. 105 and 194 — freedom of speech in the House; no liability for anything said or any vote givenP.V. Narasimha Rao v. State (CBI) (1998) 4 SCC 626 had held that an MP who took a bribe and voted was immune, while one who took a bribe and did not vote was not — an absurdity and the most notorious Indian instance of privileged immunity. Overruled by a seven-Judge Bench in Sita Soren v. Union of India (2024) 5 SCC 629: “bribery is not rendered immune under Article 105(2) or 194(2)… corruption and bribery by members of the legislature erode probity in public life.” This is the single most important recent case on privileged class deviance and MUST be cited.
Prior approval to investigate a public servants.17A PCA, 1988 (inserted 2018)No enquiry or investigation into any offence “relatable to any recommendation made or decision taken by [a] public servant in the discharge of his official functions” without the prior approval of the competent authority. Compare s.6A DSPE Act, struck down in Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI (2014) 8 SCC 682
Sanction to prosecutes.218 BNSS (s.197 CrPC); s.19 PCA; s.6 of the Commission of Sati Act; s.140 of the Railways Act etc.Delay in granting sanction has killed innumerable prosecutions. Vineet Narain directed a three-month outer limit; s.218(4)–(6) BNSS now provides a 120-day deemed-sanction rule for specified sexual offences and imposes timelines
Consent for the CBI to enter a States.6 DSPE Act, 1946Several States have withdrawn general consent — a federal device that can operate as protection for State-level elites. Fertico Marketing v. CBI (2021) considered the consequences of investigation without consent
Institutional control of the investigatorAppointment, tenure and transfer of the CBI and ED DirectorsVineet Narain (1998) — two-year minimum tenure, CVC supervision; Common Cause v. Union of India (2021) and (2023) on the successive extensions of the ED Director’s tenure, held impermissible beyond the statutory scheme

40.3 Stage 3 — PROSECUTION and TRIAL: the case is diluted or delayed

  • Charge dilution. Keshub Mahindra — s.304 Part II reduced to s.304-A, capping the sentence at two years for the worst industrial disaster in history.
  • Compounding, settlement and adjudication (§22, §39.3) — an exit route available only to the sophisticated offender (Turk).
  • Delay. The average corruption or economic-offence trial runs for a decade or more. The special courts for MPs and MLAs were created in Ashwini Kumar Upadhyay precisely because of this.
  • Hostile witnesses and the “fix” (Sutherland, §24.2). Hence the significance of the Witness Protection Scheme, 2018, approved in Mahender Chawla, and s.398 BNSS.
  • Superior legal representation, procedural stays and interlocutory appeals — a resource asymmetry that no rule of law can equalise. Balance this with Art. 39A and the Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987.
  • Inquiry commissions as deflection. The standard commentary’s own observation: “there has been a general tendency on the part of political parties in power to set up commissions of inquiry, specially if big-wigs are involved, TO SAVE THEM RATHER THAN TO PUNISH THE GUILTY. The inquiry drags on for years and finally the guilty escapes scot-free due to their political links.” Examples given: the Commission to probe the Tehelka tapes on corruption in defence deals, “appointed in January 2003 but withdrawn on 31 October 2004 and its proceedings… quashed”; and the multi-crore fake stamp-paper scam masterminded by Abdul Karim Telgi, “allegedly involving three or four top politicians… probed since 2003 but with no substantial results”.

40.4 Stage 4 — SENTENCE: the punishment does not match the harm

Set the comparison out explicitly, because it is the most persuasive thing you can write:

ConductHarmMaximum / actual punishment
Theft of a mobile phone — s.303(2) BNSOne victim, a few thousand rupees3 years; commonly months of undertrial custody for want of surety
Bhopal gas leak — s.304-A IPCThousands dead; over five lakh injured; three generations affected2 years maximum; convictions in 2010 with bail granted the same day
Possession of a small quantity of a narcotic — s.20 NDPSSelf-harm1 year — but with the s.37 bail regime if the “mixture” rule applies
Adulteration of food causing death — s.59 FSSADeath of consumersLife and fine up to ₹10 lakh — but prosecutions are rare and convictions rarer
Bank fraud of ₹13,500 crorePublic money; systemic riskExtradition proceedings pending for years; the operative sanction is attachment and confiscation, not imprisonment

Add the countervailing Indian jurisprudence, because a balanced answer must: State of Gujarat v. Mohanlal Jitamalji Porwal (1987) 2 SCC 364 — “the entire community is aggrieved if the economic offenders who ruin the economy of the State are not brought to book… A murder may be committed in the heat of moment upon passions being aroused. An economic offence is committed with cool calculation and deliberate design with an eye on personal profit regardless of the consequence to the community.” Y.S. Jagan Mohan Reddy v. CBI (2013) 7 SCC 439 — “economic offences constitute a class apart and need to be visited with a different approach in the matter of bail”. Nimmagadda Prasad v. CBI (2013) 7 SCC 466 — economic offences “affect the economic fabric of society”. Ram Narain Popli v. CBI (2003) 3 SCC 641 on the gravity of bank fraud. These are the judicial answer to privileged class deviance — and yet Satender Kumar Antil v. CBI (2022) 10 SCC 51 and Manish Sisodia (2024) hold that even in economic offences bail cannot be denied indefinitely. The tension between these two lines is itself an excellent essay.

40.5 Stage 5 — LEGITIMATION: the conduct is redescribed

The final and subtlest mechanism is linguistic. Elite deviance is renamed: a bribe becomes a “facilitation payment”; a fraudulent loan becomes a “non-performing asset”; an unlawful allocation becomes a “policy decision” which courts decline to review; a fatal industrial failure becomes an “accident”; caste violence becomes a “clash”; and corporate crime becomes “non-compliance”. This is precisely the constitutive criminologists’ point (Henry and Milovanovic) about the power of discourse, and Sykes and Matza’s techniques of neutralisation operating at the level of the whole society rather than the individual offender.

40.6 Consequences — and the remedies

Consequences
(i) Loss of legitimacy of the legal system — “the law is only for the poor”, which erodes voluntary compliance; (ii) Durkheimian anomie — when elites break rules with impunity, the normative order weakens for everyone; (iii) pattern setting and the transmission of deviance downward; (iv) economic loss and distorted development; (v) entrenchment of inequality — the poor bear both the harm and the punishment; (vi) the prison becomes, as Prison Statistics India shows, a repository of the poor, the illiterate and the socially marginalised.
Remedies — legal
Abolish or narrow the sanction requirements (s.218 BNSS, s.17A PCA); give real effect to the Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014; a strong Lokpal and Lokayuktas; statutory time limits for investigation and trial in corruption and economic cases; corporate criminal liability with debarment and disgorgement geared to profit (the 47th Report’s recommendation); mandatory asset disclosure; and reform of political funding after the electoral-bonds judgment.
Remedies — institutional
Implement the Prakash Singh police-reform directions; insulate the CBI and ED through fixed tenure and independent selection (Vineet Narain); strengthen the SFIO, FIU-IND and the NFRA; independent prosecution directorates as the 47th Report recommended; and a statutory victim-compensation regime for socio-economic harm.
Remedies — social
Pande’s own emphasis: the problem is not primarily one of drafting but of power. Remedies therefore include a free press and the RTI Act; strong civil-society and public-interest litigation; electoral and party-funding transparency; and — Bonger’s point — the reduction of the inequality that creates the privileged class in the first place.
Model conclusion for a privileged-class-deviance question. “Privileged class deviance is not an aberration in the Indian criminal justice system; it is a structural feature of it. The powerful participate in defining what is criminal, control the machinery that decides whether to investigate, can purchase delay and dilution, and can rename their conduct so that it ceases to appear deviant at all. The result is the paradox with which this topic began: the offences that fill India’s prisons are not the offences that do India the most harm. The remedy lies partly in law — removing sanction requirements, calibrating punishment to profit, protecting whistle-blowers and witnesses — but principally in the reduction of the concentrations of power that make impunity possible. As the Supreme Court put it in Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI, ‘the law does not classify offenders differently for treatment thereunder… according to their status in life’. The task of criminology is to record how far the practice of the law falls short of that promise.”

41. MASTER COMPARISON TABLES — the whole topic on two pages
