41.2 The 47th Report’s six characteristics applied to each head — the answer template

CharacteristicCyber fraudTraffickingNarcotic tradeMoney launderingPrivileged class deviance
1. Motive — avarice✔ 64.8% of cases are for fraud✔ “generating millions”✔ “huge profit in illicit trafficking”✔ by definition✔ gain, power or status
2. Non-emotional✔ offender never meets the victim✖ — there is prolonged, coercive personal contact✔ at the trafficking level; ✖ at the street level✔✔
3. Victim — State or a section of the public✔ and individual victims too✖ — there is an identifiable individual victim, which is why trafficking is placed in the chapter on offences against the human body✔ public health; also the individual addict✔ the economy✔ diffuse, poor, unorganised
4. Fraud, not force✔✖ — force, coercion and abduction are express means✖ — violence enforces the market✔✔ mostly, but includes caste and patriarchal violence
5. Deliberate and wilful✔✔✔✔✔
6. Twofold interest✔ preservation of wealth and data integrity✔ preservation of human dignity and national human resources✔ preservation of health✔ augmentation and preservation of the economy✔ both
Notice what the table shows. Trafficking fits the 47th Report’s template least well — it has an individual victim, it uses force, and the emotional element is intense. That is a genuine analytical point: trafficking is placed under “social and economic offences” in the syllabus because of its organised, commercial and transnational character, not because it fits the Law Commission’s 1972 definition. Saying so demonstrates independent analysis rather than recitation.

41.3 The theories of Topic 2 applied to the crimes of Topic 3

TheoryWhat it explains in Topic 3Where it fails
Classical / rational choice (Beccaria, Bentham, Becker)Socio-economic and organised crime almost perfectly: the offender is a calculator, the motive is “avarice”, the act is “deliberate and wilful”. Hence the correct sanctions are certainty of detection, disgorgement and fines geared to the profitCannot explain addiction, or crimes of caste and patriarchal dominance where the “profit” is status
PositivismVery little. Its search for the abnormal offender is defeated by the socio-economic offender, who is conspicuously normal, respectable and successful — this was Sutherland’s point in 1939—
Sutherland — differential association and white-collar crimeThe central theory of Topic 3. Explains white-collar crime (§23), organised crime (the 2017 DU model answer, §24.2), professional deviance (the lawyer’s influence on his client), and the concept of differential implementation that underlies privileged class devianceDoes not by itself explain why the law is made and enforced as it is — for that you need conflict theory
Merton — strain and innovationBoth ends of the social scale: the daily wager who steals food, and the corporation that innovates around a regulation to reach the culturally prescribed goal of success. Examination scams and dowry are pure Mertonian innovationAssumes value consensus; cannot explain non-utilitarian crime
Bonger — economic theoryTrafficking (“poverty, under-development, unemployment”), bonded and child labour, drug cultivation, and the “egoism” of the capitalist economy that the Santhanam Committee described as producing “mass society with a small controlling elite”Goring’s objection: the rich also offend, and out of greed rather than need — which is exactly Topic 3’s point
Conflict theory (Sellin, Vold, Turk, Quinney, Chambliss)Privileged class deviance in its entirety; the design of the statute book; Turk’s “sophistication” variable explaining regulatory exits; Chambliss’s vagrancy analysis explaining Harsh ManderWeak as a theory of individual behaviour; better as a theory of criminal law
Routine activity / opportunity (Cohen & Felson)Cyber crime almost exactly: a motivated offender, a suitable target and the absence of a capable guardian, converging in a medium where distance is irrelevantIgnores motivation and structural causes
Labelling and victimologyWhy the trafficked woman is prosecuted under s.8 ITPA and the addict under s.27 NDPS, while the trafficker and the financier are not—

42. CASE LAW — detailed briefs

The syllabus prescribes no cases for Topic 3. The briefs below are the cases through which the concepts of this topic are actually litigated in India; several have already been briefed in the body (Shama Bai §32.1; Vishal Jeet, Gaurav Jain, Budhadev Karmaskar, Prajwala §32.3; Satyam Infoway, Rediff, Yahoo! §29.3; Vijay Madanlal Choudhary §37.3). What follows completes the set.
Keshub Mahindra v. State of Madhya Pradesh(1996) 6 SCC 129 — the Bhopal casesocio-economic offence
Facts
On the night of 2–3 December 1984 approximately 40 tonnes of methyl isocyanate escaped from the Union Carbide India Ltd. pesticide plant at Bhopal. Official figures record several thousand immediate deaths and more than five lakh persons injured; unofficial estimates are far higher. The CBI charged the chairman of UCIL and other officials under s.304 Part II IPC (culpable homicide not amounting to murder) and ss.324, 326, 429 read with s.35 IPC. The accused sought discharge.
Issues
(i) Whether, on the material collected, a charge under s.304 Part II could be framed — that is, whether the accused had knowledge that their acts were likely to cause death. (ii) Or whether the material disclosed no more than a rash or negligent act under s.304-A IPC.
Arguments for the prosecution
(i) The accused knew MIC to be an ultra-hazardous substance; safety systems (the refrigeration unit, the flare tower, the vent gas scrubber) were known to be inoperative or inadequate; internal audits and press reports had warned of the danger; the plant was under-staffed and under-maintained as part of a cost-cutting programme. (ii) That knowledge satisfied the second limb of s.299 IPC and warranted a charge under s.304 Part II. (iii) The gravity of the consequences required the law to respond proportionately.
Arguments for the accused
(i) There was no material to show that any accused knew that the acts attributed to them were likely to cause death; at highest there was negligence in operation and maintenance. (ii) Criminal liability cannot be founded on hindsight; the disaster was the result of a combination of causes, including alleged sabotage. (iii) The accused were not in day-to-day control of plant operations; vicarious criminal liability is unknown to the IPC.
Decision
The Supreme Court held that the material did not support a charge under s.304 Part II and directed that the accused be tried under s.304-A read with ss.336, 337 and 338 IPC. In June 2010 the trial court convicted seven accused under s.304-A and imposed the statutory maximum of two years and a fine of ₹1 lakh each; all were released on bail the same day. A CBI curative petition to restore the s.304 Part II charge was dismissed in 2011, and the Union’s curative petition seeking enhanced compensation over and above the 1989 settlement of US $470 million (approved in Union Carbide Corporation v. Union of India (1991) 4 SCC 584) was dismissed in March 2023.
Principle evolved
Doctrinally, the threshold of “knowledge” under s.299 IPC. Criminologically, the case is the canonical Indian demonstration of the 47th Report’s thesis and of Sutherland’s differential implementation: the worst industrial disaster in history produced a maximum sentence lower than that for ordinary theft (s.303(2) BNS: three years), and the substantive response was a civil settlement rather than a penal sanction. Contrast M.C. Mehta (below), and note the legislative response — the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, the Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991 and the NGT Act, 2010.
M.C. Mehta v. Union of India(1987) 1 SCC 395 — the Oleum Gas Leak caseabsolute liability
Facts
On 4 and 6 December 1985, oleum gas escaped from a unit of Shriram Foods and Fertiliser Industries in a densely populated part of Delhi, affecting a number of persons; an advocate practising in the Tis Hazari Courts was said to have died. A writ petition under Art. 32 had already been filed seeking closure of the hazardous units.
Issues
(i) Whether Art. 32 is available against a private corporation engaged in a hazardous industry. (ii) What is the measure of liability of an enterprise carrying on a hazardous activity — does the rule in Rylands v. Fletcher, with its exceptions, apply?
Arguments
Petitioner: hazardous enterprises operating in densely populated areas owe the public an absolute duty; the nineteenth-century exceptions to strict liability are wholly unsuited to modern industrial hazards. Respondent: the company is not “State” under Art. 12 and is not amenable to Art. 32; liability is governed by the common law of strict liability, with its recognised exceptions.
Decision
Bhagwati C.J. laid down the rule of ABSOLUTE LIABILITY: “where an enterprise is engaged in a hazardous or inherently dangerous activity and harm results to anyone on account of an accident in the operation of such hazardous or inherently dangerous activity… the enterprise is strictly and absolutely liable to compensate all those who are affected by the accident and such liability is NOT SUBJECT TO ANY OF THE EXCEPTIONS which operate vis-à-vis the tortious principle of strict liability under the rule in Rylands v. Fletcher.” The Court further held that the measure of compensation must be correlated to the magnitude and capacity of the enterprise, so that it has a deterrent effect.
Principle evolved
Absolute liability, and the “deep pocket” measure of damages. This is the judicial realisation, fifteen years later, of the 47th Report’s para 3.13 description of socio-economic offending as “extra-hazardous activity” requiring a departure from ordinary rules. Followed in Indian Council for Enviro-Legal Action v. Union of India (1996) 3 SCC 212 (polluter pays) and Vellore Citizens’ Welfare Forum v. Union of India (1996) 5 SCC 647 (precautionary principle).
Ishar Das v. State of Punjab(1973) 2 SCC 6547th Report
Facts
The appellant was convicted of selling adulterated milk under the Prevention of Food Adulteration Act, 1954 and sought the benefit of the Probation of Offenders Act, 1958.
Issue
Whether the Probation of Offenders Act, 1958 applies to offences under the Prevention of Food Adulteration Act, which prescribes a minimum sentence.
Arguments
Appellant: s.4 of the Probation Act is a later general law with an overriding character (s.18 apart) and applies to all offences not punishable with death or life imprisonment; food adulteration is a technical offence. State: the PFA Act prescribes a minimum sentence precisely to exclude judicial leniency; extending probation would defeat the object of a public-health statute.
Decision
The Supreme Court held that the Probation Act is not automatically excluded, but observed — expressly noting that the 47th Report of the Law Commission had recommended the exclusion of the Probation Act from socio-economic offences — that the Prevention of Food Adulteration Act “aims at uprooting and curbing such evils and safeguarding public health, and the courts should NOT LIGHTLY give the benefit of the provisions of the Probation of Offenders Act in such cases”.
Principle evolved
Judicial recognition of the 47th Report’s prescription: socio-economic offences are a class apart in sentencing. See also Prem Ballab v. State (Delhi Admn.) (1977) 1 SCC 173 and s.18 of the Probation of Offenders Act, 1958.
Standard Chartered Bank v. Directorate of Enforcement(2005) 4 SCC 530 (Constitution Bench, 3:2)corporate liability
Facts
The bank faced adjudication and prosecution under the Foreign Exchange Regulation Act, 1973, s.56 of which prescribed a mandatory minimum sentence of imprisonment together with fine for contraventions above a threshold. The question was whether a company, which cannot be imprisoned, could be prosecuted at all.
Issues
(i) Can a corporation be prosecuted for an offence for which the statute prescribes a mandatory sentence of imprisonment and fine? (ii) If so, may the court impose fine alone?
Arguments for the bank
Relying on Assistant Commissioner, Assessment-II v. Velliappa Textiles (2003) 11 SCC 405, that a court cannot impose a sentence the statute does not permit; to impose fine alone would be to rewrite the statute; therefore a company simply cannot be prosecuted for such an offence.
Arguments for the Enforcement Directorate
That such a construction would grant blanket immunity to every corporation for the gravest economic offences — an absurd result; the corporate personality cannot be a shield; the court should adopt a purposive construction and impose the fine, the imprisonment being inapplicable.
Decision
By 3:2 the Constitution Bench overruled Velliappa Textiles and held that a company can be prosecuted and punished for an offence involving a mandatory sentence of imprisonment, and that the court may impose fine alone. “There is no immunity to the companies from prosecution merely because the prosecution is in respect of offences for which the punishment prescribed is mandatory imprisonment.” The Court referred expressly to the 47th Report’s recommendation that, where the offender is a corporation, the court should be empowered to impose fine only — a recommendation Parliament had never enacted.
Principle evolved
Corporate criminal liability in India. Completed by Iridium India Telecom Ltd. v. Motorola Inc. (2011) 1 SCC 74, which held that a corporation can be prosecuted even for offences requiring mens rea, the mental state of the “directing mind and will” being attributed to it (the alter-ego doctrine). Contrast Sunil Bharti Mittal v. CBI (2015) 4 SCC 609 — the converse attribution (from company to director) requires either active role plus mens rea or a statutory provision.
Shreya Singhal v. Union of India(2015) 5 SCC 1cyber crime
Facts
Two young women were arrested in Maharashtra in 2012 — one for a Facebook post questioning the shutdown of Mumbai on the death of a political leader, the other for “liking” it. A series of similar arrests followed. Writ petitions challenged s.66A of the IT Act (sending “grossly offensive” or “menacing” messages, or messages causing “annoyance, inconvenience, danger, obstruction, insult, injury, criminal intimidation, enmity, hatred or ill will”), s.69A (blocking) and s.79 with the Intermediary Guidelines Rules, 2011.
Issues
(i) Is s.66A a reasonable restriction on free speech under Art. 19(2)? (ii) Is it unconstitutionally vague and overbroad? (iii) Are ss.69A and 79(3)(b) valid?
Arguments for the petitioners
(i) The expressions used in s.66A have no defined meaning and are incapable of objective determination; what annoys one person does not annoy another. (ii) None of the terms corresponds to any head in Art. 19(2); “annoyance” and “inconvenience” are not grounds on which speech may be restricted. (iii) The section has a severe chilling effect. (iv) It creates an arbitrary distinction between speech on the internet and speech in other media.
Arguments for the Union
(i) There is a presumption of constitutionality; the section can be read down and guidelines issued for its use. (ii) The internet’s reach, anonymity and permanence justify a different standard. (iii) Vagueness is not by itself a ground of invalidity in Indian constitutional law.
Decision
The Supreme Court (Nariman J.) struck down s.66A in its entirety as violative of Art. 19(1)(a) and not saved by Art. 19(2). It distinguished discussion, advocacy and incitement, holding that only incitement may be restricted; held the section vague and overbroad, and rejected the argument that it could be read down. It upheld s.69A and the Blocking Rules, 2009, because they contain procedural safeguards and require reasons in writing; and it read down s.79(3)(b) so that an intermediary is obliged to take down content only on receipt of a court order or a notification by the appropriate Government.
Principle evolved
Free speech online has the same protection as offline; vagueness and overbreadth are independent grounds of invalidity; the chilling effect doctrine enters Indian constitutional law. The continuing scandal: despite the judgment, police across India continued to register cases under the dead s.66A; in PUCL v. Union of India (2021) the Supreme Court expressed “shock”, directed all States to withdraw such prosecutions and to circulate the judgment to every police station — the single best demonstration that the criminal justice system is not a machine for applying law but a set of institutions with their own habits.
State of Tamil Nadu v. Suhas KattiC.C. No. 4680 of 2004, Court of the Additional CMM, Egmore, Chennai; judgment 5 November 2004cyber crime
Facts
The accused, a family friend of the complainant, wished to marry her; she married another man and was later divorced. On his renewed advances being refused, he created a false e-mail account in her name and posted obscene, defamatory and annoying messages about her in a Yahoo message group, together with her telephone number, causing her to receive a stream of harassing calls.
Issues
Whether the accused was the author of the postings, and whether the conduct amounted to offences under s.469 IPC (forgery for the purpose of harming reputation), s.509 IPC (word or gesture intended to insult the modesty of a woman) and s.67 of the IT Act, 2000 (publishing obscene information in electronic form).
Arguments
Prosecution: cyber-café records, the internet protocol logs, the message-group postings and the e-mail headers established authorship; the content was obscene and intended to insult and harass. Defence: the postings could have been made by the complainant’s former husband; electronic documents are inherently unreliable and the chain of custody was not established.
Decision
The Additional Chief Metropolitan Magistrate convicted the accused and sentenced him to two years’ rigorous imprisonment and a fine of ₹500 under s.469 IPC, one year and ₹500 under s.509 IPC, and two years and ₹4,000 under s.67 of the IT Act, the sentences to run concurrently. The judgment was delivered within about seven months of the charge-sheet.
Significance
The first conviction in India under s.67 of the Information Technology Act, 2000. It demonstrated that electronic evidence — logs, headers and cyber-café records — can sustain a conviction, and it is the standard starting point for any discussion of cyber-crime prosecution in India. Its subject matter — online harassment of a woman by a rejected suitor — also anticipates the stalking and voyeurism provisions introduced in 2013 (now ss.77–78 BNS).
Vineet Narain v. Union of India(1998) 1 SCC 226 — the Jain Hawala caseprivileged class deviance
Facts
In 1991, in the course of an investigation into terrorist funding, the CBI seized diaries and notebooks from the Jain brothers recording payments running into crores to a large number of persons identified by initials — subsequently said to include senior politicians of several parties and senior bureaucrats. No effective investigation followed. Journalists filed a public interest petition alleging inertia in the CBI and the Enforcement Directorate because of the status of the persons involved.
Issues
(i) Can the Court compel the investigating agencies to perform their statutory duty? (ii) What institutional arrangements are required to insulate the CBI and the ED from executive influence? (iii) Is the “Single Directive” — an executive instruction requiring prior permission before investigating officers of and above the rank of Joint Secretary — valid?
Arguments for the petitioners
Inaction in the face of material disclosing cognizable offences is a violation of the rule of law and of Art. 14; the failure of the agencies is attributable to the political status of the suspects; institutional reform is required, not merely a direction in the individual case.
Arguments for the Union
Investigation is an executive function in which the Court cannot interfere; the Single Directive protects senior officers from frivolous and motivated complaints and is a matter of administrative policy.
Decision
The Court developed and applied the technique of “CONTINUING MANDAMUS” — monitoring the investigation periodically without pronouncing on the merits. It struck down the Single Directive as invalid, holding that there can be no class of public servant immune from investigation. It issued detailed directions: statutory status and supervisory power for the Central Vigilance Commission over the CBI’s anti-corruption work; a minimum two-year tenure for the Director of the CBI and of the ED; selection through a committee headed by the CVC; time limits for the grant of sanction (three months, extendable by one month where the Attorney-General is consulted); and the creation of a Nodal Agency to coordinate action on the Vohra Committee findings.
Principle evolved
“Be you ever so high, the law is above you.” The rule of law requires that investigative agencies be insulated from the persons they may have to investigate. The decision is the single most important Indian judicial response to privileged class deviance, and is the ancestor of Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI (2014) 8 SCC 682 (s.6A DSPE Act struck down), Prakash Singh (2006) and Common Cause (2021, 2023) on the ED Director’s tenure. Note the outcome, however: the hawala prosecutions themselves collapsed for want of admissible evidence — the diaries were held to be insufficient corroboration — which is itself an illustration of the thesis.
Sita Soren v. Union of India(2024) 5 SCC 629 (7-Judge Bench, 4 March 2024)privileged class deviance
Facts
The appellant, a member of the Jharkhand Legislative Assembly, was alleged to have accepted a bribe to vote for a particular candidate in the 2012 Rajya Sabha election, but in the event voted for another. She claimed immunity under Art. 194(2) of the Constitution, relying on the majority in P.V. Narasimha Rao v. State (CBI) (1998) 4 SCC 626.
Issue
Whether a legislator who accepts a bribe in connection with a vote or speech in the House enjoys immunity from prosecution under Arts. 105(2) and 194(2).
Arguments for the appellant
The words “in respect of anything said or any vote given” are of the widest amplitude; P.V. Narasimha Rao had held that a member who received a bribe and voted was immune; the immunity exists to protect the independence of the legislature and must be construed broadly; stare decisis favours a twenty-six-year-old Constitution Bench ruling.
Arguments for the Union and the CBI
The Narasimha Rao construction produces the absurdity that the member who takes the bribe and performs is immune while the one who takes it and does not perform is prosecuted; the offence of bribery is complete on the acceptance of the illegal gratification and is therefore independent of what is subsequently said or done in the House; immunity attaches to the functioning of the House, not to a personal criminal act.
Decision
A seven-Judge Bench unanimously overruled P.V. Narasimha Rao. Chandrachud C.J. held: “Bribery is not rendered immune under Article 105(2) or the corresponding provision of Article 194 because a member engaging in bribery commits a crime which is NOT ESSENTIAL to the casting of the vote or the ability to decide how the vote should be cast.” The offence “is complete at the point in time when the legislator accepts the bribe”, irrespective of whether the vote is cast as agreed. The Court added that “corruption and bribery of members of the legislature erode the foundation of Indian Parliamentary democracy… [and are] destructive of the aspirational and deliberative ideals of the Constitution”, and held that the same principle applies to voting in Rajya Sabha and Presidential elections.
Principle evolved
Legislative privilege protects the function, not the person. This is the most important recent dismantling of a legal immunity that had operated as pure privileged class deviance, and it should be cited in every answer on that head.
State of Gujarat v. Mohanlal Jitamalji Porwal(1987) 2 SCC 364economic offences
Facts
A prosecution under the Customs Act and the Gold (Control) Act in which the prosecution had been unable to produce the report of the Government analyst, and the trial court refused an application to recall a witness; the accused were acquitted, and the State appealed.
Issue
Whether the court should have permitted the prosecution to fill the lacuna, and more generally how economic offences should be approached.
Decision and principle
Thakkar J. delivered the most frequently quoted Indian passage on economic crime: “The entire community is aggrieved if the economic offenders who ruin the economy of the State are not brought to book. A murder may be committed in the heat of moment upon passions being aroused. AN ECONOMIC OFFENCE IS COMMITTED WITH COOL CALCULATION AND DELIBERATE DESIGN WITH AN EYE ON PERSONAL PROFIT REGARDLESS OF THE CONSEQUENCE TO THE COMMUNITY. A disregard for the interest of the community can be manifested only at the cost of forfeiting the trust and faith of the community in the system to administer justice in an even-handed manner without fear of criticism from the quarters which view white collar crimes with a permissive eye…” The appeal was allowed and the case remitted. Note the direct correspondence with the 47th Report’s characteristics (1) and (5) — avarice, and deliberate and wilful conduct. Followed in Y.S. Jagan Mohan Reddy v. CBI (2013) 7 SCC 439, Nimmagadda Prasad v. CBI (2013) 7 SCC 466 and Gautam Kundu v. Directorate of Enforcement (2015) 16 SCC 1.
Bandhua Mukti Morcha v. Union of India(1984) 3 SCC 161trafficking / bonded labour
Facts
An organisation working for the release of bonded labourers addressed a letter to the Supreme Court stating that a large number of workers in stone quarries in Faridabad district, Haryana were living in inhuman conditions and working as bonded labour, many of them migrants from other States, with no drinking water, no medical facilities, exposure to silicosis and no minimum wages.
Issues
(i) Whether a letter can be treated as a writ petition under Art. 32 (epistolary jurisdiction). (ii) Whether the Court can appoint commissioners to investigate facts. (iii) What is the content of the State’s obligation under Arts. 21, 23 and the Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act, 1976.
Arguments
Petitioner: bonded labour violates Arts. 21 and 23; the State has failed to identify, release and rehabilitate as the 1976 Act requires; the Court must appoint commissioners because the workers cannot themselves approach it. State of Haryana: the workers were free contract labour; the petition was based on hearsay; commissioners’ reports are not evidence and cannot be acted upon.
Decision
Bhagwati J. held that Art. 32 is not confined to the traditional adversarial form; where a person or class is unable to approach the Court by reason of poverty or disability, any public-spirited person may move it, and the Court may appoint commissioners whose reports constitute material on which it may act. On the merits: a worker found to be working for less than the minimum wage is to be presumed a bonded labourer, and the burden lies on the employer to show otherwise. The Court issued twenty-one directions for identification, release, rehabilitation, payment of minimum wages, provision of drinking water, medical facilities, safety equipment and education for children.
Principle evolved
The State’s obligation under Art. 23 is positive and enforceable; the foundation of Indian public interest litigation in labour and trafficking matters. Read with PUDR v. Union of India (1982) 3 SCC 235 (payment below minimum wage is “forced labour” under Art. 23) and Neeraja Chaudhary v. State of M.P. (1984) 3 SCC 243 (release without rehabilitation is meaningless) — a proposition that applies with equal force to trafficking victims.
M.C. Mehta v. State of Tamil Nadu(1996) 6 SCC 756 — the Sivakasi child labour casechild labour
Facts
A fire in a fireworks factory at Sivakasi in 1991 killed a number of workers, including children. A writ petition drew attention to the widespread employment of children in the match and fireworks industry in hazardous conditions.
Issues
What directions should issue to eliminate child labour in hazardous industries and to secure the child’s right to education and development under Arts. 24, 39(e) and (f) and 45?
Decision
The Court directed: (i) offending employers in hazardous industries to pay ₹20,000 per child into a Child Labour Rehabilitation-cum-Welfare Fund; (ii) the appropriate Government to contribute ₹5,000 per child where it cannot provide employment to an adult member of the family; (iii) the income from the corpus to be used for the child, on condition that the child is withdrawn from employment and sent to school; (iv) a survey of child labour within six months; and (v) the constitution of inspectorates and monitoring.
Principle evolved
A concrete, funded remedy rather than a declaration — and the recognition that child labour is an economic problem requiring an economic remedy, which is Bonger’s point and the 47th Report’s “augmentation of the wealth of the country” interest. Read with Art. 24, the Child and Adolescent Labour (Prohibition and Regulation) Act, 1986 (ss.3, 3A, 14), the Right to Education Act, 2009, and Bachpan Bachao Andolan v. Union of India (2011) 5 SCC 1.
Tofan Singh v. State of Tamil Nadu(2021) 4 SCC 1 (3-Judge Bench, 2:1)narcotics
Facts
The appellant was convicted under the NDPS Act substantially on the basis of a confessional statement recorded under s.67 by an officer of the Narcotics Control Bureau. The question of the admissibility of such statements was referred to a larger Bench because of conflicting decisions.
Issues
(i) Are officers invested with powers under s.53 of the NDPS Act “police officers” within the meaning of s.25 of the Evidence Act? (ii) Is a statement recorded under s.67 admissible as a confession?
Arguments for the appellant
Such officers exercise substantially all police powers — search, seizure, arrest, investigation and the filing of a complaint that is treated as a police report; to deny them the label while giving them the powers is to deprive the accused of the protection of ss.25–26 of the Evidence Act and of Art. 20(3); a regime with the reverse burdens of ss.35 and 54 cannot also admit confessions to the investigator.
Arguments for the State
Relying on Kanhaiyalal v. Union of India (2008) 4 SCC 668 and Raj Kumar Karwal v. Union of India (1990) 2 SCC 409, that NDPS officers are not police officers; s.67 is merely an information-gathering power analogous to s.108 of the Customs Act; excluding such statements would cripple enforcement.
Decision
By 2:1 the Court held that officers invested with powers under s.53 are “police officers” within s.25 of the Evidence Act, and that a confessional statement recorded under s.67 is NOT admissible in the trial of an offence under the NDPS Act. Kanhaiyalal and Raj Kumar Karwal were overruled to that extent. Nariman J. emphasised the extraordinary severity of the Act — mandatory minimum sentences, reverse burdens and the s.37 bail bar — as a reason for construing the protective provisions strictly.
Principle evolved
The severity of a socio-economic statute is a reason to enlarge, not to contract, the accused’s procedural protections. Contrast Vijay Madanlal Choudhary, which took the opposite view of s.50 PMLA — the two decisions together are the best available material for an essay on how far the 47th Report’s licence to depart from ordinary criminal procedure should extend.
Hira Singh v. Union of India(2020) 20 SCC 272 (3-Judge Bench)narcotics
Facts and issue
Whether, in determining whether a seizure is of a “small” or “commercial” quantity under the NDPS Act, the court must consider the weight of the pure narcotic content alone, or the entire weight of the mixture including neutral substances.
Arguments
For the accused: E. Micheal Raj v. Intelligence Officer, NCB (2008) 5 SCC 161 had held that only the actual content of the narcotic drug is relevant; otherwise a trivial quantity of heroin in a large quantity of powder would attract a ten-year minimum and the s.37 bail bar, which is disproportionate. For the Union: Note 4 to the 2001 notification expressly provides that the quantity shall be the quantity of the mixture, not merely of the pure drug; a contrary reading would enable traffickers to dilute their way out of the Act.
Decision
The Court overruled E. Micheal Raj and held that the entire quantity of the mixture, including neutral substances, is to be taken into account. It upheld the validity of Note 4 to the notification.
Principle and criticism
A strict, deterrence-oriented construction consistent with the 47th Report’s approach. But the practical result is that a small-time carrier holding a heavily adulterated substance faces a minimum of ten years and the s.37 bail bar, while the financier under s.27A is rarely reached — precisely the asymmetry Topic 3 exists to expose. This is the best single case for arguing that the “heavy hand” recommended in 1972 has fallen on the wrong hands.
Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI(2014) 8 SCC 682 (Constitution Bench)privileged class deviance
Facts and issue
Section 6A of the Delhi Special Police Establishment Act, 1946 (inserted in 2003 after the Single Directive had been struck down in Vineet Narain) required the CBI to obtain the prior approval of the Central Government before conducting any inquiry or investigation into an offence under the Prevention of Corruption Act alleged against officers of the level of Joint Secretary and above. Its constitutionality was challenged under Art. 14.
Arguments
Petitioner: the provision creates a class of officers immune from even the commencement of investigation; the classification has no rational nexus with the object of the PCA; it destroys the secrecy essential to a trap and gives the suspect advance warning. Union: senior decision-makers need protection against frivolous complaints so that they can take bona fide decisions; the classification is founded on the intelligible differentia of rank and responsibility.
Decision
The Constitution Bench struck down s.6A as violative of Art. 14: “Corruption is an enemy of the nation… the law does not classify offenders differently for treatment thereunder, including investigation of offences and prosecution for offences, according to their status in life.” Status cannot be the basis for immunity from investigation; and the protection against frivolous complaints lies in the requirement of sanction to prosecute, not in a bar on investigation.
Principle evolved
Equality before the law applies at the investigation stage. Note the sequel: s.17A of the PCA, inserted in 2018, reintroduced a prior-approval requirement in a different form (for enquiries into recommendations made or decisions taken in the discharge of official functions). Whether s.17A survives the reasoning in Subramanian Swamy is an open and highly examinable question.
Lalita Kumari v. Government of Uttar Pradesh(2014) 2 SCC 1 (Constitution Bench)crime statistics
Facts
A writ petition under Art. 32 by the father of a minor girl who had been abducted, complaining that the police had refused to register an FIR. The question of when registration is mandatory had divided earlier benches.
Issue
Is the registration of an FIR mandatory under s.154 CrPC where the information discloses a cognizable offence, or may the police first hold a preliminary inquiry?
Arguments
Petitioner: the word “shall” in s.154(1) is imperative; refusal to register defeats Art. 21 and leaves the victim without a remedy; “burking” of crime is systemic. States: a preliminary inquiry is necessary to prevent the misuse of the criminal process, particularly in matrimonial, commercial and corruption matters.
Decision
Registration of an FIR is MANDATORY under s.154 if the information discloses a cognizable offence, and no preliminary inquiry is permissible in such a case. A preliminary inquiry is permitted only where the information does not disclose a cognizable offence but indicates the need for an inquiry, and only in the illustrative categories of matrimonial/family disputes, commercial offences, medical negligence, corruption, and cases with abnormal delay; such an inquiry must be completed within seven days (later practice: 14 days). Non-registration attracts action against the officer, including under s.166A IPC.
Principle evolved and criminological significance
This is the leading case on the dark figure of crime in India: it addresses “reported but not recorded”, the third leak in the crime funnel (§10). Note the legislative sequel: s.173(3) BNSS now permits a preliminary enquiry of up to 14 days, with the prior permission of a DSP, for offences punishable with three years or more but less than seven — a statutory dilution of Lalita Kumari which will increase the dark figure and is a strong critical point.

44. Previous Year Questions, expected questions and rapid revision

44.1 The Topic-3 PYQ from the DU solved papers

2017 · Q1(c)
Attempt briefly any two of the following: … (c) Human trafficking.
Model answer — reproduce the DU answer, then extend it

1. Definition. “Human trafficking is the practice of people being trapped, lured, coerced or otherwise removed from their home or country, and then forced to work with no or low payments or on terms which are highly exploitative. Trafficking in people has been facilitated by porous borders and advanced communication technologies; it has become increasingly transnational in scope and highly lucrative.” Add the Palermo Protocol three-element test: ACT (recruitment, transportation, transfer, harbouring, receipt) + MEANS (threat, force, coercion, abduction, fraud, deception, abuse of power or of a position of vulnerability, payments to a person in control) + PURPOSE (exploitation); and note that where the victim is a child, the “means” element is not required, and that consent is irrelevant.

2. Forms. (a) Sexual exploitation — “the organized movement of people, usually women, between countries and within countries for sex work with the use of physical coercion, deception and bondage through forced debts… They are often promised work in domestic or service industry, but instead are usually taken to brothels where their passport and other identification papers are confiscated.” (b) Trafficking of children — “forcing a child into prostitution or other forms of sexual activity or child pornography”. (c) Trafficking in organs — “in many countries waiting lists for transplants are very long and criminals have seized this opportunity to exploit the desperation of patients and potential donors”. (d) Forced and bonded labour, domestic servitude, begging and bride purchase.

3. Scale and character. “In India it has become an ORGANIZED CRIME generating millions.” It is the third largest illegal transnational business after drugs and arms, generating about US$7 billion a year, the “merchandise” being “mostly women and children from marginal communities”; “the principle underlying illegal human trafficking is the sheer powerlessness of the victims arising out of gender disparities and the lack of economic choices.”

4. Causes. “The root cause of the problem is POVERTY, UNDER-DEVELOPMENT, UNEMPLOYMENT among others.” Add: illiteracy, gender discrimination, caste and tribal marginalisation, natural disaster and displacement, open borders (the India–Nepal border under the 1950 Treaty), unregulated placement agencies, demand for cheap labour and for brides in States with skewed sex ratios, and police complicity.

5. The Indian legal framework. Article 23 (traffic in human beings prohibited and punishable); s.143 BNS (s.370 IPC) as substituted by the Criminal Law (Amendment) Act, 2013 — set out the five acts and the six means and Explanation 2 on consent, and the punishment ladder from 7 years to imprisonment for natural life, with life imprisonment where a public servant or police officer is involved; s.144 (exploitation of a trafficked person); ss.98–99, 139, 96 BNS; ITPA 1956 ss.3–9; POCSO; JJ Act; Bonded Labour Act 1976; Child and Adolescent Labour Act 1986; THOTA 1994.

6. Case law. Vishal Jeet v. Union of India (1990) — directions on child prostitution and the Devadasi system; the malady is “a socio-economic problem”. Gaurav Jain v. Union of India (1997) — rescue, rehabilitation and socio-economic empowerment, not segregation of the children. Bachpan Bachao Andolan (2011) — mandatory FIR in every case of a missing child, with a presumption of trafficking. Budhadev Karmaskar (2022) — voluntary adult sex work is a profession; the police must not harass sex workers.

7. Criticism. s.8 ITPA punishes the victim for soliciting while the punishment for keeping a brothel “is too lenient”; there is no comprehensive anti-trafficking statute (the Bills of 2018 and 2021 lapsed); conviction rates are among the lowest of any offence; rescue without rehabilitation returns the victim to the trafficker; and the SAARC Convention, 2002 is confined to trafficking of women and children for prostitution, leaving labour, organ and bride trafficking outside it.

8. Conclusion. In the DU answer’s own words: “Therefore we have to address the problem both SOCIALLY and LEGALLY.” Criminologically, trafficking is Bonger’s economic theory and Merton’s innovation operating through Sellin’s enterprise model of organised crime; the legal answer is asset recovery and prosecution of the trafficker, and the social answer is livelihood, education and the removal of the “powerlessness… arising out of gender disparities and the lack of economic choices”.

2022 · Q5 — uses NCRB data
According to data published in 2019 by the National Crime Records Bureau (NCRB), as against the actual capacity of Indian prisons, i.e. 4,03,739 prisoners, there are 4,78,600 prisoners. Moreover, the conditions in jails continue to be inhuman [and] require urgent and drastic reforms as suggested and recommended in Rama Murthy v. State of Karnataka, AIR 1997 SC 1739. To what extent have the recommendations been applied and followed? Critically evaluate.
Why this question belongs to Topic 3 as well as to the prisons topic

The question is formally a prisons question, but it is built on NCRB data, and the Topic-3 contribution to the answer is the statistical part. Set out: the occupancy figure and the occupancy rate (≈118–130%); the proportion of undertrials (about three-quarters); the caveats — Prison Statistics India measures the institutionalised population, which as the standard criticism puts it is “indicative of the persons who are institutionalized rather than having any bearing upon [the] actual number of crimes or criminals” and is “reflective of why people are caught and prosecuted rather than of why they commit crimes”. Then give Rama Murthy’s nine problems — overcrowding; delay in trial; torture and ill-treatment; neglect of health and hygiene; insubstantial food and inadequate clothing; prison vices; deficiency in communication; streamlining of jail visits; management of open-air prisons — and the compliance record: the Model Prison Manual 2016, the Model Prisons and Correctional Services Act, 2023, In Re Inhuman Conditions in 1382 Prisons (2016), In Re Contagion of COVID-19 Virus in Prisons (2020), and the new s.479 BNSS (release of undertrials who have served one-half, or one-third for first-time offenders, of the maximum sentence). Close with the Topic-3 point: the prison population is overwhelmingly poor, under-educated and disproportionately from Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes and religious minorities, while the offences that cause the greatest economic and social harm are resolved by compounding, settlement and adjudication. The prison statistics are therefore the clearest single measure of privileged class deviance.

44.2 Expected questions with answer plans

1. “Distinguish between crimes mala in se and mala prohibita. Is the distinction still useful?”
Quote Bachan Singh para 62 verbatim; the twelve-row comparison table (§4.2); the six criticisms (§4.3); the four fields where it actually bites in Indian law — implied mens rea, moral turpitude, probation and compounding (§4.4); conclude that it is descriptively useful and normatively dangerous.
2. “Crime statistics are the most deceptive of all statistics.” Discuss.
Definition and importance (Taft, Bentham, Sutherland); the three sources; the eight reasons for unreliability verbatim, with Radzinowicz’s 15% and Wehner’s two-to-four times; the crime funnel diagram; the Indian additions — Lalita Kumari, burking, the principal offence rule, projected population, the BNS break in the series, the Kerala paradox. Conclude: statistics measure the criminal justice system before they measure crime.
3. “Examine the characteristics of socio-economic offences with reference to the 47th Law Commission Report.”
The six characteristics verbatim; paras 1.5, 1.6, 1.8, 1.9, 3.13, 3.15, 3.16; the intersecting-circles diagram; the eight categories with modern statutes; the recommendations (§21); the criticism (§21.2 box).
4. “What is white-collar crime? Examine its incidence in India.”
Sutherland 1939/1949; the 47th Report’s description and its two negative examples; Santhanam Committee para 2.13 verbatim; Vivian Bose, Chagla and the scam sequence; crime in the professions; tax evasion v. avoidance and R.K. Garg; why it persists; the Law Commission’s refusal to put a chapter in the IPC.
5. “Discuss organised crime in India. Which theory best explains it?”
Definitions — Reckless, Sellin, Taft, Sutherland, MCOCA s.2(1)(e), BNS s.111; the four types with Indian examples; characteristics of organised criminals; the Vohra Committee; the legal framework; and the DU model answer’s conclusion that differential association best explains it, with the rival theories acknowledged.
6. “Cyber crime is the crime of the twenty-first century.” Examine the Indian response.
Definition; the five reasons plus six modern ones; the three classifications; the catalogue of offences; the IT Act map with sections; Shreya Singhal, Suhas Katti, Anvar P.V. and Arjun Panditrao; NCRB 65,893 and 24.4%; the criticism — capacity, MLAT delay, conviction rate, and the continued registration of cases under the dead s.66A.
7. “Privileged class deviance — nature and dimensions.”
Why the term was needed (the inadequacy of “crime”, “white-collar crime” and “socio-economic offence”); the seven features; the five dimensions with Indian illustrations; the five-stage architecture of impunity; Sita Soren, Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI, Vineet Narain, Sukanya Shantha, the electoral-bonds judgment; remedies; the model conclusion at §40.6.
8. “Examine the adequacy of the PMLA, 2002.”
Concept and the three stages; hawala; the Act section by section; Vijay Madanlal Choudhary in full; FATF and the 2024 Mutual Evaluation; the six criticisms; balance with Mohanlal Jitamalji Porwal and Y.S. Jagan Mohan Reddy on the one side and Manish Sisodia and Satender Kumar Antil on the other.
9. Short notes
mala in se · dark figure of crime · principal offence rule · NCRB · public welfare offences · intersecting circles · Santhanam Committee · crime syndicate · criminal racket · political graft · Vohra Committee · the “fix” · cyber terrorism · hawala · placement–layering–integration · Palermo Protocol · SAARC Convention 2002 · golden crescent and golden triangle · s.111 BNS · s.37 NDPS · s.45 PMLA · Jan Vishwas Act 2023.
10. Problem questions to expect
(a) A trader hoards essential commodities during a shortage — classify the offence, identify the statute, and explain why mens rea is presumed against him. (b) A company’s effluent kills villagers — compare Keshub Mahindra and M.C. Mehta. (c) A woman rescued from a brothel is charged under s.8 ITPA — advise, using Budhadev Karmaskar. (d) A person is found with 4 grams of heroin mixed in 500 grams of powder — advise on quantity and bail, using Hira Singh and s.37. (e) An MLA takes a bribe to vote — advise, using Sita Soren.

44.3 Rapid revision — quotations, figures and mnemonics

Quotations to reproduce exactly
  • Bachan Singh, para 62: “Crimes mala in se embrace acts immoral or wrong in themselves, such as, murder, rape, arson, burglary, larceny (robbery and dacoity); while crimes mala prohibita embrace things prohibited by statute as infringing on others’ rights, though no moral turpitude attaches to such crimes.”
  • 47th Report, 1.4: “Motive… is avarice or rapaciousness (not lust or hate)… Mode of operation is fraud, not force.”
  • 47th Report, 1.5: “They do not involve an individual direct victim but are punished because they harm the whole society.”
  • 47th Report, 1.9: “Socio-economic offences and white-collar crimes could thus be intersecting circles.”
  • 47th Report, 3.13: These offences “require to be put down with a heavy hand… our Welfare State needs weapons of attack… that are sharp and effective.”
  • 47th Report, 3.16: “The wide distribution of goods has become an instrument of wide distribution of harm.”
  • Sutherland: white-collar crime is “a crime committed by a person of respectability and high social status in the course of his occupation”; organised criminals “are bound together by a common hostility towards law”, and “gangsters too often adopt the technique of ‘fixing’ crimes, which is far more important than executing them”.
  • Sellin: organised crime is “synonymous with economic enterprises organised for the purpose of conducting illegal activities”.
  • Reckless: “an unlawful misadventure… carried on by a boss, his lieutenants and operators who form a hierarchical structure”.
  • Radzinowicz: crimes “brought out into the open and punished represent not more than fifteen per cent of the great mass actually committed”.
  • Santhanam Committee: technology is “contributing to the emergence of ‘mass society’ with a large rank and file and a small controlling elite”.
  • Taft: “Quantification… is the basic process in [the] modern scientific approach to criminal science.”
  • Mohanlal Jitamalji Porwal: “An economic offence is committed with cool calculation and deliberate design with an eye on personal profit regardless of the consequence to the community.”
  • Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI: “The law does not classify offenders differently… according to their status in life.”
  • Sita Soren: “Bribery is not rendered immune under Article 105(2) or Article 194(2).”
  • Sukanya Shantha: “Discrimination against the marginalised castes cannot continue behind the prison walls.”
Figures to quote (NCRB, Crime in India 2022)
  • Total cognizable crimes 58,24,946 (−4.5%); IPC 35,61,379, SLL 22,63,567
  • Crime rate 422.2 (from 445.9)
  • Murder 28,522 (≈78 a day); rate 2.1; charge-sheeting 81.5
  • Crime against women 4,45,256 (+4%); rate 66.4; ≈51 FIRs an hour; cruelty by husband 31.4%
  • UP highest in number (65,743); Delhi highest rate (144.4)
  • Cyber crime 65,893 (+24.4%); fraud 64.8%
  • Economic offences 1,93,385 (+11.1%)
  • Crime against children ≈1,62,449 (+8.7%); crime against SCs 57,582 (+13.1%)
  • Metros: 8,53,470 cognizable crimes (−10.4%); Kolkata safest at 86.5
  • Prisons: capacity ≈4,03,739, inmates ≈4,78,600; ≈75% undertrials
  • Trafficking: the third largest illegal transnational business after drugs and arms, ≈US$7 billion a year
  • Substance use (AIIMS 2019): alcohol 14.6% of those aged 10–75; opioid users ≈2.26 crore, of whom over 77 lakh need help
  • ADR: ≈46% of 18th Lok Sabha members declared criminal cases, ≈31% serious ones
Mnemonics
  • A-N-V-F-D-T — the 47th Report’s six characteristics: Avarice · Non-emotional · Victim is the State/public · Fraud not force · Deliberate and wilful · Twofold interest
  • P-S-R-G — the four types of organised crime: Predatory · Syndicate · Racket · political Graft
  • P-L-I — money laundering: Placement · Layering · Integration
  • A-M-P — trafficking: Act · Means · Purpose (and for a child, no “M”)
  • The 4 Ps — anti-trafficking: Prevention · Protection · Prosecution · Partnership
  • The 6 Ds of impunity — Define · Decide (whether to investigate) · Delay · Dilute · Deflect · Dignify
  • Sayre’s eight public welfare offences — liquor, food and drugs, misbranding, narcotics, nuisance, traffic, motor vehicles, police regulations
The three sentences to have ready
Opening: “The Indian crime reality is that the offences which fill India’s prisons are not the offences which do India the most harm.”

Middle: “The 47th Law Commission Report identified in 1972 the six features that make socio-economic offences invisible — avarice, non-emotionality, a diffuse victim, fraud, deliberation and a twofold public interest — and every one of them still holds.”

Closing: “The answer lies less in enhancing punishment, which the classical school has shown to be the weakest lever, than in raising the certainty of detection, calibrating sanctions to profit, and dismantling the legal devices that convert privilege into impunity.”

45. Glossary and sources

45.1 Glossary

TermMeaning
BurkingThe deliberate non-registration or suppression of a reported crime by the police in order to improve the recorded crime figures.
Charge-sheeting rateCharge-sheets filed as a percentage of cases in which investigation was completed; a measure of police output, not of guilt.
Continuing unlawful activityA cognizable offence punishable with three years or more, undertaken as a member of or on behalf of a syndicate, in respect of which more than one charge-sheet has been filed in the preceding ten years (s.2(1)(d) MCOCA; Explanation to s.111 BNS).
Dark figure of crimeThe volume of crime that is committed but never recorded — undetected, unreported or reported-but-not-recorded.
Ecological fallacyInferring something about an individual from data about the group or area to which he belongs.
ECIREnforcement Case Information Report — the ED’s internal document initiating a money-laundering investigation; held in Vijay Madanlal not to be an FIR and not required to be supplied to the accused.
“Fix”, theSutherland’s and Dressler’s term for the arrangements by which organised criminals obtain immunity — bribery of officials, intimidation of witnesses, political contributions, economic ties with respectable business, and the purchase of public goodwill.
HawalaAn informal, trust-based value-transfer system in which no money crosses a border and no record is created; unlawful under s.3 FEMA and a principal laundering and terror-financing channel.
LayeringThe second stage of money laundering: complex transactions designed to break the audit trail.
Mala in seActs wrong in themselves, independently of any statute — murder, rape, arson, burglary, larceny.
Mala prohibitaActs that are wrong only because prohibited by statute; “no moral turpitude attaches”.
Organised crime syndicateA group of two or more persons who, singly or jointly, indulge in continuing unlawful activity (s.2(1)(f) MCOCA; Explanation to s.111 BNS).
Political graftThe utilisation of notorious criminals by politicians for political gain, and its reverse — the entry of criminals into elected office.
Predatory crimeCrime involving no service to the victim; a one-way transaction in which the victim is a total loser.
Principal offence ruleThe NCRB counting convention by which one FIR is counted once, under the most heinous offence alone; the chief cause of understatement in Indian crime data.
Privileged class devianceHarmful conduct by persons occupying positions of economic, political, bureaucratic, professional, caste or religious privilege, which the legal system either does not criminalise or does not enforce (B.B. Pande).
Proceeds of crimeProperty derived or obtained, directly or indirectly, as a result of criminal activity relating to a scheduled offence, or its value (s.2(1)(u) PMLA).
Public welfare offenceA regulatory offence, typically of strict liability, created to protect the safety, health or well-being of the community (Sayre; 47th Report, paras 3.15–3.16).
RacketeeringSystematic extortion under threat, coupled with a “service” rendered to persons engaged in legitimate business (Vold; Taft).
Scheduled offenceA predicate offence listed in the Schedule to the PMLA, without which there can be no offence of money laundering.
Smurfing / structuringBreaking a large cash deposit into many small ones to avoid reporting thresholds.
Socio-economic offenceAn offence affecting the health or material welfare of the community, or the economy of the country, rather than merely an individual victim (47th Report, para 1.8).
Twin conditionsThe statutory bail restriction — the court must be satisfied that there are reasonable grounds for believing the accused is not guilty and is unlikely to offend on bail (s.37 NDPS; s.45 PMLA; s.21(4) MCOCA; s.43-D(5) UAPA).
Victimless / public-order crimeConsensual conduct, or conduct in which only the actor is harmed, criminalised because contrary to shared norms; now preferably called public-order crime because of secondary victims.
White-collar crimeA crime committed by a person of respectability and high social status in the course of his occupation (Sutherland, 1949; 47th Report, para 1.8).

45.2 Sources

Primary (prescribed) DU:

  1. Law Commission of India, Forty-Seventh Report on “The Trial and Punishment of Social and Economic Offences” (1972) — paras 1.4, 1.5, 1.6, 1.8, 1.9, 3.13, 3.15 and 3.16 are reproduced verbatim in the DU Case Material and are reproduced verbatim in these notes at §§18–20; the recommendations at §21 are drawn from Chapters 3, 7 and 15 and the Summary of Major Recommendations.
  2. National Crime Records Bureau, Crime in India and Crime in India — Figures at a Glance 2022 (http://ncrb.gov.in), together with Prison Statistics India.
  3. Bachan Singh v. State of Punjab (1980) 2 SCC 684, para 62 — reproduced in the DU Case Material; the source of the mala in se / mala prohibita passage at §4.

Suggested readings named in the syllabus: Prof. Usha Razdan, “Combating Trafficking of Women and Children in SAARC Countries: Issues and Intricacies”, Journal of Criminology and Criminal Justice (2012); B.B. Pande, “Privileged Class Deviance — Nature and Dimensions”, in The Other Side of Development (1987).

Supplementary works used to fill the heads on which the DU Case Material is silent (typology, crime statistics, organised crime, white-collar crime in India, cyber crime, trafficking, narcotics and money laundering): N.V. Paranjape, Criminology, Penology with Victimology; the standard Indian criminology notes in the project folder; and the DU solved previous-year papers (2017 and 2022), from which the model answers at §44 are drawn.

Statutes and cases are cited as they stand after the coming into force of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023, the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 and the Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam, 2023 on 1 July 2024, with the corresponding IPC, CrPC and Evidence Act provisions given alongside, because the prescribed readings and the previous-year papers use the old numbering. NCRB data is from Crime in India 2022, published December 2023.

How to use this document. Read §2–§7 for typology and the statutory classifications; §8–§13 for crime statistics and the NCRB; §14–§17 for traditional crimes; §18–§23 for the 47th Report and white-collar crime — this is the core of the topic and the part most often examined; §24–§27 for organised crime; §28–§37 for the four heads of cyber crime, trafficking, narcotics and money laundering; and §38–§40 for privileged class deviance. In the last week before the examination read only §41 (master comparison), §42 (case briefs), §43 (the statute map) and §44 (PYQs and rapid revision). Everything marked DU comes from the prescribed material and should be cited as such in the answer-book; everything marked Statute is the allied legislation that turns a criminology answer into a law answer.
